\title{Scarcity-Driven Capital Liquidation and Delay-Amplified Instability: \\
       A Vector-Valued Flow-Balance Framework for Sustainability}
\author{Your Name \\ \small{Affiliation} \and Co-Author \\ \small{Affiliation}}
\date{\today}
\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
While modern sustainability accounting increasingly relies on vector-valued frameworks to track multidimensional resource flows and diverse capital stocks, evaluating ecological overshoot still lacks a dynamic, component-by-component threshold theory. Existing vector accounting models (such as input--output systems and life-cycle inventories) are primarily descriptive and linear, leaving a critical gap in how individual resource deficits dynamically degrade physical stocks.

We bridge this gap by introducing a dynamic, vector-valued flow-balance framework where natural capital, sustainable yield, and consumption are modelled as distinct, interacting vectors. Sustainability is treated as a flow condition: at every instant the system is in overshoot precisely when consumption exceeds the sustainable regenerative flow, and the deficit so defined --- not the stock level alone --- is the quantity that drives the dynamics. We analyse overshoot strictly component by component, providing a formal proof (Proposition~1) that no positively weighted scalar aggregate of these components can mathematically certify individual resource safety. We anchor this framework in a mass-conserved augmented system spanning natural capital, product stocks, waste compartments, and abiotic reservoirs: mass is conserved within the explicitly tracked material compartments, and the familiar logistic law of growth is recovered as a constitutive limiting form under active-pool saturation (Section~\ref{sec:frozen-A}). We treat capital liquidation explicitly: when extraction effort exceeds the locally sustainable yield, the shortfall is drawn from the asset itself, creating a positive feedback loop, and a single mass-conserving extraction rule keeps the model physically consistent, so the biological and active-pool stocks can decline to zero but never below it (the geological reservoir, drawn by direct mining, is the one compartment not covered by this guarantee; Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}). Using singular perturbation theory, we reduce the system to a three-state core model to analyse the nonlinear coupling between extraction effort, resource biology, and institutional delays. The framework's diagnostics also replace the scalar ``Overshoot Day'' with per-asset depletion horizons---the time remaining before each supporting reservoir is drawn past a critical threshold.

The dynamic model identifies a critical stabilising threshold for institutional response lags at approximately 3.8 years in the canonical effort-saturation-corrected four-state core (3.7 years for the corrected three-state limit and 6.9 years for its uncorrected form); all values are quoted at the model's baseline regeneration rate, within which the delay-induced instability exists only for slow-regenerating resources (roughly $r\lesssim0.06$ yr$^{-1}$ at maximal effort response). For institutions whose response to a perceived deficit is to mobilise additional extraction (the institutional-failure regime analysed here), decreasing the continuous response delay below the first stabilizing Hopf crossing produces an unstable equilibrium and a numerically observed large-amplitude cycle: excessively rapid, scarcity-driven mobilisation---rather than protective regulation---drives instability; the opposite-signed protective response (quotas, restoration) is a separate channel this manuscript does not model. Conversely, the slower thresholds are set by biological regeneration timescales: in the canonical core the large-amplitude cycle coexists with the stable equilibrium in a narrow lower bistable window (delays $\approx3.8$--$5.6$ yr) and throughout a wide upper bistable window (delays $\approx64$--$150$ yr) in which the coexisting large-amplitude cycle is captured from histories that begin at high stock --- abundant, well-stocked systems with slowly adjusting institutions --- for delays as short as $\approx85$--$95$ yr, whereas depleted-stock histories are captured only as the delay approaches the destabilising threshold ($\tau\gtrsim140$ yr); capture by the cycle is thus history-dependent within the upper window, not a single threshold. The standard assumption that the supporting environment stays fixed (the ``frozen environment'' simplification) used to derive the core model cannot be justified at realistic parameters: the supporting abiotic pool must be kept as an active fourth state, which leaves the fast threshold essentially unchanged but implies oscillation periods of centuries at any biologically realistic regeneration rate. When tested against more than thirty real-world systems, no empirical case provided a clean, unconfounded test of the fast threshold---a negative result we report as a primary empirical finding, traced to selection bias in long-monitored records and to a structural mismatch between the multi-century oscillation periods the base and four-state cores predict at biologically realistic regeneration rates and the length of any real institutional record. Under the periodic-review (sampled) governance form real institutions actually implement, the mechanism becomes testable in review-interval space: annual review is predicted stable and consistent with the cross-sectional absence of the predicted institutional signal among annual-managed stocks, while responsive review intervals of roughly three to four years are predicted to restore multi-year institutional cycles in fast-maturing small pelagics, so the negative result is in part a governance lag gap rather than purely a biological one. The review-interval dependence is a sampling-induced instability: fixing the review interval $T_r$ and crossing the associated stability boundary is a parameter bifurcation of the sampled-data governance loop, not rate-induced tipping in the strict sense (which requires the parameter itself to be ramped in time, $\dot\mu=\varepsilon$, as in the climate-tipping literature \cite{ashwin2012}). The two mechanisms are related --- both concern the rate at which the governance loop acts relative to the resource's relaxation timescale --- but they are distinct; we use the former, more precise label. Delay-amplified instability thus spans two regimes: a near-term stabilising transition whose threshold falls within documented governance lags, and a longer-horizon, more speculative tipping phenomenon.

By moving beyond static vector accounting and introducing nonlinear feedback loops, this framework demonstrates that component-specific thresholds cannot be bypassed by aggregation. Furthermore, it reveals that institutional pacing is a primary determinant of ecological stability, where a delay of roughly a decade promotes long-term equilibrium, while hyper-reactive scarcity responses trigger systemic volatility. The folds of the canonical core are located by dt-converged long-horizon bisection ($\tau_{\mathrm{SNPO,L}}\approx5.6$~yr and $\tau_{\mathrm{SNPO,R}}\approx64.4$--$64.5$~yr); their collocation-grade classification as saddle-nodes of periodic orbits, and the persistence of the pattern in the full framework, remain open questions; the $\eta=10$ recurring intermittency has been classified as homoclinic-like slow-fast (relaxation) intermittency rather than a torus.
\end{abstract}

\section{Introduction: The Orchard, the Hen, and the Ecosystem}
\label{sec:introduction}

\paragraph{Conceptual foundations.} The framework rests on four propositions that recur throughout. First, sustainability is a flow condition: overshoot at any instant is the excess of consumption over the sustainable regenerative flow (Eq.~\eqref{eq:deficit-identity}), and depletion is deficit-driven --- only unsustainable extraction erodes the productive base (non-renewable mining excepted), so a stock is a carrier of future flow rather than an independently valuable quantity (Section~\ref{sec:deficit-definitions}). Second, the productivity illusion has two distinct senses --- a compensatory sense (an aggregate can mask a component-level deficit, Proposition~1) and a yield-inflation sense (a measured yield can exceed the true sustainable yield by drawing down the supporting pool) --- and the framework's diagnostics address both (Section~\ref{sec:productivity-illusion-narrative}). Third, substitution is admissible only where an identified physical pathway exists; no substitute is assumed merely because an aggregate production function permits it (Section~\ref{sec:weak-vs-strong}). Fourth, rate matters as much as level: systems can be destabilised by the speed of change relative to relaxation timescales (rate-induced tipping), which is why the governance-loop's review interval, not just its delay, is a control parameter (Section~\ref{sec:empirical-hunt}). The economy is internal to the biosphere's material cycles, not an exogenous forcing (Section~\ref{sec:spatial-heterogeneity}).

\subsection{The Stepping Stone: Capital Liquidation in a Single Asset}
Consider a single renewable asset: a hen. The hen produces a sustainable flow of eggs \(S(N)\) each year, where \(N\) is the hen's body condition or the size of the flock. Consumption \(D\) is the number of eggs eaten. If \(D \leq S(N)\), the system is sustainable. However, if \(D > S(N)\), the shortfall cannot be conjured---the farmer must slaughter the hen to cover the difference. This is \textbf{capital liquidation}: the asset is physically destroyed to satisfy present demand.

This process is not a static trade-off; it is a runaway positive feedback loop:
\[
\text{deficit} \uparrow \;\Rightarrow\; \text{liquidation pressure} \uparrow \;\Rightarrow\; \text{capital stock} \downarrow \;\Rightarrow\; \text{sustainable yield} \downarrow \;\Rightarrow\; \text{deficit} \uparrow\uparrow .
\]
Delayed supply responses have long been known to generate real production cycles --- the classical cobweb theorem \cite{ezekiel1938} and the spruce-budworm outbreak cycles of slow forest systems \cite{ludwig1978} --- and the models of this process (Section~\ref{sec:core-model}) yield a stark survival condition: for the equilibrium to exist, effort-driven extraction must not persistently exceed the sustainable yield. If it does, the stock converges toward zero without recovering --- the outcome when no such positive equilibrium exists under that extraction regime. Where such an equilibrium does exist, persistent overshoot instead sustains a large-amplitude boom-bust cycle rather than monotone collapse (Section~\ref{sec:hopf-verified}). This is the essence of overshoot. Notably, in light of Section~\ref{sec:empirical-hunt}'s bounded-window analysis, the spruce budworm is one of the few real systems whose effective regeneration rate ($r\approx0.025$--$0.033$~yr$^{-1}$ under a $1/\text{period}$ mapping of its documented $30$--$40$-year outbreak cycle) approaches the upper edge of the model's delay-instability window at the baseline effort response (and falls inside it at elevated effort response, up to $r\lesssim0.06$~yr$^{-1}$) --- though its cycles are endogenous predator--prey outbreak dynamics rather than institutionally driven, so it corroborates the mechanism class, not the institutional $\tau_-$ claim.

\paragraph{Not all liquidation is the same: hens versus eggs versus apple seeds.} The hen story, taken literally, describes a specific liquidation mechanism: killing a hen removes an existing, reproductively active adult from the population immediately, permanently reducing the standing stock. But not every form of ``eating more than the sustainable flow'' works this way. Eating fertilised eggs consumes only \emph{next generation's} recruits — it need not touch a single existing adult, yet it still deterministically prevents those particular offspring from ever existing. An apple, by contrast, is looser still: eating the fruit frequently leaves the seed inside unharmed (and some fruit are evolutionarily shaped specifically to be eaten so the seed is dispersed), so consumption and reproduction can be substantially decoupled. These are three physically distinct liquidation channels — culling the standing stock, suppressing recruitment, and (in the weakest case) barely touching viability at all — and a single resource can plausibly sit anywhere along this spectrum depending on what, exactly, is being extracted. Section~\ref{sec:two-channel} extends the reduced core to represent the first two of these mechanisms explicitly, as a parameterised mixture, and shows that while they share the same equilibrium and the same local stability structure, they produce measurably different large-amplitude dynamics once the system is pushed away from equilibrium.

\subsection{The Vector Escalation: The Ecosystem as a Portfolio of Hens}
\label{sec:productivity-illusion-narrative}
But the biosphere is not one hen. It is an interdependent vector of stocks and services---biomass, zinc, groundwater, pollinators. Each component produces its own flow. A farmer may eat \emph{all} the eggs (sustainable for biomass) while simultaneously draining the pond (groundwater) and mining the soil (zinc) to keep the hen producing. The aggregate appears stable, but the supporting capital is being liquidated silently. This is the \textbf{productivity illusion}: the vector of services provided by the ecosystem can appear adequate when measured by a single component (biomass), while other components that are essential for long-term human well-being are eroding silently.

The hen captures the \textbf{dynamics} of a single asset under delayed stress. The orchard captures the \textbf{diagnostic blindness} of compensatory aggregate metrics, where the survival of one asset masks the silent liquidation of others. The productivity illusion has two distinct senses, and the framework's diagnostics address both. The first is compensatory: an aggregate indicator can mask a component-level deficit (Proposition~1). The second is a yield-inflation sense: a measured yield can exceed the true sustainable yield when it is maintained by drawing down the supporting pool (groundwater, soil carbon, bioavailable nutrients) rather than by that pool's regeneration --- the case the framework's Abiotic Depletion Horizons and Depletion Stress Indices are built to detect (Section~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}). Our mathematical framework unifies these two realities: the scalar DDE (Sections~\ref{sec:core-model}--\ref{sec:numerical-continuation-corrected}) supplies the dynamical engine of liquidation, while the vector accounting (Sections~\ref{sec:spatial-heterogeneity}--\ref{sec:deficit-dynamics}) supplies the multi-component diagnostics to detect it.

\subsection{Weak vs.\ Strong Sustainability, Re-examined}
\label{sec:weak-vs-strong}
The weak sustainability paradigm argues that natural and manufactured capital are substitutable: if soil zinc declines, we can fortify flour with synthetic zinc. The strong sustainability paradigm argues that some natural stocks---the soil microbiome, the climate system, the genetic library of biodiversity---cannot be substituted in any practical sense \cite{daly1990}. Our vector framework shows that this debate is not binary but \textbf{component-specific}. Substitution can replace some service components (calories can come from synthetic food) but cannot replace others (no technological substitute exists for the ozone layer). The relevant question is not ``can we maintain total capital?'' but ``for which components of the service vector can substitution keep pace with depletion, and for which is it already failing?''

Substitutability is accordingly component-specific, not an aggregate property of the whole capital stock: the aggregate question ``can we maintain total capital?'' is ill-posed for exactly the reason Proposition~1 makes precise (Section~\ref{sec:why-frameworks-fail}), and the correct framing asks which services can be substituted, at what rate, and at what cost, component by component. The framework therefore treats substitution as admissible only where an identified physical pathway exists: a manufactured substitute must actually provide the service without drawing down a different critical stock, and no substitute is assumed merely because an aggregate production function permits it. More fundamentally, weak and strong sustainability are not competing hypotheses but distinct regimes of the same dynamical system, indexed by the rate at which substitution can occur relative to the rate of depletion: in the weak-sustainability regime substitution keeps pace with depletion and the flow condition \(E(t)\le B_s(t)\) is maintained; in the strong-sustainability regime substitution cannot keep pace --- either because no physical pathway exists (the stock is non-substitutable) or because the rate of depletion exceeds the rate at which a pathway can be deployed --- and the system is in overshoot. Whether a particular system is in one regime or the other is an empirical question about substitution rates and pathways, not a philosophical commitment.

\subsection{Why Existing Frameworks Fail}
\label{sec:why-frameworks-fail}
Scalar aggregation underlies many sustainability metrics --- from the systems-dynamics overshoot models \cite{meadows1972} and Earth Overshoot Day \cite{lin2018,wackernagel2019,blomqvist2013}, which collapse the biosphere to a single flow or stock balance, to communication indices derived from multi-dimensional frameworks such as the planetary boundaries \cite{rockstrom2009,steffen2015}. Three failure modes are fatal for such scalar aggregation specifically:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Dimensional collapse:} Aggregating incommensurable services (biomass, zinc, carbon sequestration)
      into a single number destroys the information needed to detect component-specific collapse.
\item \textbf{Flow-only blindness:} Earth Overshoot Day compares a scalar consumption flow to a scalar
      regeneration flow. It cannot detect that the regeneration flow is being maintained by mining
      non-renewable stocks (fossil aquifers, soil phosphorus).
\item \textbf{The scalar deficit conceals vector crises:} A positive aggregate balance can coexist with a deep
      deficit in a critical micronutrient. The system appears sustainable while a specific service component is
      already in committed collapse.
\end{enumerate}
The third of these failure modes is not merely a qualitative risk but an exact, unavoidable algebraic fact about \emph{any} scalar index built by weighting and summing a componentwise deficit vector, however the weights are chosen. We state this once, formally, since it is the load-bearing justification for replacing the scalar index with a vector one throughout the rest of this manuscript.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Proposition 1 (No scalar weighting certifies componentwise sustainability).} Let $n\ge2$ and let $\mathbf b=(b_1,\dots,b_n)\in\mathbb R^n$ denote a service-component deficit vector, with $b_i>0$ indicating component $i$ in surplus and $b_i<0$ indicating component $i$ in deficit. Fix \emph{any} positive weight vector $\mathbf w\in\mathbb R^n_{>0}$ and define the scalar aggregate index $\sigma(\mathbf b)=\mathbf w^\top\mathbf b$. Then for every component index $k$ and every threshold $M>0$, there exist deficit vectors $\mathbf b\in\mathbb R^n$ such that
\begin{equation}
b_k \le -M \quad\text{while}\quad \sigma(\mathbf b) > 0, \label{eq:prop1-direction-a}
\end{equation}
and, symmetrically, deficit vectors $\mathbf b'\in\mathbb R^n$ such that
\begin{equation}
b_k' \ge M \quad\text{while}\quad \sigma(\mathbf b') < 0. \label{eq:prop1-direction-b}
\end{equation}
Consequently, no choice of positive weights $\mathbf w$ makes $\sigma(\mathbf b)>0$ imply $b_i\ge0$ for all $i$, nor makes $\sigma(\mathbf b)\le0$ identify which component is actually in deficit: an arbitrarily severe single-component deficit is compatible with an arbitrarily positive scalar aggregate, and an arbitrarily large single-component surplus is compatible with an arbitrarily negative scalar aggregate.
\end{quote}

\paragraph{Proof.} Fix $k$ and $M>0$, and pick any $j\ne k$ (possible since $n\ge2$). For direction~\eqref{eq:prop1-direction-a}, set $b_k=-M$, $b_j=(w_kM+1)/w_j>0$ (well-defined since $w_j>0$), and $b_i=0$ for all other $i$. Then $\sigma(\mathbf b)=w_kb_k+w_jb_j=-w_kM+(w_kM+1)=1>0$ while $b_k=-M\le-M$. For direction~\eqref{eq:prop1-direction-b}, set $b_k'=M$, $b_j'=-(w_kM+1)/w_j<0$, and $b_i'=0$ otherwise; then $\sigma(\mathbf b')=-1<0$ while $b_k'=M\ge M$. Since $j,M$ were arbitrary (subject only to $j\ne k$, $M>0$), and $\mathbf w$ was an arbitrary fixed positive weight vector, this holds for every $k$, every $M$, and every choice of positive weights. $\blacksquare$

Both directions of the construction, together with a stress test over thousands of randomised weight vectors and dimensions $n\in\{2,\dots,8\}$ and threshold values up to $M=10^{12}$ (verified exactly via rational arithmetic to rule out floating-point artifacts), are confirmed computationally (Section~\ref{sec:data-code}). Proposition~1 is the formal statement underlying the informal ``productivity illusion'' described qualitatively in Section~\ref{sec:productivity-illusion-narrative} below: the phenomenon itself has precursors in portfolio theory, functional redundancy, and Simpson's-paradox-style aggregation effects, and the contribution here is its formalisation as a general property of compensatory aggregation: Proposition~1 shows that the illusion is not a special or unlucky case but the generic behaviour of \emph{every} positively-weighted scalar aggregation scheme, which is why this manuscript replaces the scalar deficit with the vector deficit $\boldsymbol\Delta_i^{\text{phys}}$ (Section~\ref{sec:deficit-definitions}) rather than searching for a better set of scalar weights.

\paragraph{What Proposition~1 does and does not establish.} Proposition~1 concerns \emph{compensatory linear} aggregation specifically: any index built as a positively weighted sum of the component deficits. It does not assert that \emph{no} scalar certificate of componentwise safety exists. Non-compensatory scalar certificates do exist and certify componentwise non-negativity exactly --- the minimum margin $m_{\min}=\min_i b_i$, the violation magnitude $V=\|[-\mathbf b]_+\|$, and the distance from $\mathbf b$ to the positive cone $\mathbb R_+^n$ --- precisely because they never allow a surplus in one component to offset a deficit in another. The per-component diagnostics of this manuscript (Sections~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon} and \ref{sec:deficit-definitions}) are the component-resolved generalisation of such non-compensatory certificates, retaining \emph{which} component is failing, which a bare minimum-margin scalar cannot. Proposition~1 is accordingly an argument not against scalar reporting as such, but against compensatory scalar indices, and in favour of diagnostics that either preserve component identity or take non-compensatory form.

\paragraph{Relationship to established vector-accounting frameworks.} The vector representation of sustainability is not introduced here as a new accounting concept. Multi-capital accounting --- the UN System of Environmental-Economic Accounting (SEEA) \cite{seea2014}, the multi-capital framework of integrated reporting \cite{iirc2013}, and comprehensive-wealth accounting \cite{cwon2021,dasgupta2021} --- tracks several capital stocks and their balance equations in parallel; environmentally extended input--output analysis \cite{leontief1970} and life-cycle assessment \cite{iso14040} are vector balance systems over sectors and substances; and economy-wide material-flow accounting \cite{eurostat2001} closes mass balances over physical compartments, including transboundary water and material balances \cite{hoekstra2002}. What the present framework adds to this established body of work is not the vector form itself but three properties in combination. First, a closed, mass-conserved augmented accounting in which every flux is donor-limited and routed between explicit compartments (including a two-compartment abiotic reservoir and a detritus pool), so that the conservation law is derived from the flux ledger rather than assumed (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}). Second, a certification logic: the non-compensatory, componentwise diagnostics of Proposition~1 and Sections~\ref{sec:deficit-definitions}--\ref{sec:depletion-horizon} certify componentwise safety directly, rather than relying on the summary indices that the accounting frameworks above also produce. Third, a dynamical layer: the vector accounting is coupled to delayed institutional feedback whose bifurcations (Hopf crossings and amplitude-discontinuous folds) are computed exactly (Sections~\ref{sec:core-model}--\ref{sec:numerical-continuation-corrected}), turning the accounting identities into falsifiable dynamical predictions --- a property the accounting traditions do not provide. The critique of scalar aggregation above accordingly applies to the summary indices these frameworks produce when used for certification, not to their vector structure.

Rather than collapsing the componentwise state into a single compensatory aggregate, the framework works directly with the service vector, the vector deficit, and the vector of service capacities. It reveals that the current biophysical trajectory is not a possible future fragility but an already-breached, multi-component degradation.

\subsection{Evidence of Committed Degradation}
\label{sec:evidence-of-committed-degradation}
The model's diagnosis of overshoot is a statement about the model's own state vector: the current multi-boundary state of the Earth system --- six of nine planetary boundaries now transgressed \cite{richardson2023} --- is consistent with the componentwise-overshoot pattern the framework describes. componentwise demand exceeding sustainable yield, computed from the framework's stocks and flows. The empirical record below does not, by itself, instantiate that computation --- the framework's full state vector is not yet calibrated to these systems, and model-defined overshoot thresholds are not directly measured. Three distinct claims must therefore be kept separate: (i) \textbf{empirical degradation}, the documented biophysical pressures below; (ii) \textbf{model-defined overshoot}, the framework's own deficit computation, which these data support qualitatively but do not execute; and (iii) \textbf{committed collapse}, irreversible or effectively irreversible loss, which neither the data nor the model establishes for the systems below. With that distinction explicit, the empirical record indicates substantial multi-component degradation in the direction the model identifies:
\begin{itemize}
\item Atmospheric CO\(_2\) above Holocene range, committed warming \cite{ipcc2021}.
\item Historical decline in grain Zn/Fe density, attributable to breeding for yield rather than to soil depletion: archived UK wheat grain from the Broadbalk experiment shows significant decreases in Zn, Cu, Fe and Mg after the introduction of semi-dwarf, high-yielding cultivars in the late 1960s, an overall decline of approximately 20--30\%, with the Zn decline larger still in the most-recent-versus-historical comparison \cite{fan2008}; an independent, more recent dataset of Indian rice and wheat cultivars released since the Green Revolution corroborates declines of the same order over the same 1960--2010 period (rice Zn $-33\%$, Fe $-27\%$; wheat Zn $-30\%$, Fe $-19\%$), also attributed to yield dilution rather than soil depletion \cite{debnath2023}. A mechanistically distinct, CO\(_2\)-driven effect acts on top of this: field trials at elevated CO\(_2\) show further single-digit-to-low-double-digit reductions (e.g., wheat Zn $-9.3\%$, wheat Fe $-5.1\%$; rice Zn $-3.3\%$) across staple C3 crops \cite{myers2014}. These are two separate mechanisms — breeding-driven dilution and CO\(_2\)-driven dilution — not a single combined percentage.
\item Groundwater extraction exceeding natural recharge in major agricultural basins, in some cases by an order of magnitude or more \cite{famiglietti2014,rodell2018}.
\item Soil erosion under conventional tillage exceeding natural soil-formation rates by roughly one to two orders of magnitude (i.e., $\sim$10--100$\times$) \cite{montgomery2007}.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Architecture: General Framework, Augmented Closure, and Reduced Limit}
The framework rests on a \textbf{mass-conserved augmented state space}. Beyond the conventional natural capital, product stocks, and waste compartments, it explicitly includes a \textbf{two-compartment abiotic reservoir} — an active, bioavailable pool \(\mathbf{A}^{\text{act}}_i\) that directly supplies regeneration and receives technological recovery, and an effectively infinite geological reservoir \(\mathbf{A}^{\text{geo}}_i\) that slowly recharges it and absorbs direct anthropogenic mining — together with an \textbf{inert geological sink} \(\mathbf{I}_j\). This closure (Section 3) makes the source of the regeneration flux an explicit, finite, mass-conserved pool rather than an unmodelled input, resolving a structural conflict between requiring the abiotic pool to be simultaneously dynamically responsive (for a well-defined recovery timescale and a genuine effect of recycled/recharged matter) and effectively infinite (for the frozen-$A$ logistic-growth limit): regeneration and recovery act on the finite active pool, while the frozen-$A$ limit and mining apply to the geological reservoir. The total mass across the explicitly tracked material compartments is conserved; the geological reservoir is the one compartment whose non-negativity is not guaranteed (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}). The logistic growth law \(R(N) = rN(1-N/K)\) emerges as the leading-order approximation of the active pool's regeneration function in the limit that it is large relative to a saturation constant (not relative to the geological reservoir).

The general framework (Sections 2--6) is a high-dimensional vector system with four layers, mass conservation within the augmented loop, and generalised economic responses. Singular perturbation theory reduces this general framework to a three-state core model (Sections~\ref{sec:core-model}--\ref{sec:numerical-continuation-corrected}) under well-defined assumptions: fast market clearing, weak waste feedback, and the institutional failure regime (\(\chi=1\)). The reduction is formal on \textbf{finite time intervals} (Theorem, Section 6, Appendix G). At the model's baseline parameters, however, the four-state core of Section~\ref{sec:four-state-core} is the more faithful reduction target; the three-state core is its leading-order frozen-$A$ limit. In this manuscript's terminology the effort-saturation-corrected four-state core of Section~\ref{sec:four-state-core} is the \textbf{canonical model} --- the default referent for untagged threshold statements (threshold registry, Section~\ref{sec:core-model}) --- while the three-state core developed in this section is its frozen-$A$ limit, retained for the exact-cubic completeness certificate (Section~\ref{sec:exact-cubic}), the two-channel and thermodynamic-tether extensions (Sections~\ref{sec:two-channel}--\ref{sec:thermodynamic-tether}), and the derivation chain.

The three-state core exhibits a bifurcation sequence: a stabilising Hopf crossing, a destabilising Hopf crossing, and two amplitude-discontinuous global folds --- candidate Saddle-Nodes of Periodic Orbits (SNPO), with the real-multiplier fold signature collocation-confirmed for the corrected core's lower fold and SNPO-consistent for the remaining folds (Section~\ref{sec:numerical-continuation-corrected}) --- that create two narrow bistable windows flanking a wide monostable interval. These global structures hold for the core. For the full augmented system, their persistence over infinite time remains a \textbf{structurally stable conjecture}, supported by numerical continuation and center-manifold arguments, but its proof requires an extension of Geometric Singular Perturbation Theory to delay-differential equations---an open mathematical problem Appendix H states explicitly.

We introduce a vector of \textbf{Abiotic Depletion Horizons} (Section~\ref{sec:deficit-dynamics}) to replace the scalar Overshoot Day: for each service component, this metric measures the time remaining before its associated abiotic reservoir reaches a critical threshold, providing a dimensionally correct early-warning system.

This architecture reframes delay-amplified instability as a finite-horizon tipping phenomenon with a basin-escape structure that is numerically supported but not yet formally proven over infinite time, offering one explanation for why real-world ecological collapses often appear sudden. The structure belongs to the precursor class studied in the tipping-elements and early-warning-signals literature \cite{lenton2008,scheffer2009}: subcriticality, amplitude-discontinuous folds, and ghost transients are the deterministic signatures of systems that can transition abruptly, and the critical slowing down reported here near each Hopf point (Sections~\ref{sec:hopf-verified}--\ref{sec:numerical-continuation-corrected}) is the corresponding early-warning indicator. The current core represents these precursors but not the full hysteresis or alternative-stable-state dynamics of a complete tipping element; representing those is an open extension (Section~\ref{sec:limitations-further-work}).

\paragraph{The unifying causal chain connecting every section of this manuscript.} The results above are not a list of independent contributions but three tightly linked consequences of a single causal chain: \textbf{physical conservation} $\to$ \textbf{vector observability} $\to$ \textbf{delayed institutional feedback}. \emph{Conservation} (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}'s mass invariant, Eq.~\eqref{eq:invariant}, generalised to the stoichiometric conservation law $\mathbf L^\top\mathbf x(t)=\mathbf L^\top\mathbf x(0)$ of Section~\ref{sec:unified-ontology}, Eq.~\eqref{eq:unified-conservation}) fixes \emph{where} liquidated capital physically goes: it cannot vanish, only move between the stock, product, waste, abiotic, and inert compartments this manuscript tracks explicitly. \emph{Vector observability} (Proposition~1, Section~\ref{sec:why-frameworks-fail}) then shows that no \emph{compensatory} scalar aggregation of this conserved but multi-component reality can certify componentwise safety: a positive scalar balance is compatible with an arbitrarily severe deficit in any single component, which is why Sections~\ref{sec:spatial-heterogeneity}--\ref{sec:economic-layer} replace the scalar deficit with the vector deficit $\boldsymbol\Delta_i^{\text{phys}}$ and the Abiotic Depletion Horizons of Section~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}. \emph{Scope note:} these three layers form one narrative arc and share the general feedback architecture of Section~\ref{sec:general-feedback}, but they are not yet one formally reduced system: the scalar core is a phenomenological proof-of-principle, not a rigorous projection of the vector accounting (Section~\ref{sec:variant-registry} and the opening of Section~\ref{sec:core-model}), and a rigorous reduction of the vector system to the core remains an open item (Appendix~G). Finally, \emph{delayed institutional feedback} (Sections 7--9) supplies the dynamical consequence of acting on a signal that is both filtered through this observability bottleneck and delayed: the general feedback characteristic equation of Section~\ref{sec:general-feedback}, Eq.~\eqref{eq:general-feedback}, shows that this manuscript's local bifurcation results --- both the three-state core's stabilising/destabilising Hopf pair and the unified core v2's Hopf structure --- are instances of one architecture in which an ecological resolvent sets the admissible oscillation frequencies, an institutional feedback gain determines whether the loop is strong enough to destabilise them, and the delay $\tau$ supplies the phase shift that closes the loop at a given frequency. Read in this order, the vector accounting of Sections 2--6 is not a separate diagnostic layer bolted onto the dynamical core of Sections 7--9: it is the first two links of the same causal chain whose third link the core makes dynamically explicit, and the productivity illusion of Section~\ref{sec:productivity-illusion-narrative} and the delay-amplified instability of Sections 7--9 are two stages of one mechanism rather than two unrelated findings sharing a title.

\paragraph{A registry of the model variants used in this manuscript.}
\label{sec:variant-registry}
Because several symbols and formulas below are stated once but apply differently across the manuscript's distinct model variants, we register those variants explicitly here, to be checked against whenever a symbol is reused: \textbf{(i) the general framework} (Sections 2--6), with the full compartment set $(\mathbf N_i,\mathbf P_i,\mathbf W_j,\mathbf I_j,\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i,\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i,\mathbf U_i,K,L,A_{\text{TFP}},T,\mathbf p_i,Q)$; \textbf{(ii) the rational-optimisation regime} ($\chi=0$) versus \textbf{the institutional-failure regime} ($\chi=1$) within variant (i), which differ in whether extraction is set algebraically from service demand ($\mathbf C_i^{\text{demand}}=\mathbf M_i^\top\mathbf D_i$) or dynamically from effort ($\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}=q\,\mathbf E_i\odot\mathbf N_i$); \textbf{(iii) the original ungated three-state core} (Sections~\ref{sec:core-model}--\ref{sec:numerical-continuation-corrected}, Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core}), the $\chi=1$, frozen-$A$, macro-and-detritus-eliminated limit with state $(N,Z,E)$, mathematically self-consistent but not enforcing $E\le E_{\max}$; \textbf{(iv) the effort-saturation-corrected three-state core} (Section~\ref{sec:effort-saturation-fix}, Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core-corrected}), identical state space to (iii) but with the whole right-hand side multiplied by $(1-E/E_{\max})$, boundary-exact by construction and sharing (iii)'s closed-form equilibrium exactly while relocating its Hopf and fold thresholds; \textbf{(v) the four-state core} (Section~\ref{sec:four-state-core}), which restores $A^{\text{act}}$ to variant (iii)'s state space as a fourth state (using the ungated effort law of variant (iii), not variant (iv); Section~\ref{sec:four-state-model} flags this explicitly) but, like variant (iii), still has no detritus compartment; \textbf{(vi) the unified stoichiometric core}, itself comprising a rejected sigma-gated form (Eq.~\eqref{eq:unified-core-v1}, Section~\ref{sec:unified-core-model}, verified to lack the manuscript's central delay-induced Hopf mechanism) and a repaired hybrid form (unified core v2, Eq.~\eqref{eq:unified-core-v2}, Section~\ref{sec:unified-core-fixed}), the latter built on a scalar primitive-flux state space $(X,U,E)$ distinct from the $(N,Z,E)$ or $(N,A^{\text{act}},Z,E)$ state spaces of variants (iii)--(v); \textbf{(vii) two additive extensions to the three-state core}, the two-channel liquidation decomposition (Section~\ref{sec:two-channel}, same state space and equilibrium as its base core) and the thermodynamic-tether extension (Section~\ref{sec:thermodynamic-tether}, which adds a capital state $K_{\text{cap}}$ to variant (iii)'s state space specifically and is not used in any of this manuscript's core quantitative results); \textbf{(viii) a hybrid effort law} (Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core-hybrid}, Section~\ref{sec:effort-saturation-fix}), identical state space to (iii)/(iv), adding a single term $-\mu_EE$ outside variant (iv)'s multiplicative gate to make the $E=E_{\max}$ boundary strictly inward rather than weakly invariant, while preserving variant (iv)'s delay-dependent institutional gain; not used in any of this manuscript's core quantitative results, and offered as an additive candidate rather than a replacement for variant (iv). \textbf{(ix) the canonical model}: the four-state core under the effort-saturation-corrected effort law (Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core-corrected} applied to the four-state system, Section~\ref{sec:four-state-model}) --- the default referent for untagged threshold statements throughout this manuscript, of which variants (iii)--(v) are the frozen-$A$ and ungated limit/derivation forms. A formula that is correct in one variant is not automatically correct in another once that variant's compartment set differs; each such case is flagged explicitly at the point of use below, Appendix~\ref{sec:symbol-table} tabulates every symbol deliberately reused across these variants, and Section~\ref{sec:core-model}'s threshold registry (with its accompanying nomenclature rule) is the authoritative source for which numerical threshold belongs to which of variants (iii), (iv), (v), and (vi).

Section~\ref{sec:four-state-core} presents a distinct extension, additive to rather than a revision of the three-state core above: it shows that the frozen abiotic pool cannot be formally eliminated at the model's baseline parameters, restores it as a fourth core state, and reports the results of a dedicated empirical search for real-world confirmation of the resulting institutional-delay threshold. That search finds no clean, unconfounded case among more than thirty candidates examined, a negative result we treat as a primary empirical finding of this work in its own right, distinct from the core's mathematical results.

\section{Spatial Heterogeneity and the Vector Projection}
\label{sec:spatial-heterogeneity}
Many elemental inventories are, to a good approximation, materially closed at the planetary scale on the timescales this manuscript considers (Section~\ref{sec:unified-ontology} makes this precise): almost every atom of carbon, phosphorus, and zinc relevant to human economic activity is already present, and human activity overwhelmingly reconfigures matter from one form and location to another rather than creating or destroying it. The planet as a whole, however, is not thermodynamically closed --- it is \emph{energetically} open, continuously receiving low-entropy solar radiation and exporting long-wave radiation --- and this energetic throughput, not material creation, is what powers biological regeneration. In a sustainable system, matter cycles continuously: the nitrogen in a protein was once in the soil, then in a plant, then in an animal, then in a decomposer, then in the soil again. In the idealised closed-cycle limit there is no ``waste'' and no ``depletion''---only a rhythmically paced reconfiguration of matter through temporary configurations; real natural systems also contain slow, effectively irreversible sinks (burial, refractory organic matter), represented in this framework by the inert reservoir of Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}. The defining act of industrial civilisation was to break these loops open into a straight line. In this reading the economy is not an exogenous forcing acting on a passive substrate: it is an internal, autocatalytic sub-network of the biosphere's material cycles, and its flows are part of the same conserved ledger the framework tracks.

Ecological systems are spatially heterogeneous and high-dimensional. Regeneration functions (logistic, Allee) are nonlinear; therefore, the regeneration of an aggregate stock is not equal to the sum of local regenerations---an instance of Jensen's inequality. A policy that observes only coarse-grained aggregates (total crop tonnage, GDP, aggregate biocapacity) can therefore miss local collapses until they propagate to a scale that breaches the aggregate threshold. Local micro-collapses---soil zinc depletion, aquifer subsidence, pollinator crash---can be entirely invisible in the projected macro-indicators until they manifest as a sudden drop in the aggregate flow.

Human economic institutions do not observe the full spatial field; they operate on coarse-grained, sector-aggregated metrics. This projection is not an innocent simplification. Because the macro-indicators appear stable, economic policy continues to inject capital and energy into extraction, accelerating the very local degradations that the aggregate hides. The relationship between the full spatial reality and the coarse-grained cockpit is therefore \textbf{enactive}: the projection is not merely a description of what we measure; it is the active decision environment that drives overshoot. The diagnostics are accordingly chosen for causal relevance --- deficit, depletion horizon, stress index --- rather than for observability convenience: a variable that is easy to count (aggregate biomass, GDP) is not thereby the one that drives the system's survival, and the framework deliberately tracks the quantities that do.

The remainder of the general framework works deliberately within this projected vector space. We do so not as an approximation of a spatially explicit field theory, but as a representation of the information environment that actually governs human economic activity. The state variables are the aggregated stock vectors \(\mathbf{N}_i\), the service vectors \(\mathbf{S}_i\), and the waste vectors \(\mathbf{W}_j\) that emerge from this coarse-graining. We explicitly disclaim that the vector equations are a rigorous projection of a higher-dimensional field; they are a phenomenological model of the dynamics that unfold within the blinded cockpit of aggregate indicators. A fully spatially resolved generalisation---which would require defining a projection kernel, deriving moment-closure terms for nonlinear aggregation, and validating the bifurcation structure under that projection---is a distinct research program that we leave to future work. Its leading-order content is already determined by the curvature of the regeneration function: for the logistic law $S(N)=rN(1-N/K)$, the aggregate regeneration of a spatially heterogeneous stock satisfies \[ \mathbb E[S(N)] = S(\bar N) - \frac{r}{K}\,\mathrm{Var}(N), \] exactly (the identity holds because $S$ is quadratic; verified numerically). The spatial variance of the stock therefore enters as a strictly negative correction to the aggregate regeneration --- the quantitative form of the aggregation bias --- so two systems with identical mean stock but different spatial variance have different regenerative capacity, and a mean-only observer overestimates aggregate regeneration by $(r/K)\,\mathrm{Var}(N)$. The full programme (projection kernel, higher moments, and the bifurcation structure under the projection) remains future work; the analogue of this identity for the Ricker recruitment form, and a closed dynamical treatment of the variance, are developed in Appendix~\ref{sec:scaffold}.

A simple thought experiment illustrates why this aggregation bias matters even when total mass is held fixed. Consider redistributing a forest's total biomass, unchanged in mass, volume, and metabolic rate, into a handful of massive trees rather than a large, spatially dispersed population. The aggregate accounting is unaffected: total stock and total flow are identical under either configuration. But several services this stock vector is meant to deliver---soil stabilisation, local hydrological regulation, habitat structure---depend on how that mass is distributed in space, not merely on how much of it exists, and a small number of concentrated stocks is also more exposed to a single localised shock than the same mass spread across many independent units. Both effects sit outside what the present, spatially aggregated state variables $\mathbf{N}_i$ can represent: the model has no mechanism, at present, for a component's effective service yield or exposure to depend on the spatial or statistical configuration of the underlying stock, only on its magnitude. This does not indicate an error in the equations as written; it is a direct consequence of working in the coarse-grained projection just described, and it is the reason the spatially explicit extension noted above, and a companion extension incorporating idiosyncratic, unit-level shocks, are left as distinct items of future work rather than folded into the current state variables.

\section{The Accounting Core: Stock Vectors and Service Vectors}
\label{sec:accounting-core}
We adopt a dual-unit convention, measuring ecological state variables in their native physical dimensions and economic variables in monetary or index units, with prices acting as exchange rates.

\subsection{State Variables}
For each resource system \(i = 1,\dots,n\) we define:
\begin{itemize}
\item \(\mathbf{N}_i(t) = \bigl(N_{i,1},\, N_{i,2},\, \dots,\, N_{i,m_i}\bigr)^\top\) --- \textbf{natural capital
      stock vector} (physical units: e.g., GtC, km\(^3\), tonnes, dimensionless abundance indices). Each
      component \(N_{i,\kappa}\) is a distinct form of natural capital (soil organic carbon, plant-available zinc,
      groundwater volume, pollinator abundance, \dots).
\item \(\mathbf{S}_i(t) = \bigl(S_{i,1},\, S_{i,2},\, \dots,\, S_{i,k_i}\bigr)^\top\) --- \textbf{service vector}
      (flow, physical units per year). Each component \(S_{i,c}\) is the sustainable yield of a specific
      ecosystem service (biomass, zinc yield, carbon sequestration rate, \dots).
\item \(\mathbf{D}_i(t) = \bigl(D_{i,1},\, D_{i,2},\, \dots,\, D_{i,k_i}\bigr)^\top\) --- \textbf{demand vector}
      (flow, same units as \(\mathbf{S}_i\)). This is the rate at which human activities extract or degrade
      each service.
\item \(\mathbf{W}_j(t)\) --- \textbf{waste stock vector} for environmental compartment \(j\) (physical units:
      GtC, km\(^3\), tonnes, \dots). Accumulated dispersed matter (atmospheric CO\(_2\), oceanic nitrate,
      landfill plastics).
\item \(\mathbf{I}_j(t)\) --- \textbf{inert reservoir vector} for compartment \(j\) (same units as \(\mathbf{W}_j\)).
      Matter degraded beyond economic or ecological reprocessing.
\item \(\mathbf{P}_{i}(t)\) --- \textbf{economic product stock} for resource system \(i\) (physical units, same as \(\mathbf{N}_i\)). This tracks the mass of extracted matter that durable goods, structures, or other long-lived products incorporate before it eventually decays back to waste, closing the material loop.
\item \(K(t)\) --- manufactured capital (monetary units, e.g., trillion USD).
\item \(K_{\text{ref}}\) --- reference capital level (monetary units).
\item \(L(t)\) --- labour/population (dimensionless index; \(L_{\text{ref}}\equiv 1\)).
\item \(A(t)\) --- total factor productivity (monetary flow per year).
\item \(Q(t)\) --- total economic output (monetary flow per year).
\item \(\mathbf{p}_i(t)\) --- price vector for resource \(i\) (monetary units per physical unit of each service).
\item \(T(t)\) --- technological reconfiguration level (dimensionless).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Abiotic Reservoir, Inert Sink, and the Global Mass Invariant}
\label{sec:abiotic}
To close the material balance physically, we introduce four additional state vectors:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $\mathbf{A}^{\text{act}}_i(t)$ --- \textbf{active abiotic pool vector} for each resource system $i$, measured in the same physical units as $\mathbf{N}_i$ (e.g., GtC, km$^3$, tonnes). This is the bioavailable fraction of the abiotic environment — soil solution nutrients, the oceanic mixed layer, shallow groundwater, atmospheric $\text{CO}_2$ — that directly supplies the regeneration flux $\mathbf{R}_i$ and receives technologically recovered matter (Section~\ref{sec:tech-recovery}). It is \textbf{finite and comparable in scale to $\mathbf N_i$}, not effectively infinite: this is essential (see below) for it to have a well-defined, finite recovery timescale and for recovered/recharged mass to have a non-negligible effect on regeneration.
    \item $\mathbf{A}^{\text{geo}}_i(t)$ --- \textbf{geological reservoir vector}, physically distinct from $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ (deep aquifers, bedrock mineral pools, the deep ocean, long-term carbon stores). It is effectively infinite on policy-relevant timescales ($10^2$--$10^4\times$ the active stock) and slowly recharges $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ via natural weathering, upwelling, or diffusion at rate $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}$; it also acts as the destination for direct anthropogenic mining (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}, below), since resources like fossil groundwater or phosphate rock are extracted from deep, slowly-recharging deposits, not from the fast-cycling active pool.
    \item $\mathbf{U}_i(t)$ --- \textbf{detritus vector}, physically distinct from both the anthropogenic waste stock $\mathbf W_j$ and the abiotic pools above: dead biomass turned over from $\mathbf N_i$ (litterfall, root exudate, coarse woody debris) that is still within the natural biogeochemical cycle and decomposes back to $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ on its own, independent of any recovery technology $T(t)$ (defined and motivated fully below).
    \item $\mathbf{I}_j(t)$ --- \textbf{inert reservoir vector} for each waste compartment $j$, as defined above. This pool receives matter that has left the active anthropogenic--ecological loop (e.g., geological burial).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Why a single abiotic compartment is structurally inadequate.} A single reservoir $\mathbf A_i$ cannot consistently serve both roles required of it: supplying $\mathbf R_i$ (which requires it to be dynamically responsive, so that recovered or recharged mass measurably affects regeneration) and being ``effectively infinite... typically much larger than the actively cycling stocks'' (which is exactly the frozen-$A$ approximation the logistic growth law needs to recover, Section~\ref{sec:frozen-A}). These two requirements are in direct tension: if $\mathbf A_i\gg A_0$ so that $R(N,A)\approx rN(1-N/K)$ holds (the frozen-$A$ limit), then $\partial R/\partial A_i\to0$ by the same limit, and technological recovery (Section~\ref{sec:tech-recovery}) dilutes any mass it routes into $\mathbf A_i$ into an effectively infinite pool with no measurable effect on regeneration — the loop is topologically closed (mass is conserved on paper) but dynamically inert (the recovered mass can never measurably re-enter the active cycle). Separately, because such a reservoir has no recharge term when only $\mathbf B_i$ depletes it, the ``recovery timescale'' $\mathcal T^{\text{rec}}_{i,c}$ used in the Depletion Stress Index (Section~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}) is undefined: with zero extraction, a single reservoir with turnover $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}>\mathbf 0$ and no recharge drains monotonically and never recovers, confirmed by direct integration of $\dot A=-B(N,A)$ with zero extraction and no recharge term, in which $A$ decreases without bound rather than relaxing back to any equilibrium. We resolve both problems by splitting the reservoir into the two compartments above. $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ is small enough to be dynamically responsive, so recovery and recharge matter, and it possesses a finite relaxation timescale, uniform in $N$ — provided its resupply from $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$ takes the form of a genuine bidirectional exchange rather than a one-way transfer, since a one-way transfer alone reintroduces a divergent relaxation time in exactly the mature-stock regime this diagnostic is meant to cover (Section~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}). $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$, meanwhile, retains the frozen, effectively-infinite character the logistic-growth-law derivation needs (Section~\ref{sec:frozen-A}), but no longer also needs to serve as the dynamically responsive target of recovery.

\paragraph{Net regeneration is not the same flux as gross abiotic uptake.} A first candidate for the active pool's dynamics would set $d\mathbf{A}^{\text{act}}_i/dt=-\mathbf{R}_i(\mathbf{N}_i,\mathbf{W})$, i.e., draw it down by exactly the \emph{net} regeneration flux that also appears in the natural capital equation (\ref{eq:nvec-corrected}). This is inadequate: net regeneration is a bookkeeping \emph{difference} between gross biological uptake and gross biological turnover (respiration, litterfall, exudation, natural mortality), and at any interior equilibrium of the stock — in particular at carrying capacity, $\mathbf N_i=\mathbf K_i$, where $\mathbf R_i(\mathbf K_i,\cdot)=\mathbf 0$ by definition of carrying capacity — this net flux vanishes identically. A model that lets net regeneration alone drive abiotic depletion would therefore imply that a stable, mature stock draws \emph{nothing} from its abiotic support, which contradicts basic ecological stoichiometry (a mature forest still draws continuously on soil nutrients and water even while its standing biomass is constant) and would leave the model structurally incapable of representing the very phenomenon motivating this metric: an ecosystem that appears stable in its stock while silently drawing down the abiotic capital that sustains it (the ``productivity illusion,'' Section~\ref{sec:introduction}).

We therefore introduce a \textbf{turnover parameter} $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\ge\mathbf 0$ (units yr$^{-1}$, one component per stock, mirroring the $\kappa$ of Section~\ref{sec:two-channel}) and define the \textbf{gross abiotic uptake}
\[
\mathbf{B}_i(\mathbf{N}_i,\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i,\mathbf{W}) = \mathbf{R}_i(\mathbf{N}_i,\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i,\mathbf{W}) + \boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf{N}_i\odot\frac{\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i}{\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i+\mathbf A_{0,i}},
\]
i.e., net regeneration plus a turnover flux proportional to the standing stock itself, so that $\mathbf B_i>\mathbf 0$ even when $\mathbf R_i=\mathbf 0$ (whenever $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}>\mathbf 0$ and $\mathbf N_i>\mathbf 0$). The form does not guarantee $\mathbf B_i\ge\mathbf 0$ when $\mathbf R_i<\mathbf 0$ (i.e., when the stock exceeds carrying capacity and net regeneration is negative); $\mathbf B_i$ remains positive in the parameter regime used here provided $\kappa_{A,i}N_i\ge|R_i|$ there, but a primitive-flux formulation separating gross assimilation from mortality would make the sign guaranteed by construction. Here $\mathbf R_i(\mathbf N_i,\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i,\mathbf W)$ takes the \emph{active} pool as its argument, exactly as in Section~\ref{sec:frozen-A}. \textbf{The turnover term carries the same saturating factor $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i/(\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i+\mathbf A_{0,i})$ that already multiplies $\mathbf R_i$}, rather than depending on $\mathbf N_i$ alone: this is a donor-limitation correction, not a cosmetic addition. Both terms of $\mathbf B_i$ physically withdraw mass \emph{from} $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ (Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic} below), so both must vanish as $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i\to\mathbf 0^+$ for the same reason a donor-limited flux cannot draw from an empty donor; the undivided form $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf N_i$ fails this requirement (it remains strictly positive at $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i=0$ whenever $\mathbf N_i>\mathbf 0$), which is confirmed by direct simulation to drive $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ negative from an admissible initial condition (small $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i(0)$, undeveloped detritus pool $\mathbf U_i(0)\approx\mathbf 0$, moderate-to-high $\mathbf N_i$) at the model's baseline parameters. The correction leaves the frozen-$A$ limit exactly unchanged, since $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i/(\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i+\mathbf A_{0,i})\to\mathbf 1$ in the same limit $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i/\mathbf A_{0,i}\to\infty$ that Section~\ref{sec:frozen-A} already requires for $\mathbf R_i$ itself, confirmed symbolically: both the corrected and uncorrected forms of $\mathbf B_i$ converge to the identical limit $\mathbf R_i\to r\mathbf N_i(1-\mathbf N_i/\mathbf K_i)$ plus $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf N_i$ as $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i\to\infty$, so no result stated in terms of that limit is affected.

\paragraph{A one-way recharge is not a restoring force, and the relaxation timescale it implies vanishes exactly where the productivity illusion is most acute.} Consider instead resupplying the active pool through a one-way transfer $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\odot\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$ from the (nearly constant, on policy timescales) geological reservoir, independent of $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$'s own level. This is inadequate as the basis for a recovery timescale: the only $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$-dependent term left in the active-pool equation would then be $\mathbf R_i(\mathbf N_i,\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i,\mathbf W)$ itself (via $\mathbf B_i$), whose restoring strength is $\partial \mathbf B_i/\partial \mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i=\partial\mathbf R_i/\partial\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ — and by direct computation, $\partial R/\partial A^{\text{act}}=rN(1-N/K)A_0/(A^{\text{act}}+A_0)^2$ vanishes \emph{exactly} as $N\to K$, since the $(1-N/K)$ factor is common to $R$ and its derivative alike. This is not a minor edge effect but a genuine divergence: numerically integrating the perturbation-relaxation experiment of Section~\ref{sec:abiotic} at fixed $N$ approaching $K$ shows the $1/e$ relaxation time $\mathcal T^{\text{rec}}_{i,c}$ growing without bound (from $\sim7\times10^3$ time units at $N=0.5K$ to $>2\times10^6$ at $N=0.9999K$, diverging exactly at $N=K$), \emph{not} settling to a finite value as a one-way recharge alone would suggest. This is a serious problem specifically for the diagnostic this section exists to support: the Depletion Stress Index $\Sigma^{\text{act}}_{i,c}=\mathcal H^{\text{act}}_{i,c}/\mathcal T^{\text{rec},\text{act}}_{i,c}$ (Section~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}) would collapse to zero exactly at $N\approx K$ — i.e., exactly in the mature-stock, ``productivity illusion'' regime (Section~\ref{sec:introduction}) that the two-pool architecture and this index exist to diagnose — falsely certifying a mature ecosystem that is silently mining its active abiotic pool at rate $\kappa_{A,i}N$ as having zero chronic stress, because the biological restoring feedback alone happens to vanish there.

The root cause is that $\partial R/\partial A^{\text{act}}$ measures the sensitivity of \emph{biomass accumulation} to abiotic availability, not the buffering capacity of the abiotic pool itself; a mature ecosystem's net growth being insensitive to nutrient availability (because it is already at its ceiling $K$) says nothing about whether the active pool has any independent tendency to re-equilibrate toward a baseline level. We therefore replace the one-way recharge with a genuine \textbf{bidirectional geochemical exchange} with the geological reservoir, which restores $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ toward an equilibrium level $\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i$ that weathering/dissolution/sorption equilibria set independent of the biological state:
\[
\text{exchange}_i = \boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\odot\bigl(\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i - \mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i(t)\bigr),
\]
so that the gross biological flux depletes the active pool, the geochemical exchange replenishes it (acting as a genuine restoring force, independent of $N$, whenever $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ deviates from $\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i$), and technological recovery (Section~\ref{sec:tech-recovery}) adds further mass:
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\mathbf{A}^{\text{act}}_i}{dt} = -\mathbf{B}_i(\mathbf{N}_i,\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i,\mathbf{W}) + \boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\odot\bigl(\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i-\mathbf{A}^{\text{act}}_i(t)\bigr) + \sum_j \boldsymbol{\chi}^{\text{rec}}_{i,j}\,\bigl( \boldsymbol{\rho}_{i,j}(T) \odot \mathbf{W}_j(t) \bigr). \label{eq:abiotic}
\end{equation}
\paragraph{Forward invariance of $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i\ge\mathbf 0$ (verified exactly).} At $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i=\mathbf 0$, both terms of $\mathbf B_i$ vanish by the donor-limiting factor introduced above ($\mathbf R_i(\mathbf N_i,\mathbf 0,\mathbf W)=\mathbf 0$ and $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf N_i\odot\mathbf 0/(\mathbf 0+\mathbf A_{0,i})=\mathbf 0$), so Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic} reduces there to $d\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i/dt=\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\odot\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i+\sum_j\boldsymbol\chi^{\text{rec}}_{i,j}(\boldsymbol\rho_{i,j}(T)\odot\mathbf W_j)\ge\mathbf 0$ identically, since $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i},\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i,\boldsymbol\chi^{\text{rec}}_{i,j},\boldsymbol\rho_{i,j},\mathbf W_j\ge\mathbf 0$ throughout (confirmed symbolically in \texttt{verify\_abiotic\_nonnegativity.py}). $\Omega^{\text{act}}=\{\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i\ge\mathbf 0\}$ is accordingly forward invariant, closing a gap the undivided $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf N_i$ term leaves open (that term does not vanish at $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i=\mathbf 0$, so nothing in the undivided equation prevents $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ from being driven negative). \textbf{This correction does not extend to $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$}: the direct mining flow $\mathbf C^A_i$ (introduced below) is not itself donor-limited by $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$, so sustained mining can still drive the geological reservoir formally negative once it is physically exhausted; because $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$'s own timescale is, by construction, effectively infinite on any policy-relevant horizon (Section~\ref{sec:frozen-A}), this is flagged here as a known, lower-priority limitation of the same kind (a representational artifact of an unbounded-donor ODE reaching physical exhaustion) rather than a correction attempted in this manuscript.
Linearising the exchange-plus-uptake term around any quasi-equilibrium gives a restoring rate of $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}+\partial\mathbf B_i/\partial\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$, bounded below by $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}>\mathbf 0$ \emph{independent of $N$}, so that $\mathcal T^{\text{rec},\text{act}}_{i,c}\le 1/\omega_{A,i,c}$ uniformly, including exactly at $N=K$ where the biological term alone vanishes; repeating the perturbation-relaxation experiment above with the exchange term included confirms this numerically, giving $\mathcal T^{\text{rec}}_{i,c}\to1/\omega_{A,i,c}$ to within numerical tolerance for every $N$ tested, from $N=0.5K$ through $N=K$ exactly, with no divergence. Physically, $\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i$ represents the geochemical equilibrium level the active pool relaxes to via mineral solubility, sorption/desorption, or gas exchange (e.g., the equilibrium partial pressure of atmospheric $\text{CO}_2$ against dissolved ocean carbon, or the equilibrium concentration of a soil nutrient against its mineral-bound reservoir), a process that occurs on geochemical timescales set by $\omega_{A,i}$ regardless of the biological uptake rate. Because $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$ stays effectively constant on policy timescales (Section~\ref{sec:frozen-A}), we take $\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i$ to be a slowly-varying function of $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$ (e.g., proportional to it, for a linear sorption isotherm), so that a genuine long-term decline in the geological reservoir still, correctly, lowers the active pool's equilibrium level and hence $\mathcal H^{\text{act}}_{i,c}$; on the policy-relevant timescales this manuscript analyses, $\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i$ can be treated as a fixed parameter without loss of the qualitative structure.



\paragraph{The equilibrium target must be defined relative to the turnover it has to offset, not specified independently of it.} Because the geochemical exchange is the \emph{only} restoring force at $N=K$ (Section~\ref{sec:frozen-A} shows the biological term vanishes there), the active pool's steady state at carrying capacity solves $0=-\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf K_i+\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\odot(\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i-\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i)$, giving $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i\big|_{N=K}=\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i-\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf K_i/\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}$. If $\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i$ is specified as an independent constant, this steady state is only physically valid (non-negative) when the inequality $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\odot\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i>\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf K_i$ happens to hold between two otherwise-unrelated calibration constants---an inequality that is not automatically respected across the full literature-anchored ranges of $\kappa_A$ and $\omega_A$ in Table~\ref{tab:params} (e.g., the table's own baseline $\kappa_A=0.05$, $\omega_A=10^{-3}$ against $K=100$ requires $A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}>5000$, an order of magnitude above the table's stated range for that parameter). We therefore define the geochemical equilibrium level as a \textbf{derived} quantity rather than an independently specified one, parameterised explicitly by whatever steady-state return flux $\mathbf D^{\text{ret}}_i\ge\mathbf 0$ already reaches the active pool through compartments introduced elsewhere in this section (a placeholder resolved fully once the detritus pathway is introduced below; until then, $\mathbf D^{\text{ret}}_i=\mathbf 0$, since no such compartment has yet been defined):
\begin{equation}
\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i := \mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq,intrinsic}}_i + \bigl(\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf K_i - \mathbf D^{\text{ret}}_i\bigr)/\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i},
\label{eq:aeq-derived}
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq,intrinsic}}_i>\mathbf 0$ is the (now freely choosable, literature-anchored) geochemical target in the absence of biological turnover. Substituting Eq.~\eqref{eq:aeq-derived} into the steady-state condition above gives $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i\big|_{N=K}=\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq,intrinsic}}_i+\mathbf D^{\text{ret}}_i/\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}-\mathbf D^{\text{ret}}_i/\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}=\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq,intrinsic}}_i>\mathbf 0$ identically, for \emph{any} choice of $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}>\mathbf 0,\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}>\mathbf 0$ within their stated ranges and \emph{any} $\mathbf D^{\text{ret}}_i$ consistent with the compartments actually present: the active pool's positivity at carrying capacity no longer depends on an unenforced inequality between independently calibrated constants, but follows from the algebraic structure of Eq.~\eqref{eq:aeq-derived} itself, and does so \emph{regardless of which model variant (Section~\ref{sec:variant-registry}) is in use}, since $\mathbf D^{\text{ret}}_i$ absorbs that variant-dependence explicitly rather than leaving it unstated. This is a relabelling of the same two physical parameters $\kappa_A,\omega_A$ (plus the now-explicit $D^{\text{ret}}_i$), not a new mechanism: $\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq,intrinsic}}_i$ carries the same physical interpretation (weathering/dissolution/sorption equilibrium level) that $\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i$ carried before, evaluated in the hypothetical absence of both the standing stock's metabolic draw and any compartment separately returning matter to offset it.

\paragraph{The limit $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\to\mathbf 0$ is a boundary condition, not a modelling failure.} The bidirectional exchange formulation of this section is appropriate for active pools with a genuine geochemical restoring force operating on policy-relevant timescales (soil nutrient solutions, the oceanic mixed layer, shallow, actively-recharging groundwater). For a genuinely non-renewable active pool---one with no meaningful exchange with any larger reservoir on any timescale relevant to this model, such as a fossil aquifer with negligible natural recharge or a deep mineral deposit accessed without any recharge pathway---the physically correct limit is $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\to\mathbf 0^+$ (small but strictly positive), not an exact zero. Because $\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i$ is itself defined in terms of $1/\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}$ (Eq.~\eqref{eq:aeq-derived}), setting $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}$ to exactly zero is a removable-singularity limit, not a simple deletion of the exchange term: the two divergences cancel, and Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic}'s exchange flux $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\odot(\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i-\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i)$ tends to the finite residual $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf K_i-\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\odot\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i\to\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf K_i$ (confirmed by direct expansion and by simulation), not to zero. The physically meaningful statement is instead about the \emph{recovery timescale}: because $\mathcal T^{\text{rec},\text{act}}_{i,c}\le1/\omega_{A,i,c}$ (established above) diverges as $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\to\mathbf 0^+$, the active pool becomes practically non-recovering on any finite policy horizon well before $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}$ reaches zero, confirmed numerically: the steady state at $N=K$ remains exactly $\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq,intrinsic}}_i$ for every $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}>\mathbf 0$ tested (by construction of Eq.~\eqref{eq:aeq-derived}), but the time to reach it grows without bound, so a resource with $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}$ small enough is correctly diagnosed as functionally non-renewable by the Depletion Stress Index $\Sigma^{\text{act}}_{i,c}$ (Section~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}) despite never formally losing its restoring force. For resources genuinely extracted with \emph{no} exchange pathway at all (rather than an extremely slow one), the correct model is not this formulation with $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\to\mathbf 0$, but the single-compartment, non-recharging limit obtained by omitting the exchange term entirely, $\dot{\mathbf A}^{\text{act}}_i=-\mathbf B_i(\mathbf N_i,\mathbf W)+\boldsymbol\gamma_{U,i}\odot\mathbf U_i+\sum_j\boldsymbol\chi^{\text{rec}}_{i,j}(\boldsymbol\rho_{i,j}(T)\odot\mathbf W_j)$ (with $\mathbf U_i$ still governed by Eq.~\eqref{eq:detritus} exactly as before, since the detritus pathway is independent of the geochemical exchange rate $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}$ being set to zero)---the same limit the direct-mining channel $\mathbf C^A_i$ already uses for the geological reservoir (Section~\ref{sec:frozen-A})---which is a distinct modelling choice from taking $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}$ small within the bidirectional-exchange equation, not an algebraic limit of it. Distinguishing which of these two representations applies is a resource-specific empirical question (Table~\ref{tab:params} lists $\omega_A\in[10^{-4},10^{-2}]\,\text{yr}^{-1}$ as an illustrative range for renewable active pools; a genuinely fossil active pool with no exchange pathway should use the no-exchange formulation directly, not a vanishingly small $\omega_A$ within Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic}), not a defect in the governing equation. \textbf{This discussion is scoped to $\mathbf D^{\text{ret}}_i=\mathbf 0$}, the placeholder value in effect at this point in the text (Eq.~\eqref{eq:aeq-derived} above), before the detritus compartment is introduced; once detritus is defined below and $\mathbf D^{\text{ret}}_i$ is resolved to $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf K_i$ for that variant, the correction term in Eq.~\eqref{eq:aeq-derived} cancels completely and this removable-singularity discussion becomes moot for that variant specifically (though it remains exactly as stated here for the detritus-free four-state core, Section~\ref{sec:four-state-core}).

The geological reservoir equation correspondingly states the exchange term with matching sign, preserving mass conservation exactly as before:
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\mathbf{A}^{\text{geo}}_i}{dt} = -\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\odot\bigl(\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i-\mathbf{A}^{\text{act}}_i(t)\bigr) - \mathbf{C}^A_i(t), \label{eq:abiotic-geo}
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf C^A_i(t)\ge\mathbf 0$ is the direct anthropogenic mining flow introduced below. This reduces to a one-way, near-constant source into the active pool only in the special case $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i\ll\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i$ (far below equilibrium), which is why a simpler, one-way formulation of the recharge term can appear adequate under a restricted numerical check but fails as $N\to K$ (see the preceding paragraph).

To keep the total mass invariant (below), the additional turnover flux withdrawn from $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ must reappear somewhere in the active loop; physically, it is exactly the litterfall/respiration/exudate flux that a living stock continuously returns to the environment even at constant biomass. This flux is \textbf{natural biogeochemical detritus}---dead organic matter still undergoing decomposition entirely within the non-anthropogenic carbon/nutrient cycle (leaf litter, root exudate, coarse woody debris, soil organic matter not yet mineralised)---and is physically distinct from \textbf{anthropogenic waste} $\mathbf W_j$ (landfill material, atmospheric pollutant loading, industrial effluent): the former decomposes and re-enters the abiotic pool on its own, on an ordinary ecological timescale, whether or not any human technology exists to recover it; the latter requires an explicit technological recovery pathway $\boldsymbol\rho_{i,j}(T)$ (Section~\ref{sec:tech-recovery}) precisely because it does \emph{not} decompose back into a bioavailable form on its own. Routing $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf N_i$ into $\mathbf W_j$ would conflate these two categories, making the model's own baseline (technology-free) limit, $\boldsymbol\rho_{i,j}=\mathbf 0$, permanently strand this detritus flux in the waste compartment with no way back to $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ --- i.e., a pre-industrial ecosystem with no recovery technology at all would, under that routing, silently and irreversibly drain its own active abiotic pool into an inert sink purely as an artefact of the compartment definitions, contradicting the framework's opening premise (Section~\ref{sec:introduction}) that ``in a sustainable system, matter cycles continuously... there is no `waste'.''

We therefore introduce a dedicated \textbf{detritus vector} $\mathbf U_i(t)$ (same physical units as $\mathbf N_i$), representing biomass that has left the living stock but remains within the natural biogeochemical cycle, with its own first-order decomposition dynamics back to the active abiotic pool at rate $\boldsymbol\gamma_{U,i}>\mathbf 0$ (units yr$^{-1}$; $1/\boldsymbol\gamma_{U,i}$ is the detritus mean residence time, an independently measurable quantity reported across ecosystem types, e.g.\ from $\sim$0.25--2.5 years in tropical forest litter to several decades in boreal systems, corresponding to Olson-type decomposition constants $k\in[0.02,4]\,\text{yr}^{-1}$ \cite{olson1963}). The turnover flux is routed here, not to $\mathbf W_j$:
\begin{align}
\frac{d\mathbf U_i}{dt} &= \boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf N_i\odot\frac{\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i}{\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i+\mathbf A_{0,i}} - \boldsymbol\gamma_{U,i}\odot\mathbf U_i, \label{eq:detritus}\\
\frac{d\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i}{dt}&\ \text{gains an additional term}\quad+\boldsymbol\gamma_{U,i}\odot\mathbf U_i,\notag
\end{align}
added to Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic}, so that natural turnover leaves $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ immediately (via $\mathbf B_i$, unchanged from before) and returns to it after a decomposition delay of order $1/\boldsymbol\gamma_{U,i}$, entirely bypassing $\mathbf W_j$, $\boldsymbol\rho_{i,j}(T)$, and the inert sink $\mathbf I_j$. This is mass-neutral by construction, since Eq.~\eqref{eq:detritus}'s gain term for $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ exactly matches its own loss term as $\mathbf U_i$ decomposes, and it recovers the correct pre-industrial limit: setting $\boldsymbol\rho_{i,j}=\mathbf 0$ (no recovery technology) no longer strands any mass, since natural decomposition of $\mathbf U_i$ is entirely independent of $T(t)$.
