\paragraph{Resolving $\mathbf D^{\text{ret}}_i$ in Eq.~\eqref{eq:aeq-derived} now that detritus is defined.} With the donor-limiting correction above, the detritus compartment's steady state is $\mathbf U_i^*=\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf N_i\odot[\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i/(\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i+\mathbf A_{0,i})]/\boldsymbol\gamma_{U,i}$ (exact, for any $\boldsymbol\gamma_{U,i}>\mathbf 0$, from Eq.~\eqref{eq:detritus} with $\dot{\mathbf U}_i=\mathbf 0$), so the steady-state detritus-decomposition flux reaching $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ is $\boldsymbol\gamma_{U,i}\odot\mathbf U_i^*=\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf N_i\odot[\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i/(\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i+\mathbf A_{0,i})]$ --- \emph{exactly} equal to the turnover drain in the corrected $\mathbf B_i$ (same paragraph, Section~\ref{sec:abiotic} above), \emph{term by term}, since both now carry the identical donor-limiting factor. This equality no longer requires evaluating at $N=K$ specifically, nor does it require the correction term to reduce to a $N$-independent constant $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf K_i$: the two occurrences of $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf N_i\odot[\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i/(\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i+\mathbf A_{0,i})]$ (one draining $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ via $\mathbf B_i$, one returning to it via detritus decomposition) cancel identically for \emph{any} $\mathbf N_i,\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i\ge\mathbf 0$, confirmed symbolically in \texttt{verify\_abiotic\_nonnegativity.py}. Consequently $\mathbf D^{\text{ret}}_i=\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf K_i$ in Eq.~\eqref{eq:aeq-derived} continues to hold \emph{at the specific equilibrium $N=K$} (where the two cancelling terms both reduce to $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf K_i\odot[\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i/(\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i+\mathbf A_{0,i})]$, itself then cancelling against the matching term inside $\mathbf B_i$ regardless of the donor-limiting factor's value), so the formula simplifies to $\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i=\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq,intrinsic}}_i$ exactly at $N=K$, with \emph{no} dependence on $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}/\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}$ once detritus return is correctly included, exactly as before the donor-limiting correction: the exchange-term correction and detritus return are two distinct restorative mechanisms acting on $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$, and treating them as independent rather than jointly accounting for the turnover drain each targets would compensate that drain twice, silently shifting $\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i$ upward by $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf K_i/\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}$ beyond its intended value. \textbf{This resolution applies specifically to variants of the model that include the detritus compartment $\mathbf U_i$}; the reduced four-state core of Section~\ref{sec:four-state-core}, which has no detritus compartment and hence $\mathbf D^{\text{ret}}_i=\mathbf 0$, correctly retains the full, uncancelled correction term of Eq.~\eqref{eq:aeq-derived}, and its own reported equilibrium values are updated below to reflect the donor-limiting correction (Section~\ref{sec:variant-registry}'s variant registry; verified symbolically in \texttt{verify\_flux\_ledger.py} and \texttt{verify\_abiotic\_nonnegativity.py}, which check both variants explicitly). $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}$'s role as the active pool's restoring-force rate is unchanged by this resolution: at $N=K$, once $\mathbf U_i$ has reached its own steady state, $d\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i/dt=\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\odot(\mathbf A^{\text{act,eq,intrinsic}}_i-\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i)$ exactly, so the restoring force is still governed entirely by $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}$; detritus return cancels only the constant turnover drain, not $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}$'s own restoring role, and the finite-recovery-timescale argument of the paragraph above (bounded below by $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}$, uniformly in $N$) is unaffected. The turnover flux $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf N_i$ accordingly has exactly one destination, $\mathbf U_i$, matching how every other single-purpose flux in this framework is routed (Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected}). Setting $\boldsymbol\gamma_{U,i}\to\infty$ (instantaneous decomposition) recovers the undelayed routing $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i\mathrel{+}=\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf N_i$ as a special case, so this formulation strictly extends, rather than replaces, the mechanism already used elsewhere in this section. Anthropogenic waste $\mathbf W_j$ continues to receive only genuinely anthropogenic flows --- immediate extraction waste, product decay, and waste-damage conversion (Eq.~\eqref{eq:wvec-corrected}) --- consistent with its role throughout the rest of this manuscript.

\paragraph{A second, structurally distinct drain: direct anthropogenic mining acts on the geological reservoir, not the active pool.} The gross uptake $\mathbf B_i$ above is a purely biological/ecological flux — it is proportional to the living stock $\mathbf N_i$ and vanishes when $\mathbf N_i\to\mathbf 0$. This is appropriate for describing an ecosystem's own metabolic draw on the bioavailable, active pool, but it cannot represent the class of real-world overshoot problems in which humans extract directly from a deep, slowly-recharging abiotic deposit \emph{without any living intermediary} — fossil (non-recharging on human timescales) groundwater, phosphate rock, crude oil, and other subsurface mineral deposits are pumped or mined straight out of the geological reservoir $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$, entirely bypassing both $\mathbf N_i$ and $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$. A model that routes every anthropogenic flow through $\mathbf C_i$ (which acts on $\mathbf N_i$ alone; Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected}) has no mechanism for this, and would misattribute all such extraction to biological turnover regardless of how the resource is actually used. We therefore introduce a second, independent extraction channel, the \textbf{direct abiotic mining flow} $\mathbf C^A_i(t)\ge\mathbf 0$ (same physical units as $\mathbf C_i$), governed by its own dynamical mining-effort variable exactly as its own dynamical effort variable $\mathbf E_i$ governs $\mathbf C_i=q\mathbf E_i\odot\mathbf N_i$ (Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected}), draining $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$ directly (Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic-geo}).

Because $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$'s only source is nothing (it is the ultimate, non-renewed reservoir on any policy-relevant timescale; geological replenishment operates on $>10^4$\,yr timescales explicitly outside this model's horizon), its own recharge/sustainable-yield is legitimately zero, and \emph{any} sustained $\mathbf C^A_i>\mathbf 0$ constitutes liquidation of the geological reservoir in the same sense that any extraction exceeding $S(N)$ liquidates natural capital (Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected}). Splitting $\mathbf C^A_i$ against the gross biological uptake $\mathbf B_i$, treating $\mathbf B_i$ as the abiotic pool's ``sustainable yield,'' would be a category error: $\mathbf B_i$ is itself a sink draining a \emph{different} compartment ($\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$, via Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic}), not a regenerative capacity of $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$, so comparing $\mathbf C^A_i$ against it would arbitrarily launder mined geological material into a ``sustainable'' bookkeeping category whenever the local biosphere happened to have high metabolic turnover, with no causal connection between the two quantities. The physically correct diagnostic instead splits $\mathbf C^A_i$ against $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$'s actual (zero, on this model's timescale) recharge: $\bar{\mathbf C}^A_i=\mathbf 0$ and $\boldsymbol\Lambda^A_i=\mathbf C^A_i$ identically — i.e., this diagnostic always classifies direct mining as full liquidation, exactly matching the physical reality that pumping a fossil aquifer or mining phosphate rock has no sustainable-yield component on human timescales.

This diagnostic verdict, however, must not be read as a physical routing instruction, for exactly the reason argued in Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected} for the biological channel: ``100\% liquidation'' is a statement about the mined material's relationship to the geological reservoir's own (zero) regenerative capacity, not a statement about what the mined material is physically used for. Smelting, refining, or other processing routinely turns mined ore, oil, or phosphate rock into durable infrastructure, plastics, or fertiliser that persists in the anthropogenic product stock $\mathbf P_i$ for years to decades — forcing $\bar{\mathbf C}^A_i=\mathbf 0$ to also mean ``0\% enters $\mathbf P_i$'' would leave the model structurally incapable of representing any durable stockpile built from mined material, which is physically absurd. We therefore route $\mathbf C^A_i$ physically using the same immediate-waste fraction $\boldsymbol\alpha_i$ already used for $\mathbf C_i$ (Eq.~\eqref{eq:product}), independent of its liquidation status:
\[
\frac{d\mathbf P_i}{dt}\ \text{gains an additional term}\quad +(1-\boldsymbol\alpha_i)\odot\mathbf C^A_i(t),
\]
added to Eq.~\eqref{eq:product}, with the complementary fraction routed to waste,
\[
\frac{d\mathbf W_j}{dt}\ \text{gains an additional term}\quad +\sum_i\boldsymbol\varepsilon_{i,j}\bigl(\boldsymbol\alpha_i\odot\mathbf C^A_i(t)\bigr),
\]
added to Eq.~\eqref{eq:wvec-corrected}, so that $\mathbf C^A_i$ leaves $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$ (Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic-geo}) and reappears, in full, split only by $\boldsymbol\alpha_i$, across $\mathbf P_i$ and $\mathbf W_j$ — mirroring exactly how $\mathbf C_i$ is routed, and preserving $dM_{\text{tot}}/dt=0$ by the same argument as Eq.~\eqref{eq:invariant}. The diagnostic quantities $\bar{\mathbf C}^A_i=\mathbf 0,\boldsymbol\Lambda^A_i=\mathbf C^A_i$ remain exactly as stated above and continue to drive the depletion diagnostics of Section~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}; they do not, however, act as physical routing valves for $\mathbf P_i$ versus $\mathbf W_j$. Setting $\mathbf C^A_i=\mathbf 0$ recovers the pure-biological-uptake model as a special case; for resources where extraction is understood to act on $\mathbf N_i$ alone (fisheries, forestry, most agricultural soil nutrients), $\mathbf C^A_i=\mathbf 0$ remains the appropriate choice, but for resources where extraction acts predominantly or entirely on $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$ directly (fossil aquifers, phosphate rock, oil and gas), $\mathbf C^A_i$ is the flow that should drive the depletion diagnostic of Section~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}.

\paragraph{Which state components are mass-conserved, and which are not.} Before defining a mass invariant, we partition every component of every vector state into exactly one of two categories, since conflating them is a category error the rest of this subsection is built to avoid. A \textbf{mass-conserved physical stock} is a component measured in an actual, singular physical unit (mass, volume, or a mole-equivalent of one material) and governed by a stoichiometric conservation law: this includes every component of $\mathbf N_i$ that denotes a physical material (soil organic carbon, groundwater volume, elemental zinc), together with $\mathbf P_i,\mathbf W_j,\mathbf I_j,\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i,\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i,\mathbf U_i$ in their entirety. A \textbf{functional state} is dynamically meaningful but carries no conservation law and must never be summed into a mass invariant: this includes any component of $\mathbf N_i$ that is a dimensionless abundance index or a non-material functional proxy (pollinator abundance, a biodiversity index), together with institutional/economic states such as $Z,E,K,L,A_{\text{TFP}},T,\mathbf p_i,Q$. The mass invariant below accordingly sums only mass-conserved components, and only \textbf{within one resource system $i$'s own consistent physical unit} at a time: summing a fishery's tonnes of biomass against a zinc mine's tonnes of ore, for instance, is not a meaningful physical mass sum without an explicit common-mass-equivalent conversion, which this manuscript does not provide and does not claim. A single global mass invariant $M_{\text{tot}}$ spanning every resource system and every component indiscriminately is accordingly not asserted; the well-posed conservation statement below applies to any one resource system's own physical-mass compartments. In the dynamical equations below, the mass-routing terms --- extraction $q\mathbf E_i\odot\mathbf N_i$, recycling $\boldsymbol\nu_i\odot\mathbf P_i$, waste damage $\boldsymbol\mu_{i,j}\mathbf W_j$, and the product/waste splits of Section~\ref{sec:waste-loop} --- are physical-mass fluxes and act only on the mass-conserved (material) components of $\mathbf N_i$; functional-state components (dimensionless indices) enter none of these routing terms and follow their own non-conserved evolution, outside the closed-form scope of this manuscript's core.

For the total mass invariant to hold, the conversion tensors $\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\text{rec}}_{i,j}$ (waste-to-active-abiotic-pool recovery, Section~\ref{sec:tech-recovery}), $\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\text{decay}}_{i,j}$ (product-decay-to-waste routing, Eq.~\eqref{eq:wvec-corrected}), and $\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\text{damage}}_{j,i}$ (damaged stock-to-waste conversion) must each satisfy their own unit-sum constraint, summed over the index appropriate to that flow's own direction:
\begin{align}
\sum_i \boldsymbol{\chi}^{\text{rec}}_{i,j} &= \mathbf{I}_{m_j}, \label{eq:chi_constraint} \\
\sum_j \boldsymbol{\chi}^{\text{decay}}_{i,j} &= \mathbf{I}_{m_i}, \tag{\ref{eq:chidecay_constraint}}\\
\sum_j \boldsymbol{\chi}^{\text{damage}}_{j,i} &= \mathbf{I}_{m_i}, \label{eq:chidamage_constraint}
\end{align}
where $\mathbf{I}_{m_j}$ and $\mathbf{I}_{m_i}$ are identity matrices of appropriate dimensions. In the common scalar or diagonal case, these reduce to $\chi^{\text{rec}}=\chi^{\text{decay}}=\chi^{\text{damage}}=1$. \textbf{These three constraints sum over different indices precisely because $\chi^{\text{rec}}$ and $\chi^{\text{decay}}$ route physically opposite-direction flows}: a single shared tensor for both, constrained only by $\chi^{\text{rec}}$'s summation condition, would conserve mass for recovery but not for decay routing, so the two are kept notationally and dimensionally distinct throughout (Appendix~\ref{sec:symbol-table}). The same unit-sum requirement applies to the waste-generation tensor $\boldsymbol\varepsilon_{i,j}$ (Section~\ref{sec:waste-loop}, Eq.~\eqref{eq:epsilon_constraint}).

Table~\ref{tab:ledger} lists every flux entering or leaving every mass-conserved compartment of one resource system $i$ (scalar case shown for clarity; the vector case follows identically once the tensors above satisfy their stated constraints), with its physical direction and destination. Rather than arguing mass conservation flux-by-flux in prose, the invariant below is derived directly from this ledger: every outflow row is matched by exactly one inflow row elsewhere in the table, verified by direct symbolic substitution and summation (Appendix~\ref{sec:symbol-table}; the check is implemented as a re-runnable script in the accompanying code, Section~\ref{sec:data-code}).
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{Flux ledger for the mass-conserved compartments of one resource system $i$ (scalar case). Every row's outflow terms are matched by an inflow term of identical magnitude elsewhere in the table (final column).}
\label{tab:ledger}
\begin{tabular}{lp{4.3cm}l}
\toprule
\textbf{Compartment} & \textbf{Flux} & \textbf{Matches} \\
\midrule
$N$ & $+R$ (regeneration); $-C^{\text{effort}}$ (extraction); $-N\mu W$ (damage); $+\nu P$ (recycling) & internal; $\to P,W$; $\to W$; $\leftarrow P$ \\
\midrule
$P$ & $+(1-\alpha)C^{\text{effort}}$, $+(1-\alpha)C^A$; $-\lambda P$ (decay); $-\nu P$ (recycling) & $\leftarrow N,A^{\text{geo}}$; $\to W$; $\to N$ \\
\midrule
$W$ & $+\varepsilon\alpha C^{\text{effort}}$, $+\varepsilon\alpha C^A$; $+\chi^{\text{decay}}\lambda P$; $+\chi^{\text{damage}} N\mu W$; $-\beta W,-\zeta W$; $-\rho W$ & $\leftarrow N,A^{\text{geo}}$; $\leftarrow P$; $\leftarrow N$; $\to I$; $\to A^{\text{act}}$ \\
\midrule
$I$ & $+\beta W,\ +\zeta W$ & $\leftarrow W$ \\
\midrule
$A^{\text{act}}$ & $-B$ (gross uptake); $+\omega_A(A^{\text{act,eq}}-A^{\text{act}})$; $+\chi^{\text{rec}}\rho W$; $+\gamma_U U$ & internal; $\leftrightarrow A^{\text{geo}}$; $\leftarrow W$; $\leftarrow U$ \\
\midrule
$A^{\text{geo}}$ & $-\omega_A(A^{\text{act,eq}}-A^{\text{act}})$; $-C^A$ & $\leftrightarrow A^{\text{act}}$; $\to P,W$ \\
\midrule
$U$ & $+\kappa_A N\,A^{\text{act}}/(A^{\text{act}}+A_0)$ (turnover, donor-limited); $-\gamma_U U$ (decomposition) & $\leftarrow N$; $\to A^{\text{act}}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Define the total mass of the mass-conserved compartments of the closed system as:
\begin{equation}
M_{\text{tot}}(t) =
\sum_i \left( \mathbf{1}_{m_i}^\top \mathbf{N}_i^{\text{mass}}(t) + \mathbf{1}_{m_i}^\top \mathbf{P}_i(t) + \mathbf{1}_{m_i}^\top \mathbf{A}^{\text{act}}_i(t) + \mathbf{1}_{m_i}^\top \mathbf{A}^{\text{geo}}_i(t) + \mathbf{1}_{m_i}^\top \mathbf{U}_i(t) \right)
+ \sum_j \left( \mathbf{1}_{n_j}^\top \mathbf{W}_j(t) + \mathbf{1}_{n_j}^\top \mathbf{I}_j(t) \right), \label{eq:Mtot}
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf{1}_{m_i}$ and $\mathbf{1}_{n_j}$ are column vectors of ones, restricted to the mass-conserved subset of components (denoted $\mathbf N_i^{\text{mass}}$, per the partition above), and the sum over $i$ is understood componentwise within one resource system's own consistent unit rather than as a single scalar spanning heterogeneous materials.

Differentiating (\ref{eq:Mtot}) and substituting the ledger's fluxes (Table~\ref{tab:ledger}) and applying the constraints (\ref{eq:chi_constraint}), (\ref{eq:chidecay_constraint}), and (\ref{eq:chidamage_constraint}) gives:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{ \frac{dM_{\text{tot}}}{dt} = 0 } \quad \Longrightarrow \quad M_{\text{tot}}(t) = M_{\text{tot}}(0) \quad \forall t \ge 0. \label{eq:invariant}
\end{equation}
This is strict mass conservation for the mass-conserved compartments of one resource system, \emph{derived} from the ledger rather than asserted, and it continues to hold under the two-pool abiotic split and the detritus compartment: the geochemical exchange term $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\odot(\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i-\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i)$ leaves $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$ in Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic-geo} with a sign exactly opposite to its gain in $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ in Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic}; the mining flow $\mathbf C^A_i$ leaves $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$ and enters $\mathbf P_i$ and $\mathbf W_j$ (split by $\boldsymbol\alpha_i$, exactly as $\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}$ is split; Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}) with matching total sign — \emph{not} routed entirely to $\mathbf W_j$ via the diagnostic $\boldsymbol\Lambda^A_i$, which (as emphasised in Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}) is a reporting quantity only and does not itself appear in any mass-balance ODE; and the detritus flux $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf N_i$ leaves $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ (via $\mathbf B_i$) and enters $\mathbf U_i$ with matching sign in Eq.~\eqref{eq:detritus}, while $\mathbf U_i$'s own decomposition loss $\boldsymbol\gamma_{U,i}\odot\mathbf U_i$ exactly matches its gain back into $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$; all cancellations follow by direct symbolic substitution against Table~\ref{tab:ledger} and are additionally checked by \texttt{verify\_flux\_ledger.py}.

\paragraph{Remark on the frozen-$A$ approximation.}
\label{sec:frozen-A}
Two distinct approximations are at work here, and the two-pool split (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}) exists precisely to keep them from being conflated. First, the \textbf{geological reservoir} $\mathbf{A}^{\text{geo}}_i$ is typically orders of magnitude larger than the active stocks $\mathbf{N}_i$ on policy-relevant timescales; over 10--100 years it changes by a negligible relative amount even under sustained direct mining $\mathbf C^A_i$, which justifies $\mathbf{A}^{\text{geo}}_i(t) \approx \mathbf{A}^{\text{geo}}_i(0)$ and lets the geochemical exchange it participates in (Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic}) depend only on $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ and the (near-constant) equilibrium target $\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i$ it sets. Second, and separately, the \textbf{regeneration function} $R(N,A^{\text{act}}) = rN(1-N/K)\cdot A^{\text{act}}/(A^{\text{act}}+A_0)\to rN(1-N/K)$ recovers the logistic growth law in the limit $A^{\text{act}}/A_0\to\infty$. This limit only requires the \emph{active} pool $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ to be large relative to the saturation constant $A_0$, not relative to $\mathbf N_i$ or to $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$. Choosing $A^{\text{act}}_i(0)$ comparable in scale to $K$ (or to $N_i(0)$) while keeping $A_0\ll A^{\text{act}}_i(0)$ (e.g., $A_0\sim0.01K$) therefore satisfies the near-logistic frozen-ratio limit \emph{and} keeps $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ small enough to be dynamically responsive to recovery and geochemical exchange (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}) at the same time. A single-reservoir formulation cannot satisfy both conditions simultaneously, since it would require $\mathbf A_i\gg\mathbf N_i$ for the frozen-flux argument, driving $\partial R/\partial A_i\to0$ and making the reservoir dynamically inert to recovered mass. This construction does \emph{not} assume $\dot{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{act}}_i\approx0$ for the gross uptake term specifically under $A^{\text{act}}_i\gg A_0$ — the gross uptake $\mathbf B_i=\mathbf R_i+\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf N_i$ remains a finite, unfrozen flux draining $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$, which the (also finite, and, crucially, $N$-independent) geochemical exchange and recovery terms of Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic} exactly balance at quasi-equilibrium, giving $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ a finite, $N$-uniform relaxation dynamic (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}) rather than an assumed-constant value or a relaxation time that diverges as $N\to K$. The gross uptake $\mathbf B_i$ remains strictly positive at $N=K$ whenever $\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}>\mathbf 0$, exactly as required to represent the ecosystem's continued metabolic draw on the active pool even at a stable, mature stock (the ``productivity illusion,'' Section~\ref{sec:introduction}) — and, because the exchange term supplies an $N$-independent restoring force, the corresponding stress diagnostic $\Sigma^{\text{act}}_{i,c}$ (Section~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}) remains well-behaved and non-degenerate precisely in this mature-stock regime.

\paragraph{Calibration and unknowns.}
The initial geological reservoir $\mathbf{A}^{\text{geo}}_i(0)$ is not globally well-constrained for all components (e.g., bioavailable zinc in the crust). We treat it as a large parameter that the bifurcation analysis does not vary, provided it remains $\gg \max(\mathbf{N}_i,\mathbf{P}_i,\mathbf{W}_i)$, consistent with the frozen-$A$ approximation (Section~\ref{sec:frozen-A}). The initial active abiotic pool $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i(0)$, by contrast, should be sized comparably to $\mathbf N_i(0)$ (Section~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}) since it is the dynamically responsive compartment the frozen-$A$ approximation does \emph{not} apply to. The initial inert reservoir $\mathbf{I}_j(0)$ is set to zero without loss of generality, since only its changes affect the mass balance. The recovery and damage tensors are assumed known from stoichiometric data; their empirical calibration for every service component is left to future work.

\subsection{Service-to-Stock Mapping}
\label{sec:service-to-stock}
One or more stock components produce a service component \(S_{i,c}\). We define a \textbf{stock-service
conversion matrix} \(\mathbf{M}_i\) (dimensions \(k_i \times m_i\)) with entries
\[
M_{i,c,\kappa} \ge 0, \qquad [M_{i,c,\kappa}] = \frac{[\text{stock component } \kappa]}{[\text{service component } c]},
\]
specifying the mass (or amount) of stock component \(\kappa\) consumed per unit of service component \(c\) produced. This matrix defines a \textbf{service-space accounting identity} for extraction, used in the rational-optimisation regime ($\chi=0$, variant (ii) of Section~\ref{sec:variant-registry}):
\[
\mathbf{C}_i^{\text{demand}}(t) = \mathbf{M}_i^\top \mathbf{D}_i(t), \qquad
C_{i,\kappa}^{\text{demand}}(t) = \sum_{c=1}^{k_i} M_{i,c,\kappa} \, D_{i,c}(t).
\]
Because \(M_{i,c,\kappa}\) carries units \([\text{stock}_\kappa]/[\text{service}_c]\) and \(D_{i,c}(t)\) is a flow of service \(c\) (units \([\text{service}_c]/\text{time}\)), the product has units \([\text{stock}_\kappa]/\text{time}\), making each component of \(\mathbf{C}_i^{\text{demand}}\) dimensionally homogeneous with the corresponding stock component in the differential equation \(d\mathbf{N}_i/dt\). No addition of incommensurable quantities occurs: the sum over \(c\) for a fixed \(\kappa\) is a sum of terms all carrying the same units \([\text{stock}_\kappa]/\text{time}\). The vector space structure is thus legitimate: the vector \(\mathbf{N}_i\) is a tuple of distinct physical quantities, and linear operations such as \(\mathbf{M}_i^\top \mathbf{D}_i\) act componentwise with units carried by the matrix entries. In many cases, \(m_i = k_i\) and \(\mathbf{M}_i\) is diagonal (one-to-one stock-service correspondence), but the framework allows for coupled production.

\paragraph{This is not the same object as the effort-driven extraction law used in the institutional-failure regime.} Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected} introduces a second, operative extraction law, $\mathbf{C}_i^{\text{effort}}(t)=q\,\mathbf{E}_i(t)\odot\mathbf{N}_i(t)$, used throughout the institutional-failure regime ($\chi=1$) that drives every dynamical result in this manuscript (Sections~\ref{sec:core-model}--\ref{sec:four-state-core}). Because effort $\mathbf E_i$ already acts directly on the stock vector $\mathbf N_i$ componentwise, $\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}$ is already a stock-space object and \textbf{must not} be wrapped in $\mathbf M_i^\top$: doing so would be a dimension mismatch whenever $m_i\neq k_i$, since $\mathbf M_i^\top$ is $m_i\times k_i$ and expects a $k_i$-dimensional (service-space) argument, not the $m_i$-dimensional (stock-space) vector $q\mathbf E_i\odot\mathbf N_i$ actually is. The two extraction laws, $\mathbf C_i^{\text{demand}}$ and $\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}$, are accordingly kept notationally distinct throughout this manuscript (Appendix~\ref{sec:symbol-table}): the former is retained only for the deficit-diagnostic vocabulary and the rational, $\chi=0$ narrative; the latter is the sole extraction law appearing in $\dot{\mathbf N}_i$ itself (Eq.~\eqref{eq:nvec-corrected}) and in every quantitative result this manuscript reports.

\subsection{Deficit Definitions}
\label{sec:deficit-definitions}
We distinguish two deficit vectors, both \textbf{diagnostic quantities computed from the physical state}, not dynamical drivers of the physical equations.

\textbf{Physical stock deficit} (diagnostic, governing the sustainability assessment):
\[
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{i}^{\text{phys}}(t) = \mathbf{C}_i(t) - \mathbf{M}_i^\top \mathbf{S}_i(t),
\]
where $\mathbf C_i(t)$ denotes whichever extraction law is operative for the regime under diagnosis (Section~\ref{sec:variant-registry}): $\mathbf C_i^{\text{demand}}$ under $\chi=0$, or $\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}$ under $\chi=1$; we write the bare $\mathbf C_i$ here and throughout the remaining diagnostic and mass-balance equations of this section specifically because this deficit and the routing/mass-balance machinery built on it (Sections~\ref{sec:waste-loop}--\ref{sec:tech-recovery}) are regime-agnostic bookkeeping, applying identically to whichever extraction flow is dynamically active.
For the reduced core in Section~\ref{sec:core-model}, we define a smoothed deficit for use in the economic feedback. To avoid the singular dependence on the smoothing parameter \(k\) and to ensure a well-defined positive baseline effort at equilibrium, we introduce a \textbf{shifted softplus} with an additive baseline \(\delta > 0\):
\[
\Delta(t) = \frac{1}{k}\ln\!\bigl(1 + e^{k(qE(t)N(t) - S(N(t)))}\bigr) - \frac{\ln 2}{k} + \delta,
\]
where \(\delta\) is a constant baseline deficit (with the same units as the deficit, i.e., stock per time) that represents the minimum effort required to maintain the extraction system even when the physical deficit is exactly zero (e.g., maintenance, operational friction). This formulation ensures that:

- At the sustainable equilibrium (\(qEN = S(N)\)), the argument of the softplus is zero, the shifted softplus vanishes, and \(\Delta^* = \delta\), \textbf{independent of \(k\)} — this is the property the shift is specifically designed to guarantee, and it is used directly in the core's leaky-integrator memory equation (Section~\ref{sec:generalized-effort}) so that the closed-form equilibrium $Z^*=\delta$ of Section~\ref{sec:equilibrium-core} inherits the same $k$-independence.

- As \(k \to \infty\), the softplus converges to the positive part, and the equilibrium remains finite and physically meaningful.

- The resulting equilibrium effort \(E^*\) itself is \emph{not} a simple linear function of \(\delta\) once the baseline-panic term of the full effort dynamics (Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core}) is retained in full; it instead solves the quadratic of Section~\ref{sec:equilibrium-core} (Eq.~\eqref{eq:Estar-quadratic}), which correctly reduces to a $k$-independent, but generally irrational, function of $\delta$ and the other effort parameters.

\paragraph{The price weight must saturate, since the price itself does not.} Market prices are defined in Section~\ref{sec:layer4} to diverge as any service component becomes scarce, $p_{i,c}(t)=p_{i,c,0}+\pi_{i,c}/(S_{i,c}(t)/S_{i,c}^{\text{ref}})\to\infty$ as $S_{i,c}\to0$; this divergence is deliberate, since it is what lets price act as an early, strong scarcity signal (Section~\ref{sec:layer4}). Using the raw price $p_{i,c}(t)$ as a linear multiplicative weight on the economic deficit would let that same divergence propagate, unsaturated, into every equation that consumes $\Delta_i^{\text{econ}}$ (Eqs.~\eqref{eq:k-corrected}--\eqref{eq:t-corrected}): because the shifted softplus itself does \emph{not} vanish as $S_{i,c}\to0$ (it approaches the positive floor $\delta$, by the same construction that keeps $Z^*$ well-defined above), the product $p_{i,c}\cdot\text{softplus}(\cdot)$ would grow without bound as any single service component becomes scarce, driving unbounded loss rates in the capital, TFP, and technology equations from a single scarce component alone. We therefore weight the deficit by a \textbf{saturating} transform of price rather than price itself,
\[
\tilde p_{i,c}(t) = \frac{p_{i,c}(t)}{p_{i,c}(t) + p_{i,c,0}} \in [0,1),
\]
using the baseline price $p_{i,c,0}$ already defined in the price equation above as the natural saturation scale, so no new parameter is introduced. This transform still increases monotonically toward $1$ as $p_{i,c}\to\infty$ (preserving price as a strong, correctly-ordered scarcity signal) but remains bounded for any finite or infinite price, so that the economic deficit, written out in full with the same \textbf{shifted, floored softplus} convention used throughout this manuscript (baseline $\delta$, Section~\ref{sec:generalized-effort}),
\[
\Delta_{i,c}^{\text{econ}}(t) = \tilde p_{i,c}(t)\,\max\Bigl(0,\ \text{softplus}_k\bigl(D_{i,c}(t) - S_{\text{meas},i,c}(t)\bigr) - \frac{\ln 2}{k} + \delta\Bigr),
\]
removes specifically the divergence contributed by the \emph{price factor}: $\tilde p_{i,c}\in[0,1)$ for any $p_{i,c}\ge0$, whereas the unweighted $p_{i,c}$ itself has no such bound. This is a relabelling of the same scarcity signal, not a new mechanism: $\tilde p_{i,c}$ is a monotonic, order-preserving rescaling of $p_{i,c}$ onto $[0,1)$. Bounding the price factor does not, by itself, bound the softplus factor: the shifted, floored softplus of $D_{i,c}-S_{\text{meas},i,c}$ can still grow without limit if the deficit $D_{i,c}-S_{\text{meas},i,c}$ itself grows without limit as $S_{i,c}\to0$. Whether this occurs depends on how demand responds to scarcity. Under the Layer 4 specification of Section~\ref{sec:layer4}, demand $D_{i,c}$ is derived from CES utility maximisation subject to a fixed budget constraint, so $D_{i,c}\to0$ at least as fast as $p_{i,c}^{-1}\to0$ as $p_{i,c}\to\infty$; the deficit $D_{i,c}-S_{\text{meas},i,c}$ then remains bounded (indeed shrinks toward zero) as $S_{i,c}\to0$, and $\Delta_{i,c}^{\text{econ}}$ is bounded as a consequence of the budget constraint together with the price-factor fix, not from the price-factor fix alone. A demand specification without this budget-driven self-limiting property (e.g., a fixed, price-inelastic demand level) would still permit $\Delta_{i,c}^{\text{econ}}$ to grow via the softplus factor even with $\tilde p_{i,c}$ bounded; guaranteeing boundedness under such a specification would require applying the same saturating transform to the softplus argument itself, which we do not do here since it is not required under the demand specification this manuscript actually uses.

\paragraph{Scope of the scalar reduction.}
The three-state core model analysed in Sections~\ref{sec:core-model}--\ref{sec:numerical-continuation-corrected} is not a rigorous projection of the full vector deficit \(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{i}^{\text{phys}}\). It is a phenomenological reduction to a single resource component assumed to dominate the dynamics (e.g., a fishery, an aquifer, a soil nutrient). The scalar deficit \(\Delta^{\text{eff}}\) used in the core is a smoothed version of \(qEN - S(N)\) (Eq.~\eqref{eq:deficit-identity}) for that dominant component, with the baseline \(\delta\) representing institutional overhead. The full vector framework provides the diagnostic architecture; the scalar core provides the dynamical proof of principle. Future work should derive a rigorous reduction from the full vector deficit space to the scalar core---via center-manifold or projection methods.

\paragraph{The specific gap this leaves in the core's own regeneration term.} One consequence of this scope limitation is precise enough to state as a concrete target for that future reduction, rather than only as a general caveat. The core's stock equation (Eq.~\eqref{eq:stock-core}) uses the frozen-$A$ logistic law $S(N)=rN(1-N/K)$, which is derived (Section~\ref{sec:frozen-A}) as the leading-order limit of $R(N,A^{\text{act}})=rN(1-N/K)\cdot A^{\text{act}}/(A^{\text{act}}+A_0)$ as $A^{\text{act}}/A_0\to\infty$ --- i.e., it holds only when the active abiotic pool is not itself a binding constraint. The vector framework's own Abiotic Depletion Horizons and Depletion Stress Indices (Section~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}) are built specifically to diagnose the opposite regime: a mature stock ($N\approx K$) silently drawing down $A^{\text{act}}_i$ through the gross uptake $\mathbf B_i=\mathbf R_i+\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i}\odot\mathbf N_i$ (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}) even while $N$ itself remains stable. Because $S(N)$ in the core has no dependence on $A^{\text{act}}$, this diagnosed depletion has no dynamical channel back into $\dot N$: the core cannot itself generate the collapse its own diagnostic layer is designed to warn about, and the two layers currently operate in parallel rather than as a single coupled system. Closing this gap --- e.g., promoting $A^{\text{act}}$ to a slow state variable inside the core and replacing $S(N)$ with an explicit consumer-resource or nutrient-limited growth law (Droop-type kinetics \cite{droop1973} or a Rosenzweig--MacArthur-style formulation \cite{rosenzweig1963} are natural candidates) so that a depleted $A^{\text{act}}$ dynamically suppresses regeneration --- is not a parameter adjustment to the present core: it necessarily abandons the frozen-$A$ limit that the current closed-form equilibrium, characteristic equation, and Hopf/SNPO analysis (Sections~\ref{sec:equilibrium-core}--\ref{sec:numerical-continuation-corrected}) all depend on, and would require re-deriving that entire analysis for the resulting higher-dimensional system rather than extending it. We flag this explicitly as the single most direct route to a productivity illusion that is dynamically endogenous to the core rather than diagnosed alongside it, and as a separate undertaking from the present manuscript rather than an incremental fix to it. In the terms of Section~\ref{sec:productivity-illusion-narrative}, this is precisely the yield-inflation sense of the illusion made dynamical: with the supporting pool $A^{\text{act}}$ retained as a state, measured throughput can exceed the true sustainable yield while the pool is being drawn down, and the Abiotic Depletion Horizons and Depletion Stress Indices (Section~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}) are the side-on diagnostics that detect this divergence before it becomes visible in the aggregate.

\subsection{Waste as the Dispersed End of the Material Loop}
\label{sec:waste-loop}
Extraction of services reconfigures matter. The \textbf{waste-generation tensor} \(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{i,j}\) maps a fraction of the extraction vector \(\mathbf{C}_i\) to immediate waste. The remaining fraction enters the \textbf{economic product stock} \(\mathbf{P}_i\), which decays back to waste with a characteristic timescale \(\tau_{P,i}\).
For a single extraction flow split across product and waste compartments to conserve mass, the routing tensor must satisfy the same unit-sum requirement imposed on the $\boldsymbol\chi$ tensors (Eqs.~\eqref{eq:chi_constraint}--\eqref{eq:chidamage_constraint}):
\begin{equation}
\sum_j \boldsymbol\varepsilon_{i,j} = \mathbf I_{m_i}, \label{eq:epsilon_constraint}
\end{equation}
so that every unit of extracted material reappears exactly once, split only by $\boldsymbol\alpha_i$, across $\mathbf P_i$ and the $\mathbf W_j$ (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}).

\subsection{Technological Recovery and Loop Closure}
\label{sec:tech-recovery}
A \textbf{recovery rate tensor} \(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{i,j}(T)\), with dimensions \([\text{time}]^{-1}\), returns matter recovered from waste to the material loop. A natural-looking but physically inaccurate routing would add this recovered mass directly to the natural capital stock $\mathbf N_i$, via $\boldsymbol\chi^{\text{rec}}_{i,j}(\boldsymbol\rho_{i,j}(T)\odot\mathbf W_j)$ in the $\dot{\mathbf N}_i$ equation. This is a thermodynamic short-circuit: real recovery processes (composting, mineralisation, industrial carbon capture, water treatment) return dispersed matter to the \emph{abiotic} pool — soil minerals, atmospheric or dissolved carbon, groundwater — not directly to living or standing biomass; the biosphere then takes up that recovered matter through the same regeneration process $\mathbf R_i(\mathbf N_i,\mathbf W)$ it uses for any other abiotic input (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}), rather than incorporating waste-derived matter as stock with no biological uptake step. Routing recovery straight to $\mathbf N_i$ would let the model synthesise standing biomass directly from dispersed waste (e.g., landfill plastics or oceanic nitrate becoming forest biomass with no intervening abiotic or biological process), which is not how recovery works physically, and it would bypass exactly the abiotic bottleneck this framework's closure (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}) exists to track.

We therefore route recovered mass to the \emph{active} abiotic pool $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ specifically, not to the geological reservoir $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$: real recovery processes return matter to fast-cycling, bioavailable compartments (topsoil, the mixed ocean layer, shallow groundwater), not to deep geological storage, and — as discussed in Section~\ref{sec:abiotic} — only a compartment that is dynamically responsive (not effectively infinite) lets recovered mass have any measurable effect on regeneration. The active-pool equation, Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic}, incorporates this routing via the term $\sum_j\boldsymbol\chi^{\text{rec}}_{i,j}(\boldsymbol\rho_{i,j}(T)\odot\mathbf W_j)$; the recovered mass then re-enters the active biological loop only indirectly, through its effect on $\mathbf R_i(\mathbf N_i,\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i,\mathbf W)$ via the pool it augments, exactly mirroring how a genuine increase in bioavailable nutrients would raise regeneration. This, together with the product stock decay, the direct-recycling term $\boldsymbol\nu_i\odot\mathbf P_i$ in Eq.~\eqref{eq:nvec-corrected} (durable products disassembled and returned to stock without first dispersing to waste, e.g., direct reuse), and the geochemical exchange $\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i}\odot(\mathbf A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_i-\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i)$ of Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic}, closes the material loop with a dynamically-responsive recovery pathway: the model accounts for all extracted mass either in immediate waste, in the product stock, in the active abiotic pool via recovery, or eventually in waste via decay, and recovered mass has a real, non-negligible effect on future regeneration rather than being diluted into an effectively-infinite sink. The rate mismatch between linear extraction and circular reprocessing is the root cause of both ``depletion'' and ``pollution''; they are the same molecules, observed at opposite ends of the broken pipeline, passing back through a finite, responsive abiotic pool rather than either skipping it entirely or vanishing into an infinite reservoir.

\section{Structural Production Function: Smooth Vector Liebig's Law}
\label{sec:structural-production}
A vector-valued function produces the service vector \(\mathbf{S}_i\):
\[
\mathbf{S}_i = \mathbf{F}_i(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{W}),
\]
where \(\mathbf{N}\) is the collection of all stock vectors and \(\mathbf{W}\) that of all waste vectors (regulatory stocks and waste can affect service provision non-locally).

For each service component \(c\), we employ a smooth minimum (Liebig) formulation over the stocks and regulatory subsystems that service requires. Let \(\mathcal{M}_{i,c}\) index the critical subsystems (e.g., soil microbiome, climate stability, pollinator network) that affect service \(c\) of resource \(i\), and let \(Y_{m,i,c}(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{W})\) denote the sub-yield subsystem \(m\) contributes. To ensure the service yield never exceeds its physical maximum \(S_{i,c}^{\max}\), each sub-yield uses a \(C^\infty\) smooth saturation function. A convenient form is the exponential saturation or the hyperbolic tangent:
\[
\tilde{Y}_{m,i,c} = S_{i,c}^{\max} \left( 1 - \exp\left(-\frac{Y_{m,i,c}}{S_{i,c}^{\max}}\right) \right)
\quad \text{or} \quad
\tilde{Y}_{m,i,c} = S_{i,c}^{\max} \tanh\left(\frac{Y_{m,i,c}}{S_{i,c}^{\max}}\right).
\]
Both functions are strictly bounded above by \(S_{i,c}^{\max}\) for all positive \(Y_{m,i,c}\), are \(C^\infty\) differentiable everywhere, and preserve the physical interpretation of a saturating yield. The saturated sub-yields then determine the service yield:
\[
S_{i,c} = S_{i,c}^{\max}\,
\Bigl[-\frac{1}{\rho_{i,c}}\ln\Bigl(\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{i,c}} w_{m,i,c}
\exp\!\Bigl(-\rho_{i,c} \frac{\tilde{Y}_{m,i,c}(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{W})}{S_{i,c}^{\max}}\Bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr],
\]
with \(\sum_m w_{m,i,c} = 1\). The parameter \(\rho_{i,c}\) controls the sharpness; as \(\rho_{i,c}\to\infty\), the expression approaches the strict minimum (Liebig's law). For finite \(\rho_{i,c}\), the expression smoothly approximates the minimum while retaining differentiability. Sub-yields are taken nonnegative throughout, and the saturation and soft-minimum claims apply on this domain (a negative sub-yield would correspond to a subsystem actively degrading rather than providing the service, a regime outside the present scope). Because the saturated sub-yields lie in \([0,S_{i,c}^{\max})\) and the weighted soft-minimum of the normalised ratios \(\tilde Y_{m,i,c}/S_{i,c}^{\max}\) lies in \([0,1)\), the resulting service yield is capped at its physical maximum \(S_{i,c}^{\max}\), as required. The approximation error relative to the strict minimum is bounded: with normalised weights (\(\sum_m w_{m,i,c}=1\)), the smooth minimum exceeds the strict minimum \(\min_m \tilde Y_{m,i,c}/S_{i,c}^{\max}\) by at most \((1/\rho_{i,c})\,\ln(1/w_{m^*,i,c})\), where \(m^*\) indexes the limiting (minimum) subsystem; equivalently, the bias in \(S_{i,c}\) is at most \(S_{i,c}^{\max}(1/\rho_{i,c})\ln(1/w_{m^*,i,c})\), vanishing as \(\rho_{i,c}\to\infty\) and growing only logarithmically as the limiting subsystem's weight shrinks.

\(\rho_{i,c}\) is a regularisation constant, not a physical rate: it has no empirical referent and is not intended to be calibrated against data, in the same sense and for the same reason as the softplus sharpness $k$ of the reduced core (Section~\ref{sec:parameter-audit}). Its role is purely to keep the weakest-link aggregation differentiable; $\rho_{i,c}\to\infty$ recovers the literal, textbook Liebig's Law of the Minimum, and any $\rho_{i,c}$ large enough to sharply penalise the weakest input is qualitatively equivalent for the purposes of this framework. $\rho_{i,c}$ does not enter the reduced three-state core (Sections~\ref{sec:core-model}--\ref{sec:numerical-continuation-corrected}), so none of the paper's quantitative results (equilibria, Hopf crossings, fold thresholds) depend on its value.

The sub-yields can be:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Renewable provision:} \(Y_{\mathcal{R},i,c} = R_{i,c}(\mathbf{N}_i)\), where \(R_{i,c}\) is a component of the regeneration vector. For renewable stocks, \(R_{i,c} = r_{i,c} N_{i,c} (1 - N_{i,c}/K_{i,c})\).
      For critical stocks with Allee effects \cite{courchamp1999,kramer2009}, \(R_{i,c} = r_{i,c} N_{i,c} (1 - N_{i,c}/K_{i,c})
      (N_{i,c}/N_{\mathrm{Allee},i,c} - 1)\); viability requires \(N_{\mathrm{Allee},i,c} < K_{i,c}\).
\item \textbf{Critical regulation:}
      \[
      Y_{\mathcal{C},i,c} = S_{i,c}^{\max}\,
      \Bigl[1 + \exp\!\Bigl(-\sum_{\ell} \kappa_{\ell,i,c} \frac{N_{\ell} - N_{\mathrm{crit},\ell,i,c}}
      {N_{\mathrm{crit},\ell,i,c}}\Bigr)\Bigr]^{-1}
      \exp\!\Bigl(-\sum_{j} \lambda_{j,i,c} \frac{W_j}{W_{j,\mathrm{ref}}}\Bigr),
      \]
      where \(N_{\ell}\) are critical regulatory stocks (climate, biodiversity) and \(W_j\) are waste stocks.
\end{itemize}
This structure makes every service component dependent on the weakest link among its required inputs, up to the smoothing controlled by \(\rho_{i,c}\).

\section{Deficit-Driven Dynamics: The Four-Layer Architecture}
\label{sec:deficit-dynamics}
Four strictly separated layers organise the dynamics. This structure ensures that normative conditions never violate physical conservation laws.

\subsection{Layer 1: Physical State Equations}
\subsubsection{Natural Capital Vector Dynamics}
\label{sec:natcap-corrected}
The stock vector \(\mathbf{N}_i\) evolves according to \textbf{mass conservation}. A natural but ultimately inadequate way to represent capital liquidation would split extraction into two separately-computed terms: a barrier-limited ``sustainable'' flow $\bar{\mathbf{C}}_i=\min(\mathbf{H}_{\max,i},\mathbf{C}_i)$ and a liquidation top-up $\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_i=\max(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{C}_i-\mathbf{H}_{\max,i})$ subtracted separately from $\dot{\mathbf N}_i$. \textbf{That formulation is physically inconsistent}: because $\boldsymbol\Lambda_i$ is not itself modulated by the barrier function $\boldsymbol\phi_i$, it does not vanish as $\mathbf N_i\to\mathbf 0$. Whenever gross demand exceeds the maximum feasible extraction capacity for a sustained period — precisely the collapse regime the model is meant to describe — $\boldsymbol\Lambda_i$ continues to withdraw at the full (undamped) demand rate even as the stock approaches zero, so $\dot{\mathbf N}_i\to-\mathbf C_i<\mathbf 0$ at the boundary. Numerically integrating this two-term formulation with fixed demand only marginally above the maximum sustainable yield drives $N$ through zero in finite time and diverges to $-\infty$, violating non-negativity (Section~\ref{sec:nonneg-corrected}). We therefore adopt a single-flow formulation instead (below), which avoids this failure mode by construction.

We repair this by making extraction effort itself the single dynamical driver of the physical extraction flow, rather than introducing extraction capacity and a separate liquidation channel as two disconnected constructs. Let $\mathbf E_i(t)$ be a vector of dynamical extraction-effort variables (promoted here from the reduction in Section~\ref{sec:generalized-effort}, rather than introduced only there), governed by its own bounded, saturating dynamics with $\mathbf E_i(t)\in[\mathbf 0,\mathbf E_{\max,i}]$, with one component of $\mathbf E_i$ per component of $\mathbf N_i$ (dimension $m_i$, not $k_i$), since effort acts on standing stock directly rather than on service output. The gross physical extraction is then
\[
\mathbf{C}_i^{\text{effort}}(t) = q\,\mathbf{E}_i(t)\odot\mathbf{N}_i(t),
\]
and this is the \emph{only} extraction term entering the stock equation. \textbf{No $\mathbf M_i^\top$ conversion is applied here, for any $m_i,k_i$}: unlike $\mathbf C_i^{\text{demand}}$ (Section~\ref{sec:deficit-definitions}), which converts a service-space demand vector into stock-space via the $k_i\times m_i$ matrix $\mathbf M_i$, effort $\mathbf E_i$ is already indexed by stock component and acts directly on $\mathbf N_i$ componentwise; $q\mathbf E_i\odot\mathbf N_i$ is consequently already an $m_i$-dimensional, stock-space object, and applying $\mathbf M_i^\top$ (an $m_i\times k_i$ matrix) to it would be a dimension mismatch whenever $m_i\neq k_i$. Because $\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}(t)$ is already proportional to $\mathbf N_i(t)$, it vanishes automatically and smoothly as any stock component approaches zero, for \emph{any} trajectory of $\mathbf E_i(t)$ — no separate barrier function $\boldsymbol\phi_i$ needs to protect non-negativity, and the drawdown term below uses none.

The natural capital dynamics are:
\begin{align}
\frac{d\mathbf{N}_i}{dt} &= \mathbf{R}_i(\mathbf{N}_i,\mathbf{W})
   - \mathbf{C}_i^{\text{effort}}(t) \nonumber\\
   &\quad - \mathbf{N}_i(t) \odot \sum_j \boldsymbol{\mu}_{i,j} \mathbf{W}_j(t)
   + \boldsymbol{\nu}_i \odot \mathbf{P}_i(t),
\label{eq:nvec-corrected}
\end{align}
where \(\mathbf{R}_i\) is the regeneration vector, \(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i,j}\) is the waste damage rate tensor, and \(\boldsymbol{\nu}_i\) is the recycling rate from the product stock. Unlike a natural but ultimately incorrect routing, technologically recovered waste does \emph{not} enter $\mathbf N_i$ directly (see the paragraph on technological recovery below, Section~\ref{sec:tech-recovery}, where the term $\sum_j\boldsymbol\chi^{\text{rec}}_{i,j}(\boldsymbol\rho_{i,j}(T)\odot\mathbf W_j)$ instead enters the abiotic reservoir equation, Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic}).

\textbf{Capital liquidation as a diagnostic, not a separate dynamical term.} We preserve the qualitative phenomenon the original $\boldsymbol\Lambda_i$ term was meant to capture — extraction that exceeds the locally sustainable yield and therefore draws down principal rather than living off yield — but now compute it \emph{after the fact} as a bookkeeping split of the single flow $\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}(t)$ that already appears in Eq.~\eqref{eq:nvec-corrected}:
\[
\bar{\mathbf{C}}_i(t) = \min\bigl(\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}(t),\, \mathbf M_i^\top\mathbf S_i(t)\bigr), \qquad
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_i(t) = \max\bigl(\mathbf 0,\, \mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}(t) - \mathbf M_i^\top\mathbf S_i(t)\bigr),
\]
so that $\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}\equiv\bar{\mathbf C}_i+\boldsymbol\Lambda_i$ identically, by construction, rather than by two independently-barriered terms that can fail to reconstruct the physical flow they were supposed to decompose. $\boldsymbol\Lambda_i(t)$ serves purely as a diagnostic (Sections~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}, \ref{sec:discussion-liquidation}); it never appears as an independent second subtraction from $\dot{\mathbf N}_i$. Here $\mathbf M_i^\top\mathbf S_i(t)$ is a legitimate use of the stock-service matrix, since $\mathbf S_i$ is genuinely a service-space object requiring conversion to stock-space for comparison against $\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}$; this is unrelated to the now-removed $\mathbf M_i^\top$ wrapper around $\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}$ itself.

\paragraph{Diagnostic labels must not double as physical routing valves.} A natural-looking but incorrect next step would let this diagnostic split also decide where the extracted mass \emph{physically} goes — e.g., routing $\bar{\mathbf C}_i$ (the ``sustainable'' portion) toward the durable product stock $\mathbf P_i$ and $\boldsymbol\Lambda_i$ (the ``liquidated'' portion) entirely toward waste. This conflates a normative/diagnostic judgement (is this extraction within the locally sustainable yield?) with a constitutive physical fact (what fraction of any given tonne of extracted material ends up in durable goods versus immediate waste?); the two are causally independent, since liquidated principal is exactly as capable of being smelted into steel beams or refined into durable plastics as sustainably-harvested material is. Forcing $\boldsymbol\Lambda_i$ to route 100\% to waste would leave the model structurally incapable of representing a durable stockpile built from over-extracted material (e.g., steel infrastructure built during a period of unsustainable ore mining), even though such stockpiles evidently exist. We therefore route the \emph{physical} flow using only the (diagnostic-independent) immediate-waste fraction $\boldsymbol\alpha_i$, applied to the full extraction flow $\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}$ rather than to either half of its sustainability split, reserving $\bar{\mathbf C}_i,\boldsymbol\Lambda_i$ for diagnostic use only (Sections~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}, \ref{sec:discussion-liquidation}).

\subsubsection{Economic Product Stock Dynamics}
The product stock receives the fraction $(1-\boldsymbol\alpha_i)$ of \emph{all} extraction, regardless of its sustainability classification, since durability is a material/technological property of the extracted matter, not a property of whether that extraction happened to exceed the locally sustainable yield:
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\mathbf{P}_i}{dt} = (1 - \boldsymbol{\alpha}_i) \odot \mathbf{C}_i^{\text{effort}}(t) - \boldsymbol{\lambda}_i \odot \mathbf{P}_i(t) - \boldsymbol{\nu}_i \odot \mathbf{P}_i(t),
\label{eq:product}
\end{equation}
where \(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_i\) is the immediate waste fraction (componentwise, between 0 and 1), \(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_i\) is the natural decay rate of products (e.g., demolition, obsolescence), and \(\boldsymbol{\nu}_i\) is the direct recycling rate back to natural capital. The diagnostic split $\bar{\mathbf C}_i,\boldsymbol\Lambda_i$ of Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected} does \emph{not} appear here: it remains available for reporting what fraction of \emph{current} extraction constitutes liquidation (Sections~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}, \ref{sec:discussion-liquidation}), but does not gate which physical compartment the extracted mass enters.

\subsubsection{Waste Accumulation and the Inert Reservoir}
The waste equations receive mass from immediate extraction waste, product decay, and waste-damage conversion. Consistent with the routing principle above (physical destination is governed by the immediate-waste fraction $\boldsymbol\alpha_i$ applied to total extraction $\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}$, not by the diagnostic sustainability split):
\begin{align}
\frac{d\mathbf{W}_j}{dt} &= \sum_i \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{i,j} \bigl( \boldsymbol{\alpha}_i \odot \mathbf{C}_i^{\text{effort}}(t) \bigr)
   + \sum_i \boldsymbol{\chi}^{\text{decay}}_{i,j} \bigl( \boldsymbol{\lambda}_i \odot \mathbf{P}_i(t) \bigr) \nonumber\\
   &\quad + \sum_i \boldsymbol{\chi}^{\text{damage}}_{j,i} \bigl( \mathbf{N}_i(t) \odot \boldsymbol{\mu}_{i,j} \mathbf{W}_j(t) \bigr) \nonumber\\
   &\quad - \boldsymbol{\beta}_j(\mathbf{N}) \odot \mathbf{W}_j(t)
   - \boldsymbol{\zeta}_j T(t) \odot \mathbf{W}_j(t)
   - \sum_i \boldsymbol{\rho}_{i,j}(T) \odot \mathbf{W}_j(t),
\label{eq:wvec-corrected}
\end{align}
where \(\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\text{damage}}_{j,i}\) is a conversion tensor mapping the damaged natural capital into the waste compartment, and \(\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\text{decay}}_{i,j}\) is a \textbf{separate} conversion tensor mapping decayed product mass into the waste compartment, satisfying its own unit-sum constraint
\begin{equation}
\sum_j \boldsymbol{\chi}^{\text{decay}}_{i,j} = \mathbf{I}_{m_i}, \label{eq:chidecay_constraint}
\end{equation}
distinct from, and not to be confused with, the recovery tensor $\boldsymbol\chi^{\text{rec}}_{i,j}$ of Section~\ref{sec:tech-recovery} (Eq.~\eqref{eq:chi_constraint}), which routes an opposite-direction flow (waste to the active abiotic pool) and is subject to a different constraint, summed over the other index. Because the two constraints ($\sum_i\boldsymbol\chi^{\text{rec}}_{i,j}=\mathbf I_{m_j}$ and $\sum_j\boldsymbol\chi^{\text{decay}}_{i,j}=\mathbf I_{m_i}$) sum over different indices, a single shared tensor cannot in general satisfy both simultaneously, so the two are kept notationally and dimensionally distinct throughout (Appendix~\ref{sec:symbol-table}). This ensures that the mass the damage term \(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i,j}\) subtracts from \(\mathbf{N}_i\) exactly reappears in \(\mathbf{W}_j\), strictly closing the mass balance; together with Eq.~\eqref{eq:product}, this accounts for every unit of $\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}(t)$ exactly once, split only by $\boldsymbol\alpha_i$ between immediate waste and the product stock, independent of how much of that same flow the diagnostic split of Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected} separately classifies as liquidation.

The inert reservoir receives the irreversibly degraded fraction:
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\mathbf{I}_j}{dt} = \boldsymbol{\beta}_j(\mathbf{N}) \odot \mathbf{W}_j(t)
   + \boldsymbol{\zeta}_j T(t) \odot \mathbf{W}_j(t).
\label{eq:ivec}
\end{equation}
This ensures that all extracted mass is eventually accounted for: either in waste, in the product stock, or recycled back to natural capital, with the inert reservoir capturing the irreversible fraction. This distinguishes accumulating (stock) pollutants --- CO\(_2\), persistent plastics, bioaccumulated toxics --- whose damage persists on human timescales and is captured by the inert sink, from dissipating (flow) pollutants whose effects scale with emission rate rather than cumulative load; the former, not the latter, are the sink-side counterpart of the source-side depletion the ADH diagnostics track.

\subsubsection{Non-negativity and Mass Conservation}
\label{sec:nonneg-corrected}
Non-negativity of \(\mathbf{N}_i\) follows directly from Eq.~\eqref{eq:nvec-corrected}: the sole extraction term $\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}(t)=q\mathbf E_i(t)\odot\mathbf N_i(t)$ (Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected}; \textbf{no} $\mathbf M_i^\top$ conversion, since this is already a stock-space object) is proportional to $\mathbf N_i$ itself and $\mathbf E_i(t)$ is bounded (Section~\ref{sec:generalized-effort}), so $\mathbf C_i^{\text{effort}}\to\mathbf 0$ as $\mathbf N_i\to\mathbf 0$ for any admissible effort trajectory; no separate, unbarriered top-up term can drive a component through zero. This holds both analytically, via the local linearization at $\mathbf N_i=\mathbf 0$, and numerically, by direct integration under sustained excess demand: with the single-flow formulation the stock decays smoothly to zero and stays there, whereas the original two-term $\bar{\mathbf C}_i+\boldsymbol\Lambda_i$ formulation drives the stock through zero and diverges (Figure~\ref{fig:liquidation-fix}). The product stock \(\mathbf{P}_i\) stays non-negative by construction (source terms are non-negative). The mass changes across all compartments sum to exactly zero, including the damage conversion and the diagnostic liquidation split, ensuring strict mass conservation for all matter that enters the anthropogenic extraction-processing-waste-recycling loop. We model biological regeneration as a transfer from effectively infinite abiotic reservoirs (atmospheric carbon, soil mineral pools, deep water) on the policy-relevant timescale, and omit geological recycling from the inert reservoir because its characteristic time (\(>10^4\) yr) lies outside the model horizon. Thus, the framework achieves strict accounting of the mobilised material cycle, not strict closure of the planetary geochemical cycle.

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{liquidation_fix.png}
\caption{\textbf{Effect of the extraction-term fix on non-negativity.} \emph{Left:} the original two-term formulation ($\bar{\mathbf C}=\min(H_{\max},C)$ plus an unbarriered $\Lambda=\max(0,C-H_{\max})$) drives the stock through zero and diverges once demand exceeds the maximum sustainable yield. \emph{Right:} the corrected single-flow formulation ($C=qEN$, barrier-free by construction) decays smoothly to zero and never crosses it, for the same fixed demand.}
\label{fig:liquidation-fix}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Layer 2: Diagnostic Equations}
This layer computes the physical and economic deficits from the physical state. For the reduced core, the relevant physical deficit signal is the leaky-integrator memory of Section~\ref{sec:generalized-effort} (Appendix A), which replaces a finite-window cumulative integral for the reasons given there:
\[
\dot{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{i}^{\text{phys}} = \frac{1}{\tau_m}\Bigl[\,\max\Bigl(\mathbf 0,\ \text{softplus}_k\!\bigl( \mathbf{C}_i(s) - \mathbf{M}_i^\top \mathbf{S}_i(s) \bigr) - \frac{\ln 2}{k}\mathbf 1 + \boldsymbol\delta\Bigr) - \boldsymbol{\Delta}_i^{\text{phys}}\,\Bigr],
\]
with $\mathbf C_i$ the single, barrier-free extraction flow of Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected} (no separate $\bar{\mathbf C}_i$ term), consistent with the scalar leaky integrator of Eq.~\eqref{eq:leaky-integrator-A}. The economic deficit uses the saturated price transform $\tilde p_{i,c}(t)=p_{i,c}(t)/(p_{i,c}(t)+p_{i,c,0})\in[0,1)$ introduced in Section~\ref{sec:deficit-definitions}, not the raw price $p_{i,c}(t)$ itself: using the raw price here would reintroduce, into the very equations this layer feeds (Eqs.~\eqref{eq:k-corrected}--\eqref{eq:t-corrected}), exactly the unbounded-loss-rate failure mode $\tilde p_{i,c}$ is designed to prevent.
\[
\Delta_{i,c}^{\text{econ}}(t) = \tilde p_{i,c}(t)\,\max\Bigl(0,\ \text{softplus}_k\bigl(D_{i,c}(t) - S_{\text{meas},i,c}(t)\bigr) - \frac{\ln 2}{k} + \delta\Bigr),
\]
using the same shifted, floored softplus convention as $\boldsymbol\Delta_i^{\text{phys}}$ immediately above (Section~\ref{sec:deficit-definitions}). These quantities are \emph{not} state variables in the sense of being externally imposed; $\boldsymbol{\Delta}_i^{\text{phys}}$ is itself governed by the leaky-integrator ODE above, and both it and $\Delta_{i,c}^{\text{econ}}$ are diagnostic outputs used to drive Layer 3.

\subsection{Layer 3: Economic Response Equations}
\subsubsection{Economic Output (Service-Weighted Index)}
Define the dimensionless capital and labour inputs \(\hat{K} = K/K_{\text{ref}}\), \(\hat{L} = L/L_{\text{ref}}\) (with \(L_{\text{ref}} \equiv 1\)). A \textbf{service-weighted index} replaces the natural capital aggregate:
\[
\mathcal{S}_{\text{agg}}(t) = \Bigl( \sum_{i,c} \omega_{i,c}
\Bigl( \frac{S_{i,c}(t)}{S_{i,c}^{\text{ref}}} \Bigr)^{\gamma} \Bigr)^{1/\gamma},
\]
where the sum runs over all service components that enter economic production, and the weights \(\omega_{i,c}\) sum to unity. Total output is
\[
Q(t) = A(t)\, \hat{K}(t)^\alpha \, \hat{L}(t)^\beta \, \mathcal{S}_{\text{agg}}(t)^{\delta_S}, \quad
\alpha+\beta+\delta_S=1.
\]
Because \(\hat{K}\), \(\hat{L}\), and \(\mathcal{S}_{\text{agg}}\) are dimensionless, \(Q\) has the same units as \(A\) (monetary flow per year). The elasticity of substitution among services is \(\sigma_S = 1/(1-\gamma)\); this formulation allows the economy to respond to the full vector of services rather than to a mass-weighted scalar. \textbf{The exponent $\delta_S$ here is distinct from, and should not be confused with, the baseline-panic deficit parameter $\delta$} used throughout Sections~\ref{sec:deficit-definitions}--\ref{sec:generalized-effort} and the reduced cores (Sections~\ref{sec:core-model}--\ref{sec:four-state-core}); the two never co-occur in one equation and are kept notationally distinct throughout (Appendix~\ref{sec:symbol-table}).

$\gamma$ is held fixed over the model's analysis horizon, consistent with standard practice in CES-based growth and production modelling, where the elasticity of substitution is treated as a structural technology parameter rather than a state variable: it summarises how substitutable ecological services are for one another given the existing technological base, not how that substitutability might itself evolve as new technologies are adopted. $\gamma$ does not enter the reduced three-state core (Sections~\ref{sec:core-model}--\ref{sec:numerical-continuation-corrected}), so it plays no role in any of the paper's headline dynamical results; it governs only the auxiliary macroeconomic layer used to formalise the weak/strong sustainability distinction of Section~\ref{sec:weak-vs-strong}. Letting $\gamma$ evolve with technological reconfiguration $T(t)$ (e.g., substitutability increasing as $T$ accumulates) is a natural extension we leave to future work, since it would require its own empirical grounding rather than trading one fixed parameter for another. Read together with the outer elasticity $\rho$ of the macroeconomic layer (Section~\ref{sec:limitations-further-work}), $\gamma$ is best understood as a regime parameter rather than a philosophical constant: it indexes how readily substitution can occur within the service vector, and whether a particular system operates in the weak- or strong-sustainability regime is an empirical question about the rate at which substitution keeps pace with depletion (Section~\ref{sec:weak-vs-strong}).

\subsubsection{Capital Accumulation}
\begin{equation}
\frac{dK}{dt} = \varsigma Q(t) - \delta_K K(t) - \theta K(t) \sum_i w_i
                \frac{\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}_i^{\text{econ}}(t)\|}{\Delta_{i,\text{ref}}},
\label{eq:k-corrected}
\end{equation}
where \(\varsigma\) is the savings rate (distinct from the service index) and \(\|\cdot\|\) is a suitable norm (e.g., weighted Euclidean). All terms are monetary/yr.

\subsubsection{Labour/Population Dynamics}
\begin{equation}
\frac{dL}{dt} = r_L L(t) \Bigl( 1 - \frac{L(t)}{L_{\max}(Q(t))} \Bigr), \quad
                L_{\max}(Q) = \max\bigl(L_{\min},\, L_0 (Q/Q_0)^\eta\bigr).
\label{eq:l}
\end{equation}
The floor \(L_{\min} > 0\) prevents singularities when output collapses.

\subsubsection{Technological Productivity}
\begin{equation}
\frac{dA}{dt} = \eta_A A(t) \Bigl( 1 - \frac{A}{A_{\max}} \Bigr) - \nu A(t) \sum_i w_i
                \frac{\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}_i^{\text{econ}}(t)\|}{\Delta_{i,\text{ref}}}.
\label{eq:a-corrected}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Technological Reconfiguration}
\begin{equation}
\frac{dT}{dt} = \Bigl[ \eta_{T,0} + \eta_{T,1} \Bigl( \sum_i w_i
                \frac{\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}_i^{\text{econ}}(t)\|}{\Delta_{i,\text{ref}}} \Bigr) T(t) \Bigr]
                \Bigl( 1 - \frac{T(t)}{T_{\max}} \Bigr).
\label{eq:t-corrected}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Layer 4: Market Prices, Demand, and Adaptive Expectations}
\label{sec:layer4}
\subsubsection{Market Prices and Consumption Allocation}
Service-specific prices are
\[
p_{i,c}(t) = p_{i,c,0} + \frac{\pi_{i,c}}{S_{i,c}(t)/S_{i,c}^{\text{ref}}},
\]
so that prices diverge as any service component becomes scarce. Maximising a CES utility \cite{arrow1961} over normalised service flows subject to the budget constraint \(\sum_{i,c} p_{i,c} D_{i,c} = (1-\varsigma)Q\) yields demand \(\mathbf{D}_i\), giving component-specific demand functions that respond to relative service prices.

\subsubsection{Adaptive Expectations and History Function}
Perceived service yields \(\mathbf{S}_{\text{meas},i}\) follow
\[
\frac{d\mathbf{S}_{\text{meas},i}}{dt} = \frac{1}{\tau_E} \bigl( \mathbf{S}_i(t) - \mathbf{S}_{\text{meas},i}(t) \bigr).
\]
We construct the initial history by:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Solving the pre-industrial steady state of the ODE system (\(\tau_E = 0\));
\item Integrating forward from that steady state for 200 years;
\item Using the resulting trajectory as the history segment for the DDE solver.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Structural Flux Decomposition}
For any interval \([t_0,t]\), the componentwise mass balance derived from the physical layer (eqs.~\ref{eq:nvec-corrected}, \ref{eq:product}, and \ref{eq:wvec-corrected}) is strictly closed, including the waste-damage conversion and the capital-liquidation term. The cumulative fluxes can be decomposed to reveal the gross physical throughput of the system. For the natural capital stock:
\[
\mathbf{N}_i(t) - \mathbf{N}_i(t_0) = \boldsymbol{\Phi}_R - \boldsymbol{\Phi}_E - \boldsymbol{\Phi}_D + \boldsymbol{\Phi}_P,
\]
where the cumulative fluxes are:
\begin{align*}
\Phi_{R,\kappa} &= \int_{t_0}^t R_{i,\kappa}\,dt', & \Phi_{E,\kappa} &= \int_{t_0}^t C_{i,\kappa}\,dt', \\
\Phi_{D,\kappa} &= \int_{t_0}^t N_{i,\kappa} \sum_j \mu_{i,j,\kappa} W_{j,\kappa}\,dt', & \Phi_{P,\kappa} &= \int_{t_0}^t \nu_{i,\kappa} P_{i,\kappa}\,dt',
\end{align*}
where $\Phi_{E,\kappa}$ integrates the full extraction flow $C_{i,\kappa}$ (Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected}), not a diagnostically-gated sub-flow, consistent with the single-flow, non-negativity-preserving extraction law and with the routing principle of Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected} that the sustainability diagnostic $\bar C_{i,\kappa},\Lambda_{i,\kappa}$ never gates a physical mass-balance term. The technological recovery flux $\Phi_{\text{rec},\kappa}=\int_{t_0}^t\sum_j[\chi_{i,j}(\rho_{i,j}\odot W_j)]_\kappa\,dt'$ likewise does not appear in $\mathbf N_i$'s own balance: it enters the abiotic reservoir's balance instead (Section~\ref{sec:tech-recovery}, Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic}), and from there reaches $\mathbf N_i$ only indirectly, through its effect on $R_{i,\kappa}$ (already included in $\Phi_R$). Similarly, the product stock balance is \(\mathbf{P}_i(t) - \mathbf{P}_i(t_0) = \boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\text{in}, P} - \boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\text{decay}} - \boldsymbol{\Phi}_P\), with $\Phi_{\text{in},P,\kappa}=\int_{t_0}^t(1-\alpha_{i,\kappa})C_{i,\kappa}\,dt'$ likewise driven by the full extraction flow (Eq.~\eqref{eq:product}).

Define the componentwise gross sustainability ratio:
\[
\mathcal{S}_{i,\kappa} = \frac{\Phi_{R,\kappa} + \Phi_{P,\kappa}}{\Phi_{E,\kappa} + \Phi_{D,\kappa}}.
\]
Then \(\mathcal{S}_{i,\kappa} > 1\) if and only if stock component \(\kappa\) is accumulating over the interval. Unlike a scalar ratio, the vector \(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_i\) can show some components accumulating while others decline, revealing hidden structural overshoot even when aggregate biomass appears stable. Note that $\Phi_{R,\kappa}$ already reflects any technological recovery that has raised the abiotic pool $A_{i,\kappa}$ feeding $R_{i,\kappa}$ (Section~\ref{sec:tech-recovery}), so recovery's contribution to $\mathcal S_{i,\kappa}$ is correctly indirect and delayed, rather than an instantaneous addition to the stock balance.

\subsection{The Productivity Gap as a Vector Diagnostic}
\label{sec:productivity-gap}
Define the \textbf{vector productivity gap}
\[
\mathbf{G}_i(t) = \mathbf{S}_{\text{meas},i}(t) - \mathbf{S}_i(t).
\]
Differentiating and using the adaptive expectations equation gives
\[
\frac{d\mathbf{G}_i}{dt} = -\frac{1}{\tau_E} \mathbf{G}_i(t) - \frac{d\mathbf{S}_i}{dt}.
\]
When \(\tau_E\) is large and the true service vector is declining, the gap vector can remain positive in some components, sustaining the illusion of adequacy in those services while the underlying stocks erode.

This is the same productivity illusion introduced narratively in Section~\ref{sec:productivity-illusion-narrative} and proven unavoidable for any \emph{positively weighted linear} scalar aggregate in Proposition~1 (Section~\ref{sec:why-frameworks-fail}), now given a dynamical consequence: when $\mathbf G_i$ collapses in a critical component, the institutional delay $\tau$ of the reduced core (Sections~\ref{sec:core-model}--\ref{sec:numerical-continuation-corrected}) modulates the global basin structure, potentially pushing the system toward the large-amplitude cycle discussed in Section~\ref{sec:hopf-verified}.

\subsection{Abiotic Depletion Horizon and Critical Thresholds}
\label{sec:depletion-horizon}
With the explicit, two-compartment abiotic reservoir $(\mathbf{A}^{\text{act}}_i,\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i)$ (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}) and the global groundwater-stress framing of the groundwater footprint \cite{gleeson2012}, we can now define a physically grounded depletion metric \emph{per compartment}, since the two pools are depleted by physically distinct flows and, for resources with substantial direct mining, can be on entirely different trajectories. For each service component $(i,c)$, let $A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}(0)$ and $A^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}(0)$ be the initial active and geological pools, and let $A^{\text{act},\min}_{i,c} = \epsilon_{i,c} A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}(0)$ and $A^{\text{geo},\min}_{i,c} = \epsilon_{i,c} A^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}(0)$ be critical thresholds below which regeneration, or geological reserve accessibility, effectively ceases. The threshold fraction $\epsilon_{i,c}$ is a placeholder pending failure-condition-based boundaries --- for example a minimum service level, an irreversibility threshold, or a recovery-probability constraint --- and the sensitivity range (0.01--0.30) is indicative rather than calibrated.

We define the \textbf{dynamic depletion horizon} $T^{\text{dep}}_{i,c}$ (for either compartment) as the first time it falls below its threshold under the full augmented dynamics:
\begin{equation}
T^{\text{dep}}_{i,c} = \inf\left\{ t > 0 \;\middle|\; A^{\bullet}_{i,c}(t) \le A^{\bullet,\min}_{i,c} \right\}, \quad \bullet\in\{\text{act},\text{geo}\}. \label{eq:dynamic-horizon}
\end{equation}
This horizon is obtained by forward integration of the full ODE system and is the most accurate measure of the time remaining before the service is irreversibly compromised.

For a real-time diagnostic, the denominator for each compartment must be the flow that actually depletes it. By the two-pool dynamics of Section~\ref{sec:abiotic} (Eqs.~\eqref{eq:abiotic}--\eqref{eq:abiotic-geo}), the active pool $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}$ is depleted by the gross biological uptake $\mathbf B_{i,c}=\mathbf R_{i,c}+\boldsymbol\kappa_{A,i,c}N_{i,c}$, net of the geochemical exchange with $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}$. That exchange is a transfer between the two compartments, not a loss from the system; we retain it in the horizon's underlying dynamics but exclude it as a second depleting flow in $\mathcal H^{\text{act}}_{i,c}$'s denominator, since it is a restoring rather than a depleting term. The geological pool $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}$, by contrast, is depleted by direct anthropogenic mining $\mathbf C^A_{i,c}$; when the active pool sits below its geochemical equilibrium ($A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}<A^{\text{act,eq}}_{i,c}$), the exchange term $\omega_{A,i,c}(A^{\text{act,eq}}_{i,c}-A^{\text{act}}_{i,c})$ also drains the geological compartment (Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic-geo}). For a resource with a genuinely active mining channel the exchange is typically small relative to $\mathbf C^A_{i,c}$, but it need not be, and the compartment-level question --- how long until this compartment crosses its threshold --- is governed by the compartment's own net rate of change rather than by any single nominal depleting flow; the net horizon defined below therefore uses the compartment's full loss rate.

These two denominators are not interchangeable. Using the biological extraction flow $\mathbf C_{i,c}$ (or its sustainable-yield component $\bar{\mathbf C}_{i,c}$) as either denominator would be a category error, comparable to estimating how long a mineral deposit will last from the rate at which finished goods are sold rather than from the rate at which the deposit itself is mined. Using $\mathbf B_{i,c}$ for the geological pool (or $\mathbf C^A_{i,c}$ for the active pool) would likewise conflate two physically and causally separate depletion processes. We therefore define \textbf{two} instantaneous Abiotic Depletion Horizons:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{H}^{\text{act}}_{i,c}(t) = \frac{A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}(t) - A^{\text{act},\min}_{i,c}}{B_{i,c}(t)}, \qquad
\mathcal{H}^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}(t) = \frac{A^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}(t) - A^{\text{geo},\min}_{i,c}}{C^A_{i,c}(t)}, \label{eq:instant-horizon}
\end{equation}
where $B_{i,c}(t)=R_{i,c}(t)+\kappa_{A,i,c}N_{i,c}(t)\,\dfrac{A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}(t)}{A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}(t)+A_{0,i,c}}$ is the gross abiotic uptake for the stock component underlying service $(i,c)$, in the donor-limited form of Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}, Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic} (the regeneration term $R_{i,c}$ itself carries the same factor and vanishes as $A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}\to0$), and $C^A_{i,c}(t)$ is the direct mining flow of Section~\ref{sec:abiotic} ($\mathcal H^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}$, the mining-only gross horizon, is undefined, in the ordinary sense of division by zero, whenever $C^A_{i,c}=0$, i.e., for resources where extraction acts on $\mathbf N_i$ alone and no direct mining channel is active; the net geological horizon $\mathcal H^{\text{geo,net}}_{i,c}$ defined below remains finite in that case whenever the geochemical exchange is draining the compartment). Both quantities have units of years; $\mathcal H^{\text{act}}_{i,c}$ answers \emph{``how long until the ecosystem's own bioavailable support is exhausted at its current metabolic draw,''} and $\mathcal H^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}$ answers \emph{``how long until the mined deposit itself runs out,''} two different and, for a resource where both channels are active, generally divergent questions. For a resource extracted entirely biologically (fisheries, forestry), $\mathcal H^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}$ is simply not reported; for a resource mined entirely from a geological deposit (fossil aquifers, phosphate rock), $\mathcal H^{\text{act}}_{i,c}$ still tracks the (typically much longer, near-constant) horizon of the fast-cycling active pool while $\mathcal H^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}$ tracks the actual mining exhaustion the resource user should be most concerned with. Neither horizon is about the rate of biological harvest itself, which the diagnostic $\Lambda_i(t)$ and the productivity gap $\mathbf G_i$ (Section~\ref{sec:productivity-gap}) already track separately.

\paragraph{$\mathcal H^{\text{act}}_{i,c}$ as defined above answers a gross-draw question, not a time-to-exhaustion question, and the two must not be conflated.} $B_{i,c}$ is \emph{gross} uptake: it is exactly the quantity the productivity-illusion diagnostic (Section~\ref{sec:introduction}, Section~\ref{sec:productivity-gap}) needs, since a mature, stable stock ($N_{i,c}\approx K_{i,c}$) can have $B_{i,c}>0$ even while the pool itself is at a stationary equilibrium, net inflow exactly balancing net outflow.
