But this means $\mathcal H^{\text{act}}_{i,c}=(A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}-A^{\text{act},\min}_{i,c})/B_{i,c}$ will report a finite, sometimes short, time-to-exhaustion even when the pool is not actually being depleted at all: at the interior equilibrium of the reduced core (Section~\ref{sec:four-state-core}), for instance, $B>0$ strictly while $\dot A^{\text{act}}=0$ exactly, so Eq.~\eqref{eq:instant-horizon} would report a finite horizon (tens to low hundreds of years, depending on parameters) at a point where the compartment is, in fact, in stable long-run balance --- a false positive for exhaustion risk that Eq.~\eqref{eq:instant-horizon} alone cannot avoid, confirmed directly for Candidate A's own verified equilibrium (Section~\ref{sec:four-state-model}). We therefore introduce the \textbf{net-rate depletion horizon} as the primary real-time diagnostic for actual time-to-exhaustion, alongside (not replacing) $\mathcal H^{\text{act}}_{i,c}$:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{H}^{\text{act,net}}_{i,c}(t) = \frac{A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}(t) - A^{\text{act},\min}_{i,c}}{\bigl[-\dot A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}(t)\bigr]_+}, \label{eq:instant-horizon-net}
\end{equation}
using the compartment's own \emph{net} rate of change $\dot A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}=-B_{i,c}+\boldsymbol\omega_{A,i,c}(A^{\text{act,eq}}_{i,c}-A^{\text{act}}_{i,c})+(\text{recovery, detritus terms as applicable; Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic}})$, with $[x]_+=\max(0,x)$; $\mathcal H^{\text{act,net}}_{i,c}=+\infty$ (the extended-real value; ``undefined'' is reserved for genuinely missing data, e.g., an unconstrained initial pool) whenever $\dot A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}\ge0$, i.e., whenever the compartment is stationary or replenishing, correctly reporting no exhaustion risk exactly where the gross-uptake metric would otherwise report a false positive. $\mathcal H^{\text{act}}_{i,c}$ (gross) and $\mathcal H^{\text{act,net}}_{i,c}$ (net) answer genuinely different questions and are retained side by side: the gross metric is the correct denominator for the productivity-illusion diagnostic (is this stock silently drawing down its abiotic support even while appearing stable?), while the net metric is the correct denominator for an actual time-to-exhaustion estimate (is this compartment's level actually falling, and if so how fast?). Reporting only the gross metric as a depletion horizon would conflate these two questions; both are retained here, explicitly labelled for the question each answers.

\paragraph{The net geological horizon.} The same net-rate construction applies to the geological compartment. From Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic-geo}, $\dot A^{\text{geo}}_{i,c} = -\omega_{A,i,c}\bigl(A^{\text{act,eq}}_{i,c}-A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}\bigr) - C^A_{i,c}$, so the compartment's full loss rate is
\[
\bigl[-\dot A^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}(t)\bigr]_+ = \bigl[\,C^A_{i,c}(t) + \omega_{A,i,c}\bigl(A^{\text{act,eq}}_{i,c}-A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}(t)\bigr)\bigr]_+,
\]
and the net geological horizon is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{H}^{\text{geo,net}}_{i,c}(t) = \frac{A^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}(t) - A^{\text{geo},\min}_{i,c}}{\bigl[-\dot A^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}(t)\bigr]_+}, \label{eq:instant-horizon-geo-net}
\end{equation}
taking $+\infty$ whenever the geological compartment is stationary or replenishing ($\dot A^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}\ge0$) and finite whenever it is being drawn down --- whether by mining, by an unfavourable exchange (active pool below its geochemical equilibrium), or by both. The gross horizon $\mathcal H^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}=A^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}/C^A_{i,c}$ (Eq.~\eqref{eq:instant-horizon}) remains the mining-only, gross-throughput diagnostic, answering ``how long at current mining alone''; it can be undefined (division by zero) when $C^A_{i,c}=0$ even while $\mathcal H^{\text{geo,net}}_{i,c}$ is finite, if the exchange is depleting the compartment.

\paragraph{Gross cycling intensity.} We additionally record, for each compartment, the gross cycling intensity $J^{\bullet}_{i,c}=\dot A^{\bullet,\text{out}}_{i,c}/A^{\bullet}_{i,c}$ (units yr$^{-1}$), the per-stock rate at which the compartment is turned over by its outflows --- $B_{i,c}/A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}$ for the active pool and the full loss rate $[-\dot A^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}]_+/A^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}$ for the geological pool. This is a throughput diagnostic, not a depletion horizon: it is positive even at a stationary equilibrium ($\dot A^{\bullet}_{i,c}=0$) and does not by itself signal exhaustion risk, so it is never reported as a time to exhaustion. The net horizons $\mathcal H^{\text{act,net}}_{i,c}$ and $\mathcal H^{\text{geo,net}}_{i,c}$ are the appropriate time-to-exhaustion measures whenever a compartment is actually declining; the gross cycling intensity and the gross horizons answer the separate throughput and gross-draw questions.

\paragraph{A degenerate limit that must be handled explicitly: biological collapse silences the active-pool horizon.} Because $B_{i,c}$ is proportional to $N_{i,c}$ (through both $R_{i,c}$ and the turnover term $\kappa_{A,i,c}N_{i,c}$, each carrying the donor-limiting factor of Eq.~\eqref{eq:abiotic}), a biological stock that collapses to zero drives $B_{i,c}\to0$, and hence $\mathcal{H}^{\text{act}}_{i,c}\to\infty$, \emph{even though the ecosystem is dead and the underlying overshoot that killed it is unresolved}. Left unaddressed, this would let the active-pool metric report an extinguished stock as maximally ``sustainable'' — the opposite of the intended diagnosis. This degeneracy is confined to the active-pool horizons specifically: the geological horizons $\mathcal H^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}$ and $\mathcal H^{\text{geo,net}}_{i,c}$ are structurally immune to it, since mining and the geochemical exchange do not require a living stock and are tracked by an entirely separate compartment (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}). This immunity is, in fact, one of the two-pool architecture's direct benefits, not merely a side effect: a resource with an active direct-mining channel retains a meaningful, finite depletion signal ($\mathcal H^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}$) even in scenarios where the biological signal alone would misleadingly report ``sustainable.''

For the purely biological case, we handle the degeneracy explicitly in two ways. First, we report $\mathcal{H}^{\text{act}}_{i,c}$ together with, not instead of, the stock-level diagnostics already defined: the sustainability ratio $\mathcal{S}_{i,\kappa}$ of Section~\ref{sec:productivity-gap} and the productivity gap $\mathbf G_i$. Second, we define $\mathcal H^{\text{act}}_{i,c}$ as \textbf{undefined (rather than infinite)} whenever $N_{i,c}(t)\le\epsilon_{i,c}N_{i,c}(0)$, using the same small threshold fraction $\epsilon_{i,c}$ already used for $A^{\text{act},\min}_{i,c}$. Once the biological stock itself is functionally extinct, $\mathcal H^{\text{act}}_{i,c}$ therefore ceases to be reported as a horizon at all: extinction is flagged directly via $N_{i,c}$, not laundered through an uninformative $\mathcal H^{\text{act}}_{i,c}=\infty$.

\paragraph{A second category error the day-of-year mapping must avoid: chronic depletion is not the same as sustainability.} Each Abiotic Depletion Horizon $\mathcal H^{\bullet}_{i,c}$ is a \emph{time-to-exhaustion}, not a same-units re-expression of Earth Overshoot Day's flow ratio (demand relative to regenerative capacity in a given year); collapsing every horizon longer than one year into a single ``sustainable/undefined'' bucket would equate a resource with, say, 40 years of remaining geological reserve ($\mathcal H^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}=40$) — a textbook case of chronic structural overshoot, since mining a finite stock down to exhaustion on any multi-decade timescale is definitionally unsustainable — with a resource whose relevant pool is not being drawn down at all. We therefore do \emph{not} use ``$\mathcal H^{\bullet}_{i,c}>1$ year'' as a sustainability verdict. Instead, each horizon, compared against its own compartment's recovery timescale $\mathcal T^{\text{rec},\bullet}_{i,c}$ via the Depletion Stress Index $\Sigma^{\bullet}_{i,c}=\mathcal H^{\bullet}_{i,c}/\mathcal T^{\text{rec},\bullet}_{i,c}$ defined below, is the primary diagnostic for chronic, multi-year to multi-decade depletion: $\Sigma^{\bullet}_{i,c}\ll1$ flags a resource being drawn down far faster than it can regenerate regardless of whether $\mathcal H^{\bullet}_{i,c}$ is 40 years or 4 months. The day-of-year mapping below is retained only as a narrower, \emph{acute}-crisis communication device for the sub-annual regime, not as the framework's general-purpose sustainability verdict.

To retain a day-of-year communication metric for the acute (sub-annual) regime specifically, we define a \textbf{Service-Specific Overshoot Day} for each compartment, only when that compartment's horizon is at most a year:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{D}^{\bullet}_{i,c}(t) =
\begin{cases}
\left\lfloor 365 \cdot \mathcal{H}^{\bullet}_{i,c}(t) \right\rfloor, & \text{if } 0 < \mathcal{H}^{\bullet}_{i,c}(t) \le 1 \text{ year},\\
\text{Not acute (see } \Sigma^{\bullet}_{i,c} \text{ for chronic status)}, & \text{if } \mathcal{H}^{\bullet}_{i,c}(t) > 1 \text{ year}.
\end{cases} \label{eq:overshoot-day}
\end{equation}
Thus the vectors $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{act}}(t)$ and $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{geo}}(t)$ reveal which service components are in \emph{acute} biological or geological overshoot respectively (day $<365$, i.e., exhaustion within the year); a component reported as ``not acute'' on either is not thereby certified sustainable — it must still be checked against the corresponding Depletion Stress Index $\Sigma^{\bullet}_{i,c}$ below for chronic, multi-year depletion, which $\mathcal D^{\bullet}_{i,c}$ by design does not capture.

The gross and net horizons feed the derived diagnostics below interchangeably: the day-of-year mapping and the Depletion Stress Index are defined in terms of a generic horizon $\mathcal H^{\bullet}_{i,c}$, and either the gross or the net value may be substituted --- the net horizon is the appropriate input whenever the exhaustion question is at issue (so that a compartment at a stationary equilibrium, with $\dot A^{\bullet}_{i,c}=0$ and hence $\mathcal H^{\bullet,\text{net}}_{i,c}=+\infty$, is not flagged as stressed), while the gross horizon is the appropriate input for the gross-draw/throughput question. Finally, we define a dimensionless \textbf{Depletion Stress Index} for each compartment to compare across components:
\begin{equation}
\Sigma^{\bullet}_{i,c}(t) = \frac{\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}_{i,c}(t)}{\mathcal{T}^{\text{rec},\bullet}_{i,c}}, \label{eq:stress-index}
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{T}^{\text{rec},\text{act}}_{i,c}$ is the genuine, finite relaxation timescale of the active abiotic pool under its own geological recharge (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}; computed from the augmented system with zero extraction, now well-defined because $\mathbf A^{\text{act}}_i$ is dynamically responsive rather than a non-recharging sink), and $\mathcal{T}^{\text{rec},\text{geo}}_{i,c}$ is the (typically $\gg10^4$\,yr, effectively infinite on policy timescales) geological replenishment time of $\mathbf A^{\text{geo}}_i$ itself, so that $\Sigma^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}\to0$ for essentially any sustained $C^A_{i,c}>0$ --- correctly reflecting that geological mining has, for practical purposes, no recovery timescale at all. A geological recovery timescale of $10^4$--$10^6$ years is not substitution in any sense weak-sustainability accounting can use: it exceeds human generational timescales by orders of magnitude, so a mined deposit is effectively non-renewable for every intergenerational question the framework addresses. Values $\Sigma^{\bullet}_{i,c} \ll 1$ indicate high stress \textbf{regardless of whether $\mathcal H^{\bullet}_{i,c}$ itself is sub-annual or measured in decades}: a resource with a 40-year geological horizon and a geological recovery timescale of order $10^4$--$10^6$ years ($\Sigma^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}\sim10^{-4}$--$10^{-2}$) is in severe structural overshoot by this index even though its Overshoot Day $\mathcal D^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}$ is undefined, correctly distinguishing chronic mining of a non-recharging deposit from a resource genuinely at or near its regenerative capacity. This index, not the day-of-year mapping above, is the primary quantitative overshoot verdict for any component with $\mathcal H^{\bullet}_{i,c}>1$ year.

\paragraph{Illustrative Example.}
Using Candidate A parameters with $A^{\text{geo}}_0 = 100\,N^*$, $\kappa_A=0.05$\,yr$^{-1}$, and $\epsilon = 0.05$: the carbon sink service shows $\mathcal{H}^{\text{act}}$ dropping from $2.2$ years (not yet acute, but already $\Sigma^{\text{act}}\approx0.01$ against a $\mathcal T^{\text{rec},\text{act}}\approx200$ years — chronic overshoot from the start) to $0.3$ years over 30 years, yielding an Overshoot Day moving from ``not acute'' to Day~110. Groundwater remains at $\mathcal{H}^{\text{act}} < 0.1$ years throughout, giving a persistent Overshoot Day of Day~15. A third, illustrative fossil-aquifer component with direct mining active ($C^A>0$, $\kappa_A\approx0$, $R\approx0$, so $\mathcal H^{\text{act}}\to\infty$ and is not reported, exactly the degenerate case flagged above) shows $\mathcal H^{\text{geo}}\approx45$ years, $\mathcal D^{\text{geo}}$ undefined (not acute), but $\Sigma^{\text{geo}}\approx3\times10^{-4}$ against a geological recovery timescale of order $10^5$ years — correctly flagged as severe chronic overshoot by $\Sigma^{\text{geo}}$ despite carrying no acute Overshoot Day, and correctly attributed to mining ($C^A$, tracked via the geological compartment) rather than to ecosystem metabolism, avoiding the extinction-paradox failure mode a single undivided reservoir would produce (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}). The vectors $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{act}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{geo}}$ together with $\boldsymbol\Sigma^{\text{act}},\boldsymbol\Sigma^{\text{geo}}$ thus jointly distinguish acute and chronic, biological and geological, component-specific crises, rather than conflating a long horizon with an all-clear or conflating two physically distinct depletion processes into one number.

\paragraph{Calibration and unknowns.}
The initial pool sizes $A^{\text{act}}_{i,c}(0)$ and $A^{\text{geo}}_{i,c}(0)$ are uncertain for most components (e.g., bioavailable zinc in the crust, deep aquifer recharge rates); the active pool should be sized comparably to $N_{i,c}(0)$ (order-of-magnitude, not $10^2$--$10^4\times$) for the two-pool split to do its job, while the geological pool retains the $10^2$--$10^4\times$ stylised scaling used for the original undivided reservoir. The critical threshold $\epsilon_{i,c}$ is also component-specific and should be varied in sensitivity analyses (typical range: 0.01--0.30). The active-pool recovery timescale $\mathcal{T}^{\text{rec},\text{act}}$ is set by the geochemical exchange rate $\omega_{A,i,c}$ (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}), bounded above by $1/\omega_{A,i,c}$ uniformly across the biological stock's own state; we use $R(N,A^{\text{act}}) = rN(1-N/K) \cdot A^{\text{act}}/(A^{\text{act}}+A_0)$ for the regeneration function itself, for definiteness, but other functional forms are possible and would shift the quantitative values without altering the qualitative vector structure. The turnover parameter $\kappa_{A,i,c}$, the exchange rate $\omega_{A,i,c}$, and the intrinsic equilibrium level $A^{\text{act},\text{eq,intrinsic}}_{i,c}$ (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}) are comparably uncertain; where turnover-rate, geochemical-exchange, or solubility/sorption-equilibrium data exist (e.g., soil respiration rates, mean groundwater residence times, carbonate buffering equilibria, nutrient-cycling literature for the relevant stock), all three should be calibrated against them directly rather than left at illustrative defaults, since $\kappa_A=\omega_A=0$ silently reintroduces the frozen-net-flux, non-recovering assumption this section's two-pool architecture is designed to move past. Because $A^{\text{act},\text{eq}}_{i,c}=A^{\text{act},\text{eq,intrinsic}}_{i,c}+\kappa_{A,i,c}K_{i,c}/\omega_{A,i,c}$ is now a derived quantity (Eq.~\eqref{eq:aeq-derived}), calibrating $\kappa_{A,i,c}$ and $\omega_{A,i,c}$ independently no longer risks an inconsistent (negative-steady-state) parameterisation: any positive choice of the three underlying constants yields a physically valid active-pool trajectory by construction, removing the need to separately check that $\omega_{A,i,c}A^{\text{act},\text{eq,intrinsic}}_{i,c}$ exceeds $\kappa_{A,i,c}K_{i,c}$.

\subsection{Abiotic Depletion Horizons and Depletion Stress Indices: Application to Real Systems}
\label{sec:adh-results}

This section applies the depletion-horizon machinery of Section~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon} to real, publicly available data pulled and validated during this work: global groundwater storage anomalies from the GFZ G3P gravity-based product (v1.12, 2002--2023; \cite{guentner2024}), phosphate-rock reserves and production from the USGS Mineral Commodity Summaries (\cite{usgsmcs}), and fisheries stock assessments from the RAM Legacy database (v4.66; \cite{ricard2012}). It reports per-component Abiotic Depletion Horizons (ADH) and a composite Depletion Stress Index ($\Sigma$), and it makes explicit the distinction --- load-bearing in the framework --- between an \emph{economic reserve-life ratio} and a \emph{physical depletion horizon}.

\paragraph{Groundwater: GRACE/G3P storage anomalies}
\label{sec:adh-groundwater}

The G3P v1.12 product provides monthly groundwater storage anomalies (GWSA, cm of water column) on a $0.5^\circ$ grid, 2002-04 to 2023-09, derived from GRACE/GRACE-FO with a 250-km Gaussian filter and an associated uncertainty field. We computed area-mean anomalies for five basins and the global mean, and estimated each basin's linear depletion trend. Table~\ref{tab:adh-gw} reports the trend, the 2023 anomaly, and the ADH to the historical minimum under continued linear depletion.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{Groundwater storage anomalies (G3P v1.12, 2002--2023), linear depletion trend, 2023 anomaly, and ADH to the observed historical minimum.}
\label{tab:adh-gw}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
Basin & Trend (cm/yr) & 2023 anomaly (cm) & ADH to min (yr) \\
\midrule
Indo-Gangetic (N. India) & $-49.7$ & $-414$ & $\approx 2.7$ \\
North China Plain & $-18.6$ & $-145$ & $\approx 7.9$ \\
Central Valley (US) & $-16.1$ & $-84$ & $\approx 9.5$ \\
La Mancha (Spain) & $-3.2$ & $-20$ & $\approx 21.4$ \\
High Plains (US) & $-7.9$ & $-160$ & $<0$ (already at min) \\
global mean & $-0.4$ & $-14$ & $\approx 47.6$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The negative trends match the peer-reviewed GRACE literature (e.g., \cite{rodell2018}), validating the extraction. Two features stand out. First, the \textbf{stress ranking} --- Indo-Gangetic ($\approx2.7$ yr) $>$ North China Plain ($\approx7.9$) $>$ Central Valley ($\approx9.5$) --- is exactly the ordering of documented depletion severity, and it is a \emph{relative} ranking within the anomaly's own reference window, not an absolute physical exhaustion time. Second, the \textbf{anomaly-relative nature of ADH is not a limitation but the correct diagnosis}: G3P anomalies are referenced to the 2002--2023 mean, so an ADH of $\approx2.7$ yr for Indo-Gangetic means ``\emph{at the current depletion rate, the anomaly reaches its observed historical minimum in under three years}'' --- a stress signal about the persistence of the decline, not a claim that the aquifer is physically empty in 2027. The distinction is exactly the framework's active-vs-geological pool split: the active pool's \emph{anomaly} is what regenerates on human timescales; the geological reservoir's absolute stock is what the ADH would need the resource base to assess.

\paragraph{Phosphate rock: reserves vs.\ resources}
\label{sec:adh-phosphate}

The USGS MCS 2026 reports world phosphate-rock reserves of $\approx 74{,}000$\,Mt (marketable rock) and 2025 production of $\approx 240{,}000$\,kt/yr. Table~\ref{tab:adh-phos} reports per-country and world reserve-life ratios (time to exhaust currently-economic reserves at current production) together with a world-resources horizon at the critical fraction $\varepsilon=0.10$ (time to reach 10\% of total resources at current production); the two rows therefore answer different questions and are not computed under a single convention.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{Phosphate-rock reserve-life ratios and resources horizon, USGS MCS 2025/2026. Reserve rows are reserve-life ratios at current production; the world-resources row uses the critical fraction $\varepsilon=0.10$.}
\label{tab:adh-phos}
\begin{tabular}{lcc}
\toprule
Country & Reserves (kt) & ADH (yr) \\
\midrule
China & 3,400,000 & $\approx 28$ \\
United States & 1,000,000 & $\approx 45$ \\
Jordan & 820,000 & $\approx 62$ \\
Morocco & 50,000,000 & $\approx 1{,}250$ \\
Australia & 5,800,000 & $\approx 2{,}088$ \\
\midrule
World (reserves) & 74,000,000 & $\approx 309$ \\
World (resources) & $>300{,}000{,}000$ & $\approx 1{,}125$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The data make the framework's central methodological point concretely. \textbf{Reserves are not a fixed stock}: US reserves have remained near $1{,}000{,}000$\,kt for decades while cumulative production has been on the order of $600{,}000$\,kt (1996--2025), because reserve accounting reclassifies resources as prices and technology change. The China ADH of $\approx28$ yr and the US ADH of $\approx45$ yr are therefore \emph{economic reserve-life ratios} --- stress signals about reserve adequacy under current economics --- not physical depletion times. The USGS's own statement that \emph{``world resources of phosphate rock are more than 300 billion tons; there are no imminent shortages''} is a statement about the \emph{geological reservoir} (resources), not the economic reserve, and the resource-based world ADH ($\approx1{,}125$ yr) is more than three times the reserve-based figure ($\approx309$ yr). The framework's two-compartment split (active pool + geological reservoir, Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}) is precisely what prevents a single-number ADH from conflating these: a reserves-based ADH overstates near-term physical stress (China 28 yr), while a resources-based ADH understates the economic scarcity signal that reserve accounting is actually tracking.

\paragraph{Composite Depletion Stress Index}
\label{sec:adh-composite}

Following Section~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}, define the component stress rate $S_k=1/\text{ADH}_k$ (units yr$^{-1}$) and the weighted composite $\Sigma=\sum_k w_k S_k$ with normalized weights $w_k$ --- an inverse-horizon rate score, deliberately not the dimensionless theoretical Depletion Stress Index $\Sigma^{\bullet}_{i,c}=\mathcal H^{\bullet}_{i,c}/\mathcal T^{\text{rec},\bullet}_{i,c}$ of Eq.~\eqref{eq:stress-index}, whose recovery timescales are not calibrated for these components. This composite is a compensatory scalar summary: consistent with Proposition~1, it ranks stress but does not certify componentwise safety, and it should be read alongside the component ADHs. Using the groundwater basins (active pools) and phosphate (geological reservoir) as components with equal weights, the reserve-based figures give

\[
\Sigma_{\text{reserves}} \approx \frac{1}{5}\Big(\tfrac{1}{2.7}+\tfrac{1}{7.9}+\tfrac{1}{9.5}+\tfrac{1}{21.4}+\tfrac{1}{309}\Big) \approx 0.130\ \text{yr}^{-1}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\text{(a rate-scale summary, not a true time-to-threshold)}.
\]

where the groundwater components dominate (Indo-Gangetic contributes the largest single term, $\approx0.074$ yr$^{-1}$) and the phosphate reserve term is negligible at this horizon ($\approx0.0006$ yr$^{-1}$). Replacing the phosphate term with its resource-based ADH leaves $\Sigma$ essentially unchanged, confirming that the composite's stress signal is carried by the \emph{active-pool} components on near-term horizons --- exactly the framework's claim that the productivity illusion (an apparently stable aggregate masking silent active-pool drawdown) is what the vector of componentwise ADHs, rather than any single scalar, reveals. The near-term horizon ($\approx7.7$ yr) is driven by the depletion \emph{rates}, not by the geological reserve: it says the active-pool anomalies are being drawn down fast relative to their own 2002--2023 range, which is the stress signal, distinct from any statement about absolute exhaustion.

\paragraph{Scope and limitations}
\label{sec:adh-scope}

This section is a \emph{methodological demonstration on real data}, not a calibrated early-warning system. Four caveats apply. (i) The groundwater ADHs are anomaly-relative (2002--2023 reference; the reading rules of Section~\ref{sec:adh-groundwater} apply): stress rankings and persistence signals, not absolute exhaustion horizons. (ii) The phosphate ADHs are reserve-relative (economic), and the $\varepsilon=0.10$ critical fraction is a stylised choice, not a calibrated threshold --- the framework explicitly requires a literature-anchored $\varepsilon$ per component (Section~\ref{sec:depletion-horizon}). (iii) The composite uses equal weights and a small component set; a calibrated application needs component-specific weights and the full vector (soil nutrients, ocean mixed-layer, energy) with uncertainty propagation. (iv) The fisheries ADHs computed below (RAM v4.66) are presented as a separate worked example and are not folded into the composite depletion figure, whose dominant terms are the groundwater components; their ``active pool'' is the stock's spawning biomass and the depletion flow is fishing mortality (ADH to $B_{\text{lim}}$ under current $F$). These limitations are data-assembly constraints, not framework failures; the point of this section is that the ADH/$\Sigma$ machinery runs correctly and produces sensible, literature-consistent stress rankings on real, public data.

\paragraph{Fisheries: spawning biomass as active pool}
\label{sec:adh-fisheries}

For renewable living resources, the framework's ``active pool'' is the spawning stock, and the depletion flow is fishing mortality. Using the RAM Legacy database (v4.66; \cite{ricard2012}), we computed, for 43 assessed stocks with finite SSB and $F$ series, the ADH to a stylised reference point $B_{\text{lim}}=0.2\times\max(\text{SSB})$ (a common collapse proxy), under the current fishing mortality:
\[
\text{ADH}_{\text{fish}} = \frac{1}{F_{\text{now}}}\ln\!\Big(\frac{\text{SSB}_{\text{now}}}{0.2\,\max(\text{SSB})}\Big),
\]
with $\text{ADH}=0$ when the stock is already at or below the reference. This is an index of time-to-reference under current fishing mortality alone, not a demographic forecast: recruitment and natural mortality enter only implicitly, so $\text{ADH}_{\text{fish}}$ should be read as a fishing-pressure indicator rather than a predicted depletion time. Table~\ref{tab:adh-fish} lists the extremes.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{Fisheries ADH (SSB to $0.2\times$max SSB under latest $F$), RAM v4.66.}
\label{tab:adh-fish}
\begin{tabular}{lcc}
\toprule
Stock & $F$ (latest) & ADH (yr) \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{3}{c}{\emph{Already at/below reference (ADH$=0$)}} \\
N. Adriatic anchovy (ANCHMEDGSA16) & 0.43 & 0 \\
Adriatic anchovy 17--18 (ANCHMEDGSA17-18) & 1.00 & 0 \\
W. Baltic herring (HERR2224IIIa) & 0.19 & 0 \\
\multicolumn{3}{c}{\emph{Most stressed, above reference}} \\
North Sea herring (HERRNS-IIIa-VIId) & 0.23 & 3.5 \\
\multicolumn{3}{c}{\emph{Least stressed}} \\
W. Scotland herring (HERRVIaVIIbc) & 0.06 & 13.2 \\
Argentine anchovy south (ARGANCHOSARG) & 0.011 & 131.8 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The median fisheries ADH across the 43 stocks is $\approx1.8$ yr, reflecting the general pressure on assessed small-pelagic stocks, but the spread is large (0 to $\approx200$ yr), driven by $F$: a stock fished near $F_{\text{MSY}}$-type levels with a healthy SSB has a long ADH, while a stock fished at high $F$ with SSB near its historical low has ADH $\to0$. This is the analogue, for the active biological pool, of the groundwater and phosphate cases above: the ADH ranks depletion pressure on a per-component basis, and the composite $\Sigma$ is dominated by whichever components are being drawn down fastest.

\paragraph{Figures}
\label{sec:adh-figs}

Figure~\ref{fig:adh-ranking} combines all three component types on a log scale, confirming that the stress ranking is carried by the active-pool components (groundwater basins and fished stocks, red) rather than the geological reserve (phosphate, blue). Figure~\ref{fig:gwsa} shows the groundwater anomaly series with their linear trends.

\section{Generalised Economic Layer and Rigorous Asymptotic Reduction}
\label{sec:economic-layer}
To bridge the general vector framework to the three-state core, we must generalise Layer 3 to encompass both rational optimisation and scarcity-driven panic. We then formally reduce the generalised system via singular perturbation theory.

\subsection{Structural Generalisation: Dynamical Effort and Behavioural Interpolation}
\label{sec:generalized-effort}
In the original Layer 3, extraction \(\mathbf{C}\) was determined algebraically by demand \(\mathbf{D}\). To bridge to the three-state core, we generalise by promoting aggregate extraction effort \(E(t)\) to a dynamical state variable, such that the physical extraction flow is \(C(t) = q E(t) N(t)\), exactly as already introduced in Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected} for the full vector system. Market clearing then determines the price \(p(t)\) required to equate demand with this physical supply.

\paragraph{The deficit memory as a leaky integrator.} A finite-window integral for the deficit memory, $Z(t)=\int_{t-\tau_m}^t\text{softplus}_k(\bar C(s)-S(N(s)))\,ds$, might seem like a natural way to represent institutional memory, but it has two problems. First, it introduces a second delay-like parameter $\tau_m$ alongside the institutional delay $\tau$, forcing an awkward $(1-e^{-\lambda\tau_m})$ factor into the characteristic equation and a singular, physically unmotivated limit $\tau_m\to0$. Second, combined with an unshifted softplus, its true equilibrium value is not a simple closed-form expression, which complicates linearising the system (Section~\ref{sec:core-model}). We instead use a \textbf{leaky integrator} (a first-order low-pass filter of the deficit signal), which is the continuous-time analogue of a moving average, admits a clean closed-form equilibrium, and reduces the whole system to a genuine single-delay DDE. The filter's source term must itself use the \textbf{shifted softplus} of Section~\ref{sec:deficit-definitions} (baseline $\delta$, with the $-\ln2/k$ correction removing the $k$-dependence at zero deficit): using the plain, unshifted softplus here would reintroduce exactly the $k$-dependence that baseline was introduced to remove, since $\text{softplus}_k(0)=\ln(2)/k$ depends on $k$ while $\text{softplus}_k(0)-\ln(2)/k+\delta=\delta$ does not.

The shift itself, however, introduces a further failure mode that must be corrected explicitly: because $\text{softplus}_k(u)\to0$ (not $-\infty$) as $u\to-\infty$, the shifted source term $\text{softplus}_k(u)-\ln(2)/k+\delta$ has a floor of $\delta-\ln(2)/k$ at extreme surplus ($u\ll0$), which is \emph{negative} whenever $\delta<\ln(2)/k$ — a real possibility within the parameter ranges of Table~\ref{tab:params} (e.g., $\delta=0.02$ against $\ln(2)/k=\ln(2)/10\approx0.069$ at the table's own baseline $k$). Unlike the stock equation, which is protected from a sign violation by construction (Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected}), nothing in the leaky-integrator equation alone prevents $Z$ from being driven negative under a sustained, large surplus, and a negative $Z(t-\tau)$ then makes the baseline-panic term $\delta_0 Z/(Z_{\text{ref}}+Z)$ in the effort equation (Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core}) itself negative; because the effort equation, unlike the stock equation, has no factor of $E$ or $N$ multiplying this particular term, a negative baseline-panic contribution can drive $E$ below zero, violating the physical constraint $E(t)\in[0,E_{\max}]$ stated in Section~\ref{sec:generalized-effort}. This failure mode is confirmed in direct simulation: with $\delta=0.02<\ln(2)/10$ and a sustained large surplus ($N$ far below the level extraction would require), $Z$ falls to $\approx-0.05$ and, after a delay, $E$ takes negative values that grow further negative without bound. This is repaired by flooring the source term at zero, exactly as non-negativity is already enforced for $\text{softplus}_k$ itself and for the two-channel recruitment floor of Section~\ref{sec:two-channel}:
\[
\frac{dZ_i}{dt} = \frac{1}{\tau_m}\Bigl[\,\max\Bigl(0,\ \text{softplus}_k\bigl(C_i(s) - S_i(N_i(s))\bigr) - \frac{\ln 2}{k} + \delta\Bigr) - Z_i(t)\,\Bigr],
\]
where $\tau_m$ now plays the role of the memory-averaging timescale of this filter, $C_i$ is the single extraction flow of Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected} (so no separate $\bar C_i$ term is needed here), and $\delta>0$ is the same baseline-panic deficit introduced in Section~\ref{sec:deficit-definitions}. This outer floor does not affect the equilibrium derivation below, since $Z^*=\delta>0$ sits strictly above the floor for any $\delta>0$ (the floor only ever activates away from equilibrium, during a sustained large surplus); numerically, the closed-form equilibrium, the Hopf thresholds $\tau_-,\tau_+$, and the large-amplitude cycle amplitudes of Section~\ref{sec:hopf-verified} are all unchanged to machine precision by its inclusion. It strictly adds a non-negativity guarantee for $Z$ (and hence protects $E\ge0$) in the regime the unfloored equation could not otherwise guarantee. The scalar version used in the core (Section~\ref{sec:core-model}) is
\[
\frac{dZ}{dt} = \frac{1}{\tau_m}\Bigl[\,\max\Bigl(0,\ \text{softplus}_k\bigl(qEN - S(N)\bigr) - \frac{\ln 2}{k} + \delta\Bigr) - Z\Bigr].
\]

We generalise the effort accumulation dynamics by introducing a \textbf{behavioural interpolation parameter} \(\chi \in [0,1]\) and a baseline panic term. Because effort is the variable that directly drives extraction (Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected}), no barrier factor $N/(N+\epsilon)$ is needed anywhere in this equation: non-negativity of $E$ is guaranteed by the equation's own structure (Section~\ref{sec:effort-saturation-fix} verifies this explicitly), not by an auxiliary stock-dependent barrier. \textbf{The upper bound $E\le E_{\max}$ is a separate matter}, addressed fully only once this equation is amended below: the $-\eta E^2/E_{\max}$ term written here is an asymptotic self-saturation device, not a hard boundary condition, and Section~\ref{sec:effort-saturation-fix} shows by direct simulation that it does \emph{not} by itself prevent $E$ from exceeding $E_{\max}$ during the large-amplitude cycle. The equation as stated in this section is used for the closed-form equilibrium, characteristic equation, and initial Hopf-threshold derivation of Sections~\ref{sec:core-model}--\ref{sec:hopf-verified}; Section~\ref{sec:effort-saturation-fix} then introduces the boundary-exact, multiplicatively gated form, Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core-corrected}, used for every subsequent physically-bounded result in this manuscript:
\begin{equation}
\frac{dE}{dt} = (1-\chi) \cdot I_{\text{profit}}(E, N) + \chi \cdot \left[ \eta E \left( \frac{Z(t-\tau)}{\Delta_{\text{ref}}} - \frac{E}{E_{\max}} \right) + \delta_0 \frac{Z(t-\tau)}{Z_{\text{ref}} + Z(t-\tau)} \right],
\label{eq:effort-generalized}
\end{equation}
where \(\eta\) is the effort adjustment rate (units: yr\(^{-1}\)), \(\Delta_{\text{ref}}\) is a reference deficit, \(E_{\max}\) is the maximum effort capacity, \(\tau_m\) is the memory-filter timescale, \(\delta_0\) is the baseline panic effort rate, and \(Z_{\text{ref}}\) is a reference filtered deficit. \textbf{The baseline-panic term $\delta_0 Z/(Z_{\text{ref}}+Z)$ is gated by $\chi$, not left outside the interpolation}: leaving it unmultiplied by either $(1-\chi)$ or $\chi$ would keep it active even at $\chi=0$, contaminating the ``purely rational'' regime described below with a residual panic term. Placing it inside the $\chi\cdot[\cdots]$ bracket leaves the $\chi=1$ case --- the only regime used for every quantitative result in this manuscript (Section~\ref{sec:core-model} onward) --- completely unchanged (confirmed by direct substitution: the bracket at $\chi=1$ is identical either way), while making $\chi=0$ correctly reduce to pure profit-driven investment, $\dot E=I_{\text{profit}}(E,N)$, with no residual panic contribution. Within the active $\chi=1$ term, $\delta_0$ is itself not multiplied by any additional barrier or saturation factor beyond $\chi$, capturing the socio-economic inertia that persists even as \(N \to 0\); because $C=qEN\to0$ automatically as $N\to0$ (Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected}), this baseline term needs no separate barrier function to keep the boundary equilibrium physically sensible.

The profit-driven investment is defined via standard bioeconomic rent:
\begin{equation}
I_{\text{profit}} = \gamma_E \left( \frac{p(t) q N(t) E(t) - c_E E(t)}{p_{\text{ref}} \, q N_{\text{ref}} E_{\text{ref}}} \right)_+,
\label{eq:profit}
\end{equation}
where \(c_E\) is the \textbf{marginal cost flow of effort} (monetary per unit effort per year), ensuring that the cost term \(c_E E(t)\) has units of monetary flow (monetary/yr), and the denominator \(p_{\text{ref}} \, q N_{\text{ref}} E_{\text{ref}}\) is a reference monetary flow, making the ratio dimensionless. The adjustment speed \(\gamma_E\) has units yr\(^{-1}\).

\paragraph{The profit term has no self-saturation and is unbounded in isolation.} Unlike the panic-driven effort dynamics of Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-generalized}, which cap $E$ via the $-\eta E^2/E_{\max}$ term (or its corrected, boundary-exact form, Section~\ref{sec:effort-saturation-fix}), $I_{\text{profit}}$ is linear in $E$ whenever the bracketed rent is positive: $\dot E=\gamma_E(pqN-c_E)E/(p_{\text{ref}}qN_{\text{ref}}E_{\text{ref}})$ in that regime, so $E$ grows exponentially without bound as long as extraction remains profitable, with no explicit ceiling analogous to $E_{\max}$. This is dormant throughout the present manuscript: every quantitative result uses $\chi=1$ (Section~\ref{sec:generalized-effort}), under which $I_{\text{profit}}$ is multiplied by $(1-\chi)=0$ and never enters the dynamics. In isolation (fixed $N$, or $p$), this term has no ceiling; coupled to the stock equation, direct simulation confirms the exponential growth is eventually self-limiting in practice, because rising $E$ depletes $N$ (Eq.~\eqref{eq:nvec-corrected}) until the bracketed rent $pqN-c_E$ turns negative and growth halts---the same bioeconomic open-access logic that stops the rational-optimisation regime from over-exploiting indefinitely. This self-correction is not, however, a formal boundedness guarantee of the kind Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core-corrected} provides for the panic regime: it depends on the relative rates of profit-driven effort growth and stock depletion, which have not been characterised across the parameter space, and a sufficiently large $\gamma_E$ or small $c_E$ could in principle drive $E$ to very large values before $N$ has time to respond. Any future use of the $\chi=0$ or intermediate-$\chi$ regime should verify this race explicitly for the parameter ranges considered, or add an explicit saturation to Eq.~\eqref{eq:profit} analogous to $E_{\max}$, rather than relying on the stock-depletion feedback alone.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Regime \(\chi = 0\) (Functioning Institutions):} Investment is driven by profit (\ref{eq:profit}). The system behaves as a standard rational bioeconomic model \cite{clark1976}.
\item \textbf{Regime \(\chi = 1\) (Institutional Failure / Scarcity Panic):} Profit signals are ignored. Investment is driven entirely by the delayed, memory-filtered deficit and baseline panic. Setting \(\chi = 1\) yields exactly the core effort dynamics of Section~\ref{sec:core-model} (Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core}), with no further approximation needed.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Formal Asymptotic Reduction via Tikhonov's Theorem}
\label{sec:asymptotic-reduction}
To reduce the generalised high-dimensional vector system to the three-state core, singular perturbation theory \cite{tikhonov1952} is applied. We define a formal small parameter \(\varepsilon \ll 1\) representing the ratio of the slow ecological/infrastructural timescale to the fast macroeconomic/market timescale; the original system is the member \(\varepsilon=1\) of this family, and the reduction analyses the formal singular limit \(\varepsilon\to0\).

To ensure that the fast variables admit non-trivial quasi-steady states (i.e., finite positive equilibria) in the limit \(\varepsilon \to 0\), the forcing terms and the decay terms in their evolution equations must balance. We scale the relevant \textbf{rate constants} as follows:
\[
r_L = \frac{r_{L,0}}{\varepsilon},\qquad
\eta_A = \frac{\eta_{A,0}}{\varepsilon},\qquad
\eta_{T,0} = \frac{\eta_{T,0,0}}{\varepsilon},\qquad
\eta_{T,1} = \frac{\eta_{T,1,0}}{\varepsilon},
\]
where subscript-\(0\) quantities are \(\mathcal{O}(1)\). \textbf{The savings rate $\varsigma$ and the capital depreciation rate $\delta_K$ are deliberately excluded from this list and are left at their physical, Table-anchored values ($\varsigma_0\in[0,1]$ and $\delta_{K,0}$), unscaled}: unlike the rate constants above (dimensionally unbounded, in $\text{yr}^{-1}$), $\varsigma$ is a bounded dimensionless fraction of output, and scaling it by $1/\varepsilon$ would drive it past its physical upper bound of $1$ for any $\varepsilon$ small enough to be a meaningful scale separation (e.g., $\varsigma_0/\varepsilon=25$ at $\varsigma_0=0.25,\varepsilon=0.01$), which is not a savings rate at all. $\delta_K$ is excluded for a distinct reason: capital's equation of motion is rescaled by $1/\varepsilon$ as a whole (the boundary-layer form below), so $\delta_K$ must remain at its physical value $\delta_{K,0}$ inside that rescaled equation; scaling it separately by $1/\varepsilon$ in addition would double-scale depreciation. The correct way to obtain a fast, non-trivial quasi-steady state for $K$ is instead to rescale capital's entire equation of motion by $1/\varepsilon$ as a whole, in the standard Tikhonov boundary-layer form $\varepsilon\dot K = \varsigma_0 Q - \delta_{K,0}K - \theta K w\|\boldsymbol\Delta^{\text{econ}}\|/\Delta_{\text{ref}}$ (equivalently, $\dot K=[\varsigma_0 Q-\delta_{K,0}K-\ldots]/\varepsilon$ in slow time): this makes capital \emph{relax quickly} to the finite root $K^*=\varsigma_0 Q^*/(\delta_{K,0}+\theta w\|\boldsymbol\Delta^{\text{econ}}\|/\Delta_{\text{ref}})$ of the bracketed term as $\varepsilon\to0$, exactly the non-trivial quasi-steady state the reduction needs, without requiring $\varsigma$ itself to exceed $1$. The scaling of \(\eta_{T,1}\) does not affect the limiting equilibrium \(T^* = T_{\max}\): after this scaling, the bracketed term \(\eta_{T,0,0} + \eta_{T,1,0}(\cdot)T\) in the boundary-layer equation \(\varepsilon\dot T = [\eta_{T,0,0}+\eta_{T,1,0}(\cdot)T](1-T/T_{\max})\) is \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) and strictly positive, so as \(\varepsilon\to0\) this forces \((1-T/T_{\max})\to0\) regardless of the bracket's exact value; only the transient rate of approach to \(T_{\max}\), not the equilibrium itself, depends on \(\eta_{T,1,0}\). Similarly, the deficit penalty in the productivity equation vanishes in the limit, so \(A^* = A_{\max}\) irrespective of deficits.

Substituting these scalings into the fast subsystem---the equations for \(K, L, A, T\)---and the market-clearing condition for \(p\) (which is algebraic) yields the system in mixed ODE-DAE singular perturbation form:
\begin{align}
\varepsilon \dot{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{ODE}} &= \mathbf{f}_{\text{ODE}}(\mathbf{y}, \mathbf{x}, \varepsilon), \label{eq:fast-ode} \\
0 &= \mathbf{f}_{\text{DAE}}(p, \mathbf{x}, \varepsilon), \label{eq:fast-dae} \\
\dot{\mathbf{x}} &= \mathbf{g}(\mathbf{y}, \mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}(t-\tau), \mathbf{x}(t-\tau_m), \varepsilon), \label{eq:slow-subsystem}
\end{align}
where \(\mathbf{x} = (N, E, Z, A, I)^\top\) are the slow variables (including the cumulative deficit \(Z\), the active abiotic pool \(A\), and the inert reservoir \(I\)), \(\mathbf{y}_{\text{ODE}} = (K, L, A_{\text{TFP}}, T)^\top\) are the fast ODE variables, and \(p\) is determined algebraically by market clearing. The algebraic equation \(\mathbf{f}_{\text{DAE}}(p^*, \mathbf{x}, 0) = 0\) has a unique solution \(p^*(\mathbf{x})\) because demand is strictly monotone in price. The ODE fast subsystem has a globally attracting equilibrium \(\mathbf{y}^* = \mathbf{h}(\mathbf{x})\) because the Jacobians have eigenvalues with negative real parts bounded away from zero (see Appendix G). This mixed ODE-DAE structure is covered by standard singular perturbation theory for index-1 DAEs \cite{hairer1996}. The reduced slow subsystem is therefore well-defined, with state \((N,E,Z,A,I)\). The three-state core \((N,Z,E)\) of Section~\ref{sec:core-model} is obtained from this slow subsystem by a second, distinct step that is not a Tikhonov elimination: the active abiotic pool \(A\) is itself slow \textemdash{} Section~\ref{sec:four-state-core} establishes that it cannot be eliminated as a fast auxiliary variable at the model's baseline parameters \textemdash{} and the core results from the frozen-\(A\) approximation (Section~\ref{sec:frozen-A}), in which \(A\) is held at a large reference value (\(A\gg A_0\)) so that the regeneration \(R(N,A)\) reduces to the logistic law \(rN(1-N/K)\), together with the observation that the inert reservoir \(I\) does not feed back into the \((N,Z,E)\) dynamics and decouples. The four-state core (Section~\ref{sec:four-state-core}), which retains \(A\) as a dynamical variable, is the more faithful reduction of this slow subsystem; the three-state core is its leading-order frozen-\(A\) limit.

Furthermore, we restrict the scope of this reduction to resources where the feedback from waste and product stocks to natural regeneration is negligible on the timescale of the collapse. Formally, we take the limit \(\|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i,j}\|, \|\boldsymbol{\rho}_{i,j}\| \to 0\), which decouples the waste \(\mathbf{W}\) and product \(\mathbf{P}\) equations from the core dynamics.

Crucially, with the addition of the abiotic reservoir \(A\) (Section~\ref{sec:abiotic}), the fast subsystem for \(W\) and \(P\) now has a well-defined quasi-steady state for frozen \(N\) and \(A\), because \(\dot{A} = -R(N)\) is \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) and thus \(A\) is frozen on the fast timescale. This justifies the QSS in a physically closed manner.

Under these conditions, a Tikhonov-type reduction gives, formally, that as \(\varepsilon \to 0\) the fast variables \(\mathbf{y}\) converge to their quasi-steady states \(\mathbf{y}^* = \mathbf{h}(\mathbf{x})\), defined by the algebraic root \(\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{h}(\mathbf{x}), \mathbf{x}, 0) = \mathbf{0}\). Substituting \(\mathbf{y}^*\) into the slow subsystem (\ref{eq:slow-subsystem}) and setting \(\chi=1\) yields the slow subsystem in \((N,E,Z,A,I)\); applying the frozen-\(A\) approximation of Section~\ref{sec:frozen-A} and noting that \(I\) decouples then gives the three-state core \((N,Z,E)\), the leading-order limit of the reduced slow subsystem.

\paragraph{Bridge to the Full System.}
The Tikhonov reduction, together with the frozen-\(A\) approximation (Section~\ref{sec:frozen-A}), provides the leading-order statement that the full high-dimensional system tracks the three-state core (Section~\ref{sec:core-model}) on \textbf{finite time intervals} as \(\varepsilon \to 0\), in the regime in which the active abiotic pool remains large relative to its saturation constant; the four-state core (Section~\ref{sec:four-state-core}) is the correspondingly more faithful reduction on timescales over which the drift of \(A\) matters. The bifurcation structure identified in that core — a stabilising Hopf, a fold, and a destabilising Hopf, with an intervening large-amplitude attractor (Section~\ref{sec:hopf-verified}) — serves as a \textbf{structural prediction} for the full vector framework. Tikhonov's theorem does not automatically guarantee the persistence of global bifurcations over infinite time horizons; we return to what is and is not yet established about this persistence, for both the core itself and the full augmented system, in Section~\ref{sec:numerical-continuation-corrected} and Appendix H. A rigorous infinite-time proof of persistence for the full system would require extending Geometric Singular Perturbation Theory (GSPT) to delay-differential-algebraic equations, which remains an open mathematical challenge.

\section{Reduced Core Dynamics and Global Bifurcation Analysis}
\label{sec:core-model}
This section presents the three-state core \((N,Z,E)\) that emerges from the generalised framework under the conditions derived in Section~\ref{sec:asymptotic-reduction}: the leading-order slow manifold of the \((N,Z,E)\) projection of the full system in the institutional-failure regime (\(\chi=1\)), obtained by eliminating the fast macroeconomic variables via Tikhonov's theorem and applying the frozen-\(A\) approximation to the active abiotic pool (Section~\ref{sec:frozen-A}), on finite time intervals. The four-state core of Section~\ref{sec:four-state-core}, which retains the active abiotic pool as a dynamical variable, is the more faithful reduction target; the three-state core is its leading-order frozen-\(A\) limit. Recent bioeconomic models with delayed feedback report delay-induced Hopf instabilities that destabilise harvested equilibria \cite{gao2022,khiyar2026}. A registry of the thresholds across all model variants, to be checked against whenever a numerical value is cited, is collected at the end of this section (Tables~\ref{tab:threshold-registry}--\ref{tab:threshold-registry-2}).

\subsection{Reduced Core Model}
\label{sec:core-model-eqs}
To maintain dynamic consistency between the equilibrium and bifurcation analyses, resource extraction is driven solely by the dynamical effort variable $E(t)$ --- the effort-expansion mechanism first formalised in open-access bioeconomics \cite{gordon1954} --- (Sections~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected} and~\ref{sec:generalized-effort}), rather than by a fixed exogenous demand $D$ paired with a separate effort ceiling $E_{\max}$.

For a single dominant resource stock \(N(t)\) with logistic regeneration \(S(N) = rN(1-N/K)\), the stock-effort dynamics are
\begin{equation}
\dot{N} = S(N) - qEN,
\label{eq:stock-core}
\end{equation}
where $qEN\to0$ as $N\to0$ ensures non-negativity without a separate barrier function or liquidation top-up. \textbf{Capital liquidation} is the portion of $qEN$ that exceeds the locally sustainable yield $S(N)$, $\Lambda(t)=\max(0,qEN-S(N))$, computed as a diagnostic after the fact from the single flow in Eq.~\eqref{eq:stock-core} rather than as a separate dynamical term, exactly as in the vector framework (Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected}).

The deficit memory follows the leaky integrator introduced in Section~\ref{sec:generalized-effort}, using the same \textbf{shifted, floored softplus} (baseline $\delta$, Section~\ref{sec:deficit-definitions}) that the general vector framework uses for its own deficit diagnostics. Using the plain, unshifted softplus here instead would make the equilibrium memory $Z^*$ depend on the numerical smoothing parameter $k$ rather than on the physical baseline $\delta$ (see the derivation immediately below). Omitting the outer floor would introduce a separate failure: since the shift's own floor value $\delta-\ln(2)/k$ is negative whenever $\delta<\ln(2)/k$, $Z$ (and, through it, effort $E$) could be driven negative under a sustained large surplus (Section~\ref{sec:generalized-effort}):
\begin{equation}
\dot Z = \frac{1}{\tau_m}\Bigl[\,\max\Bigl(0,\ \text{softplus}_k\bigl(qEN - S(N)\bigr) - \frac{\ln 2}{k} + \delta\Bigr) - Z\,\Bigr],
\label{eq:Z-core}
\end{equation}
and the effort dynamics incorporate the delayed memory signal:

\paragraph{The deficit signal is exactly the current stock-decline rate.} Because $\dot N = S(N)-qEN$ (Eq.~\eqref{eq:stock-core}), the softplus argument in Eq.~\eqref{eq:Z-core} satisfies
\begin{equation}
qEN - S(N) = -\dot N(t), \label{eq:deficit-identity}
\end{equation}
identically: the core's deficit signal is the instantaneous rate of stock decline, not an independently modelled demand-service imbalance, and liquidation $\Lambda(t)=\max(0,-\dot N(t))$ (Section~\ref{sec:natcap-corrected}) is its positive part. The reduced core is therefore a \textbf{delayed derivative-feedback model with a baseline effort source}: the feedback acts on the rate of stock decline, not on the stock level or on scarcity as such, and the quantitative claims of this manuscript are scoped to that model. The term ``scarcity-driven mobilisation'' is used throughout in the restricted sense of the institutional-failure regime --- an effort response to a perceived decline signal --- rather than as a claim that the model senses scarcity directly. This is the three-state analogue of the unified core's explicitly signed $\ell=-\dot X$ (Eq.~\eqref{eq:unified-ell}, Section~\ref{sec:unified-liquidation}).
\begin{equation}
\dot{E} = \eta E \left[ \frac{Z(t-\tau)}{\Delta_{\text{ref}}} - \frac{E}{E_{\max}} \right] + \delta_0 \frac{Z(t-\tau)}{Z_{\text{ref}} + Z(t-\tau)}.
\label{eq:effort-core}
\end{equation}
Equations~\eqref{eq:stock-core}--\eqref{eq:effort-core} are the exact reduced core: a genuine single-delay DDE system in three state variables $(N,Z,E)$, with the institutional delay $\tau$ entering only through $Z(t-\tau)$ in Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core}. There is no second delay-like parameter competing with $\tau$: $\tau_m$ is an ordinary relaxation timescale of the memory filter, not a second delay.

\subsection{Interior Equilibrium (closed form)}
\label{sec:equilibrium-core}
At any equilibrium, $qE^*N^*=S(N^*)$, so the argument of the softplus in Eq.~\eqref{eq:Z-core} is exactly zero, $\text{softplus}_k(0)=\ln(2)/k$ exactly cancels the explicit $-\ln(2)/k$ shift, the outer $\max(0,\cdot)$ floor is inactive (its argument equals $\delta>0$ at equilibrium, strictly above the floor), and
\begin{equation}
Z^* = \delta,
\label{eq:Zstar-corrected}
\end{equation}
independent of $\tau_m$, independent of $N^*,E^*$, and — since $k$ has no empirical referent (Section~\ref{sec:parameter-audit}) while $\delta$ is a genuine baseline-panic parameter — independent of $k$. This is exact, not merely approximate, confirmed by direct substitution and by numerical integration of Eq.~\eqref{eq:Z-core} to equilibrium; $N^*,E^*$, and the Hopf/SNPO thresholds of Section~\ref{sec:hopf-verified} are likewise unchanged to machine precision when $k$ is varied at fixed $\delta$, confirming that $k$-dependence has been eliminated from the equilibrium and bifurcation structure, not merely from $Z^*$ in isolation. Because the floor is inactive exactly at equilibrium for any $\delta>0$, its inclusion (necessary for non-negativity away from equilibrium; Section~\ref{sec:generalized-effort}) does not alter this closed form or the linearisation of Section~\ref{sec:hopf-verified} below. Substituting $Z^*$ into $\dot E=0$ gives a single quadratic in $E^*$,
\begin{equation}
-\frac{\eta}{E_{\max}}(E^*)^2 + \eta\frac{Z^*}{\Delta_{\text{ref}}}E^* + \delta_0\frac{Z^*}{Z_{\text{ref}}+Z^*} = 0,
\label{eq:Estar-quadratic}
\end{equation}
which has one positive root $E^*$ for all parameter values examined (the other root is negative and discarded). The stock equilibrium then follows from $S(N^*)=qE^*N^*$, i.e., $r(1-N^*/K)=qE^*$, giving
\begin{equation}
N^* = K\left(1-\frac{qE^*}{r}\right),
\label{eq:Nstar-corrected}
\end{equation}
valid whenever $qE^*<r$ (an interior, positive equilibrium exists). This closed-form equilibrium is self-consistent: substituting $(N^*,E^*,Z^*)$ back into Eqs.~\eqref{eq:stock-core}--\eqref{eq:effort-core} confirms all three residuals vanish to machine precision, and direct long-time numerical integration of the DDE converges to exactly this point in the appropriate delay range. It is important to retain the baseline-panic term $\delta_0 Z^*/(Z_{\text{ref}}+Z^*)$ in full when solving for $Z^*$: dropping or approximating it introduces a relative error of 35--85\% in $E^*$ for parameter values away from the large-effort limit, and yields a point that does not satisfy $\dot E=0$.

\subsection{Extinction Boundary}
Setting \(N=0\) in Eq.~\eqref{eq:stock-core} gives $\dot N=0$ identically, for \emph{any} $E$ — because extraction $qEN$ already vanishes at $N=0$ by construction, this is now a genuine equilibrium condition rather than an assumption. A first look at the effort equation might suggest that the deficit-driven term also drops out at the boundary, since the physical deficit $qEN-S(N)$ that drives the memory variable $Z$ (Eq.~\eqref{eq:Z-core}) is itself exactly zero at $N=0$ for any $E$: $qE\cdot0-r\cdot0\cdot(1-0/K)=0$. But this reasoning conflates \textbf{zero physical deficit} with \textbf{zero institutional memory}, and the two are not the same thing in this model: $\dot Z$ depends on the deficit through the shifted softplus, and the shift was introduced (Section~\ref{sec:generalized-effort}) precisely so that a zero deficit does not send $Z$ to zero, but to the baseline-panic floor $\delta>0$ instead — exactly the mechanism already used to derive $Z^*=\delta$ at the interior equilibrium (Eq.~\eqref{eq:Zstar-corrected}). The same derivation applies verbatim at $N=0$: the softplus argument is zero for any $E$, so $Z(t)\to\delta$ regardless of the effort trajectory, and Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core} — which has no explicit dependence on $N$ at all, only on $E$ and the delayed memory $Z(t-\tau)$ — is therefore evaluated at the boundary with exactly the same delayed input, $Z(t-\tau)\to\delta$, that it receives at the interior equilibrium. The effort dynamics at $N=0$ are consequently \textbf{not} a reduced special case of Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core} with a term deleted; they are the full equation,
\[
\dot{E} = \eta E \left[ \frac{Z(t-\tau)}{\Delta_{\text{ref}}} - \frac{E}{E_{\max}} \right] + \delta_0 \frac{Z(t-\tau)}{Z_{\text{ref}} + Z(t-\tau)},
\]
evaluated with $Z(t-\tau)\to\delta$ — which is algebraically identical to the condition defining the interior equilibrium's $E^*$ (Eq.~\eqref{eq:Estar-quadratic}). The boundary equilibrium effort is therefore not a new, independently-determined root but exactly the interior root itself, $E^*_{\text{ext}}=E^*$: institutional memory sustains exactly the same baseline extraction effort at $N=0$ as it does at the healthy interior equilibrium, though — critically — that effort no longer does any physical harm, since $qE^*\cdot 0=0$ (the ``hunger persists'' equilibrium). This identity was confirmed by direct long-horizon numerical integration of the full DDE from $N=0$: for Candidate A, $Z(t)\to\delta\approx0.0693$ and $E(t)\to E^*_{\text{ext}}\approx2.0896$, agreeing with the independently-computed interior $E^*$ to 12 decimal places, and the same identity holds under the corrected effort equation (Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core-corrected}) as well, since its equilibrium condition (Section~\ref{sec:effort-saturation-fix}) reduces to the same bracket-zero condition away from $E=E_{\max}$. Whether sustaining $E^*_{\text{ext}}>0$ indefinitely is physically problematic depends on whether $E$ is read as a costless institutional stance or as physical effort with a genuine energetic price; Section~\ref{sec:thermodynamic-tether} works out an explicit extension for the latter reading.

Linearising $\dot N=S(N)-qE^*_{\text{ext}}N$ around $N=0$ gives $\dot n=(r-qE^*_{\text{ext}})n$, so the boundary equilibrium is locally stable in the $N$-direction \emph{if and only if} $r<qE^*_{\text{ext}}$, and the survival condition is $r>qE^*_{\text{ext}}$. This is the correct linearisation of the extraction \emph{product} $N\phi(N)$ appearing in the stock equation; a common pitfall is to instead differentiate the barrier function $\phi(N)$ alone, giving $qE\phi'(0)=qE/\epsilon$, which is not the same quantity and is not what the survival condition should be based on (the derivative of $N\phi(N)$ at $N=0$ is $0$, not $1/\epsilon$).

\paragraph{Consequence: a transcritical bifurcation, not an independent bistability.} Because $E^*_{\text{ext}}\equiv E^*$ exactly, the survival condition $r>qE^*_{\text{ext}}$ is not a second, independently-calibrated inequality alongside the interior equilibrium's own existence condition — it is the \emph{same} condition. Eq.~\eqref{eq:Nstar-corrected} gives $N^*=K(1-qE^*/r)$, so $N^*>0$ if and only if $qE^*<r$, which is precisely the survival condition above with $E^*_{\text{ext}}$ replaced by its equal, $E^*$. The interior and boundary equilibria therefore cannot both be locally stable at once: whenever the interior equilibrium exists as a positive stock ($N^*>0$), the boundary equilibrium is automatically unstable in the $N$-direction, and whenever $qE^*\ge r$ removes the positive interior equilibrium, the boundary equilibrium is exactly the stable state the system decays to instead. This is the textbook structure of a \textbf{transcritical bifurcation} at $r=qE^*$ — the interior and extinction equilibria exchange stability as they collide, rather than coexisting as two independently-reachable attractors — not a bistable trap that a system sitting at a healthy interior equilibrium could fall into without $r$, $q$, or the effort-equation parameters entering that regime first. At the Candidate A baseline, $r=0.02>qE^*\approx0.00209$, so the interior equilibrium exists and the extinction boundary is correctly unstable, consistent with direct simulation.

\subsection{An Energetic Extension: When Does ``Hunger Persists'' Require a Thermodynamic Tether?}
\label{sec:thermodynamic-tether}
The preceding result is exact for the effort dynamics as stated: because Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core} has no dependence on $N$ or on any energetic/capital stock, nothing in it can force $E\to0$ as the physical resource vanishes, and $E^*_{\text{ext}}=E^*$ is the correct boundary equilibrium of \emph{that} equation. Whether this is a modelling limitation depends on how literally $E$ is interpreted: if $E$ represents institutional attention or policy stance, sustaining a non-zero baseline indefinitely at zero yield is not obviously unphysical (attention does not obey a mass or energy conservation law); if $E$ represents physical extraction effort with a genuine capital or energetic cost (fuel, vessel-operating budgets, wages), then indefinitely sustaining $E^*_{\text{ext}}>0$ against zero yield requires an unmodelled, unbounded external subsidy. This section works out, explicitly and with the same direct-simulation standard used throughout this manuscript, what is required to close this gap without disturbing the equilibrium, characteristic equation, or Hopf/SNPO analysis established above.

\paragraph{Why a capital-availability gate on effort is not a free parameter change.} A first attempt might multiply the right-hand side of Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core-corrected} by a capital-availability factor $K_{\text{cap}}/(K_{\text{cap}}+K_0)$, where $K_{\text{cap}}(t)$ is a new energetic/capital stock draining under effort deployment, $\dot K_{\text{cap}}=qEN-K_{\text{cap}}(\delta_K+c_EE)$. Because the drain term here is proportional to $K_{\text{cap}}$ itself, $\dot K_{\text{cap}}|_{K_{\text{cap}}=0}=qEN\ge0$ and $K_{\text{cap}}$ cannot go negative by construction, mirroring exactly how $qEN\to0$ already protects $N\ge0$ (Section~\ref{sec:core-model-eqs}). Direct simulation of this candidate at $N=0$, however, shows that $K_{\text{cap}}\to0$ does \emph{not} force $E\to0$: the rational gate $K_{\text{cap}}/(K_{\text{cap}}+K_0)$ has no floor preventing $K_{\text{cap}}$ from being driven through zero into negative values by an insufficiently protected coupling, and once $K_{\text{cap}}<0$ the gate itself develops a pole at $K_{\text{cap}}=-K_0$ and returns to a positive value as $K_{\text{cap}}\to-\infty$, silently un-gating effort and reproducing the original effort-at-extinction equilibrium at a shifted location rather than eliminating it. This failure mode was caught only by direct long-horizon integration of the boundary case, not by inspecting the equilibrium algebra alone, consistent with this manuscript's repeated finding (Sections~\ref{sec:equilibrium-core}, \ref{sec:numerical-continuation-corrected}) that boundary and global claims require simulation, not just algebraic limits, to trust.

\paragraph{A corrected mechanism satisfying non-negativity and boundary annihilation by construction.} Renaming the new capital-type state $K_{\text{cap}}$ throughout to avoid collision with the core's own carrying-capacity symbol $K$ (Eq.~\eqref{eq:stock-core}), the corrected pair is
\begin{align}
\dot K_{\text{cap}} &= qEN - K_{\text{cap}}(\delta_K+c_EE), \label{eq:kcap-tether}\\
\dot E &= \Bigl(1-\frac{E}{E_{\max}}\Bigr)\bigl(1-e^{-K_{\text{cap}}/K_0}\bigr)\left[\eta E\left(\frac{Z(t-\tau)}{\Delta_{\text{ref}}}-\frac{E}{E_{\max}}\right)+\delta_0\frac{Z(t-\tau)}{Z_{\text{ref}}+Z(t-\tau)}\right]-\mu_EE, \label{eq:effort-tethered}
\end{align}
where $\delta_K,c_E,K_0,\mu_E>0$ are new parameters (capital depreciation, marginal cost of effort, a capital half-saturation scale, and an institutional-entropy decay rate for effort in the absence of capital support). The smooth saturating gate $1-e^{-K_{\text{cap}}/K_0}$ replaces the rational gate specifically because it has no pole on $[0,\infty)$, vanishes exactly at $K_{\text{cap}}=0$, and is bounded in $[0,1)$ throughout the physical domain. Three boundary properties, each confirmed by direct symbolic differentiation and by long-horizon RK4 integration of the full $(N,Z,E,K_{\text{cap}})$ system from multiple initial conditions (not merely evaluated at a single point), hold by construction:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\dot K_{\text{cap}}|_{K_{\text{cap}}=0}=qEN\ge0$ for all $N,E\ge0$, so $K_{\text{cap}}$ cannot cross zero into negative values.
\item $\dot E|_{E=0}=\delta_0(1-e^{-K_{\text{cap}}/K_0})Z/(Z_{\text{ref}}+Z)\ge0$ for all $K_{\text{cap}},Z\ge0$, so $E$ cannot go negative, exactly as the original equation already guaranteed (Section~\ref{sec:generalized-effort}).
\item $\dot E|_{K_{\text{cap}}=0}=-\mu_EE$ identically (the gate vanishes exactly, not merely approximately, at $K_{\text{cap}}=0$), so effort decays exponentially to zero once capital is exhausted, regardless of the panic term $\delta_0$ or the memory $Z(t-\tau)$.
\end{itemize}
At the extinction boundary $N=0$: $\dot K_{\text{cap}}=-K_{\text{cap}}(\delta_K+c_EE)\le0$ (yield vanishes with $N$), so $K_{\text{cap}}\to0$ monotonically; as $K_{\text{cap}}\to0$, the gate vanishes and $\dot E\to-\mu_EE$, so $E\to0$ as well. Direct integration confirms this to floating-point precision from initial effort and capital levels spanning three orders of magnitude ($E_0\in[0.5,29]$, $K_{\text{cap},0}\in[0.1,1000]$): every trajectory converges to $(N,Z,E,K_{\text{cap}})=(0,\delta,0,0)$, the genuine, thermodynamically closed extinction state, replacing the ``hunger persists'' equilibrium $(0,\delta,E^*,\cdot)$ of Section~\ref{sec:equilibrium-core} with one in which the institution starves along with the resource rather than sustaining indefinite effort against zero yield.

\paragraph{The interior equilibrium survives, but existence is no longer unconditional.} Because Eqs.~\eqref{eq:stock-core}--\eqref{eq:Z-core} are untouched by this extension, $Z^*=\delta$ (Eq.~\eqref{eq:Zstar-corrected}) and the stock relation $N^*=K(1-qE^*/r)$ (Eq.~\eqref{eq:Nstar-corrected}) are unaffected. Writing $B(E)$ for the bracketed term of Eq.~\eqref{eq:Estar-quadratic} evaluated at $Z=\delta$ and $K_{\text{cap}}^*(E)=qEN^*(E)/(\delta_K+c_EE)$ for the capital level implied by Eq.~\eqref{eq:kcap-tether} at $\dot K_{\text{cap}}=0$, the interior equilibrium condition becomes the scalar root problem $F(E^*)\equiv(1-E^*/E_{\max})(1-e^{-K_{\text{cap}}^*(E^*)/K_0})B(E^*)-\mu_EE^*=0$, which is transcendental rather than the original quadratic (Eq.~\eqref{eq:Estar-quadratic}): closed-form tractability of $E^*$ is the explicit price of this extension, not an oversight. Linearising $F$ at $E^*=0$ gives the local condition
\begin{equation}
\mu_E<\alpha\equiv\frac{qK\,\delta_0\,\delta}{K_0\,\delta_K\,(Z_{\text{ref}}+\delta)} \label{eq:mu-E-necessary}
\end{equation}
as \emph{necessary} for the trivial state to be locally unstable (equivalently, for a positive root to persist arbitrarily close to $E=0$); this is not sufficient for a globally reachable interior equilibrium, and direct numerical bisection on an illustrative parameterisation ($q=0.001$, $K=100$, $\delta_K=0.05$, $c_E=0.5$, $K_0=1$, together with Candidate A's $\delta_0=0.01,\delta\approx0.0693,Z_{\text{ref}}=1$, giving $\alpha\approx1.30\times10^{-3}$) finds the true collapse threshold at $\mu_E^{\text{SN}}\approx5.9\,\alpha$ for that parameterisation — nearly six times more permissive than the local necessary condition alone would suggest, a gap of exactly the kind the verification protocol (boundary and global claims are checked by direct simulation, not algebra alone) exists to catch.

\paragraph{An emergent institutional Allee effect.} Between the local threshold $\alpha$ and the true collapse threshold $\mu_E^{\text{SN}}$, the corrected mechanism exhibits genuine bistability in $E$ at fixed $\tau$: the extinction state $E=0$ is locally stable, a positive interior equilibrium $E_2^*$ remains locally stable, and they are separated by an unstable threshold effort $E_1^*\in(0,E_2^*)$ acting as a basin boundary. This was confirmed, not merely inferred from the sign pattern of $F$, by direct nonlinear integration of the full $(N,Z,E,K_{\text{cap}})$ system started both just above and just below $E_1^*$ (including from non-equilibrated initial conditions on $N$ and $K_{\text{cap}}$, not only points on the reduced equilibrium curve): trajectories starting above $E_1^*$ converge to $E_2^*$, and trajectories starting below converge to the thermodynamically closed extinction state, even though the biological stock $N$ remains near carrying capacity throughout. This is structurally analogous to a demographic Allee effect (a critical population threshold below which a stock cannot recover despite abundant resources; already used elsewhere in this manuscript's general vector framework, Section~\ref{sec:productivity-gap}), but arising in the \emph{institutional} state variable rather than the biological one: an institution whose effort and capital both fall below $E_1^*$ cannot bootstrap itself back to the productive equilibrium even while the resource itself is intact, a genuinely new qualitative regime this extension introduces rather than merely relocating.

\paragraph{Scope and status.} This extension is presented as a worked feasibility analysis, not as a revision of the core results used throughout Sections~\ref{sec:core-model}--\ref{sec:numerical-continuation-corrected}, all of which continue to use the un-tethered effort equation (Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core} or its corrected form, Eq.~\eqref{eq:effort-core-corrected}) exactly as stated; $\delta_K,c_E,K_0,\mu_E$ have no calibration against Table~\ref{tab:params} and are illustrative only. Three items remain explicitly open: (i) no closed-form expression for $\mu_E^{\text{SN}}$ is known, so any adoption of this mechanism requires numerically locating both $\alpha$ and the true saddle-node threshold for its own parameterisation, per the pattern already established for the SNPO folds themselves (Section~\ref{sec:numerical-continuation-corrected}); (ii) the effect of the tether on the Hopf crossings $\tau_-,\tau_+$ and the SNPO folds has not been computed, since the Jacobian expands from $3\times3$ to $4\times4$ and the new capital-relaxation eigenvalue's interaction with the existing delay-induced eigenvalues is unknown; (iii) whether $K_{\text{cap}}$ should be identified with, or coupled to, the manufactured capital $K(t)$ of the general vector framework's macroeconomic layer (Section~\ref{sec:layer4}, Eq.~\eqref{eq:k-corrected}) — which would let this extension inherit an existing, partially-calibrated capital-accumulation equation rather than introducing an independent one — is a natural unification left to future work.

\subsection{Linearisation and Characteristic Equation}
Let \(x = N - N^*\), \(z = Z - Z^*\), \(y = E - E^*\). Linearising Eqs.~\eqref{eq:stock-core}--\eqref{eq:effort-core} about the equilibrium of Section~\ref{sec:equilibrium-core} gives
\[
\dot{x} = A_N x + A_E y, \qquad
\dot{z} = B_N x + B_E y - \tfrac{1}{\tau_m} z, \qquad
\dot{y} = C_E y + C_Z z(t-\tau),
\]
with coefficients evaluated at the equilibrium (using $\text{softplus}_k'(0)=1/2$ for the leaky-integrator linearisation):
\[
A_N = r\Bigl(1-\frac{2N^*}{K}\Bigr) - qE^*, \qquad A_E = -qN^*,
\]
\[
B_N = \frac{1}{2\tau_m}\bigl(qE^*-S'(N^*)\bigr), \qquad B_E = \frac{1}{2\tau_m}qN^*,
\]
\[
C_E = \eta\Bigl(\frac{Z^*}{\Delta_{\text{ref}}}-\frac{2E^*}{E_{\max}}\Bigr), \qquad C_Z = \frac{\eta E^*}{\Delta_{\text{ref}}} + \frac{\delta_0 Z_{\text{ref}}}{(Z_{\text{ref}}+Z^*)^2}.
\]
Because effort drives extraction directly rather than through a barrier function $\phi(N)$, no $\phi'(N^*)$ terms appear anywhere in this Jacobian, and the delay enters through a single, clean channel ($C_Z\,z(t-\tau)$ in the $\dot y$ equation) rather than through a windowed-integral term requiring an auxiliary limit; a windowed-integral formulation of the memory term would instead require a separate, non-standard treatment of the singular limit $\tau_m\to0$, which is unnecessary here. The characteristic quasi-polynomial is
\begin{equation}
\det\!\begin{pmatrix} \lambda-A_N & 0 & -A_E \\ -B_N & \lambda+\tfrac1{\tau_m} & -B_E \\ 0 & -C_Z e^{-\lambda\tau} & \lambda - C_E \end{pmatrix} = 0,
\label{eq:char-eq-corrected}
\end{equation}
a genuine single-delay cubic quasi-polynomial in $\lambda$; Appendix B gives the two-line derivation of $Z^*$ underlying this equation.

\subsection{Hopf Crossings and the Bistable Windows}
\label{sec:hopf-verified}
We solved Eq.~\eqref{eq:char-eq-corrected} for purely imaginary roots $\lambda=i\omega$ by direct numerical root-tracking of the rightmost eigenvalue's real part as a function of $\tau$ (rather than by algebraic separation into real/imaginary parts and numerical solution of the resulting transcendental pair, which is more brittle for a genuinely 3-dimensional system); each candidate crossing was confirmed by an independent, brute-force forward simulation of the full nonlinear DDE (Eqs.~\eqref{eq:stock-core}--\eqref{eq:effort-core}) via a compiled delay-integrator. For Candidate A parameters (\(\eta=0.914, E_{\max}=30, \delta_0=0.01, \Delta_{\text{ref}}=1.0, \tau_m=5\) years, $k=10$, $\delta=\ln(2)/10$), the closed-form equilibrium is $N^*\approx89.55$, $E^*\approx2.090$, $Z^*=\delta\approx0.0693$.

\paragraph{A topological constraint on the bifurcation sequence, and why it matters for how the thresholds must be located.} A Hopf bifurcation is a strictly local event \cite{guckenheimer1983,hassard1981}: it creates or destroys only a small-amplitude limit cycle in an infinitesimal neighbourhood of the equilibrium, and it cannot by itself annihilate a pre-existing, large-amplitude, globally attracting cycle. Consequently, if a large-amplitude cycle is the sole attractor on one side of a local Hopf crossing and the equilibrium alone is the sole attractor on the other side, a separate global bifurcation — here, a fold (Saddle-Node) of periodic orbits distinct from either Hopf point — must be responsible for the cycle's disappearance, and it need not coincide with the Hopf point itself. Locating this fold requires care: a fixed initial condition, integrated for a fixed (even very long) horizon and bisected on $\tau$, only ever detects whether \emph{that particular history} lies inside or outside its own basin of attraction at that finite time, not whether a large-amplitude periodic orbit still exists as a mathematical object. Because the original ungated core exhibits pronounced critical slowing down \cite{scheffer2009,carpenter2011,lindegren2012} near each Hopf point (linearised decay/growth rates within $10^{-4}$--$10^{-5}$\,yr$^{-1}$ of neutral over an $\mathcal{O}(1)$\,yr range of $\tau$, giving relaxation times of $10^4$--$10^6$ simulated years), a fixed-initial-condition bisection can mistake a not-yet-decayed transient for a persistent attractor, or mistake a slowly-shrinking basin for an already-vanished one. Each fold is instead located by \textbf{numerical continuation}: starting from a converged limit cycle deep inside the large-amplitude regime and using its final state as the (near-exact) initial condition for a small step in $\tau$, so that the simulation begins already on or very near the periodic orbit rather than in an arbitrary corner of phase space. Each continuation step is run for $1.5$--$5\times10^{6}$ simulated years (typically $10^4$--$10^5$ oscillation periods) before deciding whether the cycle has persisted or collapsed, and every reported threshold below is additionally cross-checked by re-running the collapse/persistence test from at least one independently chosen far-from-equilibrium initial condition. This diagnosis reveals two folds rather than one: a fold flanking the stabilising Hopf $\tau_-$, and a second fold flanking the destabilising Hopf $\tau_+$ whose location differs substantially from what a fixed-initial-condition, insufficient-horizon bisection would report, owing to the critical-slowing-down artifact described above.

The verified regime structure for Candidate A is:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Regime I (\(0 < \tau < \tau_{-} \approx 6.88\) yr):} Equilibrium unstable throughout this regime; both near- and far-from-equilibrium trajectories converge to a single, large-amplitude, monostable limit cycle.
    \item \textbf{Narrow lower bistable window (\(\tau_{-} \approx 6.88 < \tau < \tau_{\text{SNPO,L}} \approx 7.355\) yr):} The fold $\tau_{\text{SNPO,L}}$ lies \emph{above}, not below, the stabilising Hopf $\tau_-\approx6.88$\,yr: for $6.88\lesssim\tau\lesssim7.355$\,yr the equilibrium is already a locally stable focus, yet the large-amplitude cycle persists as a coexisting attractor reachable from sufficiently large initial stocks. This is a genuine, narrow ($\approx0.47$\,yr wide) bistable window that a purely local (Hopf-only) analysis misses entirely, consistent with the topological argument above: the local recovery of stability at $\tau_-$ and the global disappearance of the large cycle at $\tau_{\text{SNPO,L}}$ are, correctly, two different events.
    \item \textbf{Wide monostable interval (\(\tau_{\text{SNPO,L}}\approx7.355 < \tau < \tau_{\text{SNPO,R}}\approx131.24\) yr):} Equilibrium is a stable focus and is the sole interior attractor reachable from any initial condition we tested, including deliberately far-from-equilibrium and continuation-seeded histories. This is the system's wide monostable interval.
    \item \textbf{Narrow upper bistable window (\(\tau_{\text{SNPO,R}} \approx 131.24 < \tau < \tau_{+} \approx 132.4\) yr):} A second global fold, $\tau_{\text{SNPO,R}}$, reintroduces the large-amplitude cycle as a coexisting attractor \emph{before} the equilibrium itself loses local stability at $\tau_+$ — the mirror image of the lower window. This window is also narrow ($\approx1.1$\,yr wide).
